From non-unitary wheeled PROPs to smooth amplitudes and generalised convolutions
Pierre J. Clavier, Loïc Foissy, Sylvie Paycha

Abstract
We introduce the concept of TRAP (Traces and Permutations), which can roughly be viewed as a wheeled PROP (Products and Permutations) without unit. TRAPs are equipped with a horizontal concatenation and partial trace maps. Continuous morphisms on an infinite dimensional topological space and smooth kernels (resp. smoothing operators) on a closed manifold form a TRAP but not a wheeled PROP. We build the free objects in the category of TRAPs as TRAPs of graphs and show that a TRAP can be completed to a unitary TRAP (or wheeled PROP). We further show that it can be equipped with a vertical concatenation, which on the TRAP of linear homomorphisms of a vector space, amounts to the usual composition. The vertical concatenation in the TRAP of smooth kernels gives rise to generalised convolutions. Graphs whose vertices are decorated by smooth kernels (resp. smoothing operators) on a closed manifold form a TRAP. From their universal properties we build smooth amplitudes associated with the graph.



Classification:
18M85, 46E99, 47G30

Key words:
PROP, trace, distribution kernel, convolution

Introduction

State of the art

PROPs (
Pro
ducts and
P
ermutations)
1
1

1

The traditional notation is PROP, more recently prop.

provide an
algebraic structure that allows to deal with an arbitrary number of inputs and outputs. They generalise many other algebraic structures such as
operads, which have one output and multiple inputs. PROPs appeared in
[
ML65
]
and later in the book
[
BV73
]
in the context of cartesian
categories. Although operads stemmed from the study of iterated loop spaces in algebraic topology, see e.g.
[
May72
]
, their origin can also be traced back to the earlier work
[
BV68
]
2
2

2

We
thank B. Vallette for his enlightening comments on these historical aspects.
.

An important asset of PROPs over operads is that they encompass algebraic structures such as bialgebras and Hopf algebras that lie outside the realm of
operads or co-operads. This very fact is a motivation to consider PROPs in the context of renormalisation in quantum field theory. We refer the reader to
[
Pir01
]
for the study of bialgebras in the PROPs framework and

[
Mar08
,
YJ15
,
ML65
]
for other
classical examples of PROPs. In recent years, wheeled PROPs
[
Mer04
,
MMS09
]
, which allow for loops, have played an important role in the context of deformation quantisation.

A central example of PROP is the PROP
\mathbf{Hom}_{V}
of homomorphisms of a finite dimensional vector space
V
which we generalise
to the PROP
\mathbf{Hom}^{c}_{V}
of continuous homomorphisms of a nuclear Fréchet space
V
. Whereas the first is a wheeled PROP (Proposition
2.1.1
), the latter is not unless the space
V
is finite dimensional (Theorem
2.2.8
). It can nevertheless be interpreted as a TRAP (Definition
1.1
), which roughly speaking, amounts to a wheeled PROP without unit
3
3

3

To our knowledge, wheeled PROPs without units do not appear in the literature, which is why we allow ourselves to give them a shorter name.
. TRAPs introduced in this paper, offer natural structures to host morphisms of infinite dimensional spaces (see Proposition
2.1.2
) and are therefore expected to play a role in the context of renormalisation in quantum field theory.

Another class of important examples we consider, are TRAPs of graphs (Proposition
3.2.1
)
of various types.
In the context of deformation quantisation, the complex of oriented graphs whether
directed or wheeled, plays an important role in the construction of a free PROP generated by a
\mathfrak{S}\times\mathfrak{S}^{op}
-module (see e.g

[
Mer04
, Paragraph 2.1.3]
).

Our long term goal is to use the TRAP structure of graphs decorated by distribution (e.g. Green) kernels in order to build amplitudes as generalised convolutions (called
P
-amplitudes, see Definition
4.3.1
) of kernels associated with the
decorated graph. The expected singularities of the resulting amplitudes are immediate obstacles in defining such generalised convolutions. In this paper, we focus on the smooth setup, in which case the amplitudes are smooth.

Feynman rules and TRAPs

In space-time variables, a Feynman rule is expected to assign to a graph
G
with
k
incoming and
l
outgoing edges, an amplitude
(it is actually a distribution)
K_{G}
in
k+l
variables. Our long term goal is to derive the existence and the properties of the map
G\mapsto K_{G}
from a universal property
of the PROP structure on graphs.

By means of blow-up methods, generalised convolutions of Green functions were built on a closed Riemannian manifold in
[
DZ17
]
, with the goal of renormalising multiple loop
amplitudes for Euclidean QFT on Riemannian manifolds.
We hope to be able to simplify the intricate analytic aspects of the renormalisation procedure for multiple loop amplitudes, by adopting an algebraic point of view on amplitudes
using TRAPs. There were earlier attempts to describe QFT theories in
terms of PROPs (see e.g.
[
Ion07a
,
Ion07b
]
), yet to our knowledge, none with the focus we are putting on generalised convolutions to
describe amplitudes.

We therefore expect the PROP of oriented graphs, briefly mentioned in
[
Ion07b
]
, and more specifically TRAPs, their non-unitary counterparts which naturally arise in the infinite-dimensional set-up, to have concrete applications in the
perturbative approach to quantum field theory. To our knowledge, this is yet an unexplored aspect of the theory. Filling in this gap is a long term goal we have in mind.
A first step towards this goal is the study of the TRAP of smoothing symbols (Theorem
2.3.1
), which like

\mathbf{Hom}_{V}^{c}
is not a wheeled PROP due to the infinite dimensional spaces it involves.

TRAPs of graphs

TRAPs and unitary TRAPs entail two operations, the horizontal concatenation, and the partial trace map. The difference between TRAPs and unitary TRAPs is the existence of a unit for the trace in the latter.
We define a TRAP structure on various families of graphs, which can be corolla ordered
(
Definition
3.1.1
) or decorated (Proposition
3.2.1
).
The horizontal concatenation of this TRAP is the natural concatenation of
graphs and the partial trace map consists in gluing together
one of the input with one of the output, and therefore assigns to a graph
G
with
k
incoming and
l
outgoing edges
a graph with
k-1
incoming and
l-1
outgoing edge. The set of corolla ordered graphs
\mathbf{{CGr}^{\circlearrowright}}
equipped with the partial trace map builds
a unitary TRAP, and we prove that it is a free unitary TRAP (Theorem
3.3.1
):
this is the TRAP counterpart of a similar statement for free PROPs, described in terms of graphs without loops
[
Mar08
, Proposition 57]
and
[
Val03
,
Val04
,
YJ15
]
.
More generally,
the set of corolla ordered graphs
\mathbf{{CGr}^{\circlearrowright}}(X)
decorated by a set
X
on their vertices is the free unitary TRAP generated by
X
.
These unitary TRAPs contain free nonunitary TRAPs, which are combinatorially described by particular graphs,
which we call solar
4
4

4

In
[
YJ15
]
such graphs are called ordinary.
.

From TRAPs to unitary TRAPs

If
V
is a finite-dimensional vector space,
then the PROP
\mathbf{Hom}_{V}
of homomorphisms of
V
is a unitary TRAP, with the usual trace of endomorphisms.
Its unit as a TRAP is the identity map of
V
. When
V
is not finite-dimensional, one cannot equip the whole PROP
\mathbf{Hom}_{V}

with a structure of unitary TRAP.
In this case, one has to restrict to smaller classes of homomorphisms, such as that of the
homomorphisms of finite rank. This class no longer contains the identity, and we only obtain a TRAP and not
a unitary TRAP (Proposition
2.1.2
).
To circumvent this difficulty, we construct for any TRAP
P
a unitary TRAP
\mathbf{uPGr^{\circlearrowright}}(P)
which contains
P
(Theorem
3.4.1
).
This object is characterised by a universal property (Proposition
3.5.1
), which amounts to applying the left adjoint to
the forgetful functor from the category of unitary TRAPs to the category of TRAPs.
The existence of this functor comes from the inclusion of corolla ordered solar graphs, describing nonunitary free TRAPs,
in the set of corolla ordered graphs, describing unitary free TRAPs. In particular, in
\mathbf{uPGr^{\circlearrowright}}(P)
an identity
I
is added, as well as its trace, symbolised by an abstract element
\mathcal{O}
,
which is no longer an element of the base field
\mathbb{K}
.

The vertical concatenation and generalised traces

In the same way as a vertical concatenation can be built on wheeled PROPs to turn them into PROPs, any TRAP comes with a vertical concatenation (Proposition
4.1.1
).
The vertical concatenation on TRAPs
previously considered in
[
YJ15
, Definition 11.33]
generalises the composition of morphisms.
When applied to unitary TRAPs, this construction yields a functor from unitary TRAPs to PROPs (Proposition
4.1.2
). Roughly speaking, on graphs, if
G
is a graph with
k
inputs and
l
outputs,
if
G^{\prime}
is a graph with
l
inputs and
m
outputs,

G^{\prime}\circ G
is obtained by gluing together the outgoing edges of
G
and the incoming edges of
G^{\prime}
according to their indexation,
giving a graph with
k
inputs and
m
outputs. The universal property of graphs allows to generalise the construction of the vertical concatenation to arbitrary traces.
If
V
is a finite-dimensional space, the vertical concatenation of the TRAP
\mathbf{Hom}_{V}
coincides with the usual composition of
linear maps
f:V^{\otimes k}\longrightarrow V^{\otimes l}
.

Extending this to the infinite dimensional setup requires the use of a completed tensor product
\widehat{\otimes}
in order to have an
isomorphism

\mathbf{Hom}_{V}^{c}(k,l)\simeq\left(V^{\prime}\right)^{\widehat{\otimes}k}\widehat{\otimes}V^{\widehat{\otimes}l},

where
\mathbf{Hom}_{V}^{c}(k,l)
stands for the algebra of continuous morphisms from
V^{\widehat{\otimes}l}
to
V^{\widehat{\otimes}k}
(see
Definition
2.2.7
) and

V^{\prime}
for the topological dual of a topological space
V
. This holds in the framework of Fréchet nuclear spaces which form a monoidal
category under
the completed tensor product (Lemma
2.2.4
). On Fréchet nuclear spaces, the composition can
indeed be described as a dual pairing, so it comes as no surprise that
for a Fréchet nuclear space
V
, the vertical concatenation obtained from the nonunitary TRAP structure
is the usual composition.

Any TRAP also inherits a generalised trace defined on its elements with the same number of inputs and outputs. Roughly speaking, these generalised traces are obtained by grafting the outputs to the inputs according to their indexation.
These traces on TRAPs generalise the usual trace of morphisms, and they also enjoy a cyclicity property (Proposition
4.2.1
).

When
V
is a space of smooth functions on a closed Riemannian manifold
M
, the associativity of the vertical concatenation amounts to the Fubini property (Theorem
4.3.4
, 2.) and the generalised trace of a generalised kernel
K
with
k
inputs and
k
outputs is given by the integration of
K
along the small diagonal of
M^{k}
(Theorem
4.3.4
, 3.).

Amplitude of a graph decorated by a TRAP

As mentioned above, our goal in the present paper is to
provide an adequate algebraic and analytic framework in which we build generalised convolution functions associated with graphs decorated with smooth kernels.
We show that these form a TRAP (Theorem
2.3.1
), whose partial trace maps are given by a partial convolution.

When
P
is a TRAP, the universal property of the TRAP of corolla ordered graphs decorated by
P

gives rise to a canonical TRAP map, which associates to any such graph
G
an element of
P
which we call the
P
-amplitude associated with
G

(Definition
4.3.1
).
The
P
-amplitude commutes with both horizontal and vertical concatenation of
P
(Proposition
4.3.2
). It turns out that the vertical concatenation is in fact a particular example
of
P
-amplitude, where the considered graph has two vertices.
When applied to the TRAP of smooth generalised kernels, this construction generalises the usual convolution of kernels (Remark
4.3.3
) and gives rise to smooth amplitudes.

Unitary TRAPs and wheeled PROPs

A unitary TRAP is known in the literature under the name of wheeled PROP.
In order to prove that the two notions coincide, we describe TRAPs and unitary TRAPS as algebras over a monad (see Definition
5.2.1
)
which generalises the notion of monoid to the frame of category theory.
We state that unitary TRAPS are algebras over a monad
\Gamma^{\circlearrowright}
of graphs,
described as an endofunctor of a category of modules over symmetric groups
sending an object
X
to the free unitary TRAP of graphs
{\bf sol}\Gamma^{\circlearrowright}(X)
generated by
X
.
When
X
is a unitary TRAP,
\Gamma^{\circlearrowright}(X)
inherits a contraction operation to
X
, which induces the monadic structure
(Theorem
5.3.1
).
This monad
\Gamma^{\circlearrowright}
turns out to be the monad used to defined wheeled PROPs in the literature
[
YJ15
, Corollary 11.35]
,
thus relating our presentation of unitary TRAPs in terms of
family of sets with maps satisfying a set of axioms and the categorical presentation of wheeled PROPs
in terms of algebras over a particular monad. A similar result holds for (nonunitary) TRAPs, replacing graphs by
solar graphs introduced in Definition
3.1.1
.

Openings

To sum up, by means of a TRAP structure, we were able to build generalised convolutions (resp. traces) associated with graphs decorated with smooth
kernels. As announced at the beginning of the introduction, we expect this algebraic approach to enable us to tackle non smooth kernels and thus to describe (non necessarily smooth) amplitudes as generalised
convolutions of distribution kernels associated with graphs. At this stage these are open questions that we hope to address in future work.

There are other possible natural generalisations of the framework presented here, that are more algebraic in nature
5
5

5

We thank Mark Johnson for pointing out the subsequent interesting questions to us.
. One could consider coloured TRAPs, whose input and output edges are coloured, and whose partial trace maps relate inputs and outputs of the same colour. Such structures are expected to play a role in QFTs with more than one type of particles (for example QED and QCD). Coloured TRAPs could also be relevent in the more geometric context of maps between different manifolds or in the context of modules over an algebra.

There are also potential generalisations of Theorem
2.3.1
, which only requires that there be enough integral-like objects to define the partial trace maps, thereby hinting to the fact that more general spaces than the ones considered here should also carry TRAPs structures.
Weakened versions of
C^{*}
-algebras such as inverse limits of
C^{*}
-algebras
[
Phi88
]
and locally multiplicative convex
C^{*}
-algebras
[
JJ06
]
would be worth investigating in that context.

1
The category of TRAPs

1.1
Definition

Notations 1.1.1
.

For any
k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
, we write
[k]:=\{1,\cdots,k\}
. In particular,
[0]=\emptyset
.

\mathfrak{S}_{k}
denotes the symmetric group on
k
elements. An element
\sigma\in\mathfrak{S}_{k}
sends
i\in[k]
to
\sigma(i)\in[k]
.

Definition 1.1.1
.

A
TRAP
is a family
P=(P(k,l))_{k,l\geqslant 0}
of sets, equipped with the following structures:

1.

P
is a
\mathfrak{S}\times\mathfrak{S}^{op}
-module, that is to say,
for any
k,l\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
,
P(k,l)
is a
\mathfrak{S}_{l}\times\mathfrak{S}_{k}^{op}
-module. In other words, there exist maps

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{array}[]{rcl}\mathfrak{S}_{l}\times P(k,l)&\longrightarrow&P(k,l)\\
(\sigma,p)&\mapsto&\sigma\cdot p,\end{array}\right.

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{array}[]{rcl}P(k,l)\times\mathfrak{S}_{k}&\longrightarrow&P(k,l)\\
(p,\tau)&\mapsto&p\cdot\tau,\end{array}\right.

such that for any
(k,l)\in\mathbb{N}_{0}^{2}
, for any
(\sigma,\sigma^{\prime},\tau,\tau^{\prime})\in\mathfrak{S}_{l}^{2}\times\mathfrak{S}_{k}^{2}
, for any
p\in P(k,l)
,

\displaystyle\mathrm{Id}_{[l]}\cdot p

\displaystyle=p\cdot\mathrm{Id}_{[k]}=p,

\displaystyle\sigma\cdot(\sigma^{\prime}\cdot p)

\displaystyle=(\sigma\sigma^{\prime})\cdot p,

\displaystyle\sigma\cdot(p\cdot\tau)

\displaystyle=(\sigma\cdot p)\cdot\tau,

\displaystyle(p\cdot\tau)\cdot\tau^{\prime}

\displaystyle=p\cdot(\tau\tau^{\prime}).

2.

For any
k,l,k^{\prime},l^{\prime}\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
, there is a map

\displaystyle*:

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{array}[]{rcl}P(k,l)\times P(k^{\prime},l^{\prime})&\longrightarrow&P(k+k^{\prime},l+l^{\prime})\\
(p,p^{\prime})&\longrightarrow&p*p^{\prime},\end{array}\right.

called the
horizontal concatenation
, such that:

(a)

(Associativity). For any
(k,l,k^{\prime},l^{\prime},k^{\prime\prime},l^{\prime\prime})\in\mathbb{N}_{0}^{6}
, for any
(p,p^{\prime},p^{\prime\prime})\in P(k,l)\times P(k^{\prime},l^{\prime})\times P(k^{\prime\prime},l^{\prime\prime})
,

(p*p^{\prime})*p^{\prime\prime}=p*(p^{\prime}*p^{\prime\prime}).

(b)

(Unity). There exists
I_{0}\in P(0,0)
such that for any
(k,l)\in\mathbb{N}_{0}^{2}
, for any
p\in P(k,l)
,

I_{0}*p=p*I_{0}=p.

(c)

(Compatibility with the symmetric actions).
For any
(k,l,k^{\prime},l^{\prime})\in\mathbb{N}_{0}^{4}
, for any
(p,p^{\prime})\in P(k,l)\times P(k^{\prime},l^{\prime})
,
for any
(\sigma,\tau,\sigma^{\prime},\tau^{\prime})\in\mathfrak{S}_{l}\times\mathfrak{S}_{k}\times\mathfrak{S}_{l^{\prime}}\times\mathfrak{S}_{k^{\prime}}
,

(\sigma\cdot p\cdot\tau)*(\sigma^{\prime}\cdot p^{\prime}\cdot\tau^{\prime})=(\sigma\otimes\sigma^{\prime})\cdot(p*p^{\prime})\cdot(\tau\otimes\tau^{\prime}),

where, for any
(\alpha,\beta)\in\mathfrak{S}_{m}\otimes\mathfrak{S}_{n}
,
\alpha\otimes\beta\in\mathfrak{S}_{m+n}

is defined by

\alpha\otimes\beta(i)=\begin{cases}\alpha(i)\mbox{ if }i\leqslant m,\\
\beta(i-m)+m\mbox{ if }i>m.\end{cases}

(d)

(Commutativity). For any
(k,l,k^{\prime},l^{\prime})\in\mathbb{N}_{0}^{4}
, For any
p\in P(k,l)
,
p^{\prime}\in P(k^{\prime},l^{\prime})
,

c_{l,l^{\prime}}\cdot(p*p^{\prime})=(p^{\prime}*p)\cdot c_{k,k^{\prime}},

where for any
(m,n)\in\mathbb{N}_{0}^{2}
,
c_{m,n}\in\mathfrak{S}_{m+n}
is defined by:

\displaystyle c_{m,n}(i)

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}i+n\mbox{ if }i\leqslant m,\\
i-m\mbox{ if }i>m.\end{cases}

(1)

3.

For any
k,l\geqslant 1
, for any
i\in[k]
,
j\in[l]
, there is a map

t_{i,j}:\left\{\begin{array}[]{rcl}P(k,l)&\longrightarrow&P(k-1,l-1)\\
p&\mapsto&t_{i,j}(p),\end{array}\right.

(2)

called the
partial trace map
, such that:

(a)

(Commutativity). For any
k,l\geqslant 2
, for any
i\in[k]
,
j\in[l]
,
i^{\prime}\in[k-1]
,
j^{\prime}\in[l-1]
,

\displaystyle t_{i^{\prime},j^{\prime}}\circ t_{i,j}

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}t_{i-1,j-1}\circ t_{i^{\prime},j^{\prime}}\mbox{ if }i^{\prime}<i,\>j^{\prime}<j,\\
t_{i,j-1}\circ t_{i^{\prime}+1,j^{\prime}}\mbox{ if }i^{\prime}\geqslant i,\>j^{\prime}<j,\\
t_{i-1,j}\circ t_{i^{\prime},j^{\prime}+1}\mbox{ if }i^{\prime}<i,\>j^{\prime}\geqslant j,\\
t_{i,j}\circ t_{i^{\prime}+1,j^{\prime}+1}\mbox{ if }i^{\prime}\geqslant i,\>j^{\prime}\geqslant j.\end{cases}

(b)

(Compatibility with the symmetric actions). For any
k,l\geqslant 1
, for any
i\in[k]
,
j\in[l]
,

\sigma\in\mathfrak{S}_{l}
,
\tau\in\mathfrak{S}_{k}
, for any
p\in P(k,l)
,

t_{i,j}(\sigma\cdot p\cdot\tau)=\sigma_{j}\cdot(t_{\tau(i),\sigma^{-1}(j)}(p))\cdot\tau_{i},

with the following notation: if
\alpha\in\mathfrak{S}_{n}
and
p\in[n]
, then
\alpha_{p}\in\mathfrak{S}_{n-1}
is defined by

\alpha_{p}(k)=\begin{cases}\alpha(k)\mbox{ if }k<\alpha^{-1}(p)\mbox{ and }\alpha(k)<p,\\
\alpha(k)-1\mbox{ if }k<\alpha^{-1}(p)\mbox{ and }\alpha(k)>p,\\
\alpha(k+1)\mbox{ if }k\geqslant\alpha^{-1}(p)\mbox{ and }\alpha(k)<p,\\
\alpha(k+1)-1\mbox{ if }k\geqslant\alpha^{-1}(p)\mbox{ and }\alpha(k)>p.\end{cases}

(3)

In other words, if we represent
\alpha
by a word
\alpha_{1}\ldots\alpha_{n}
, then
\alpha_{p}
is represented
by the word obtained by deleting the letter
p
in
\alpha_{1}\ldots\alpha_{n}

and subtracting
1
to all the letters
>p
.

(c)

(Compatibility with the horizontal concatenation).
For any
k,l,k^{\prime},l^{\prime}\geqslant 1
, for any
i\in[k+l]
,
j\in[k^{\prime}+l^{\prime}]
, for any
p\in P(k,l)
,
p^{\prime}\in P(k^{\prime},l^{\prime})
:

t_{i,j}(p*p^{\prime})=\begin{cases}t_{i,j}(p)*p^{\prime}\mbox{ if }i\leqslant k,\>j\leqslant l,\\
p*t_{i-k,j-l}(p^{\prime})\mbox{ if }i>k,\>j>l.\end{cases}

The TRAP is
unitary
if moreover
there exists
I\in P(1,1)
such that for any
k,l\geqslant 1
, for any
i\in[k+1]
,
j\in[l+1]
,
for any
p\in P(k,l)
:

\displaystyle t_{1,j}(I*p)

\displaystyle=(1,2,\ldots,j-1)\cdot p\mbox{ if }j\geqslant 2,

\displaystyle t_{i,1}(I*p)

\displaystyle=p\cdot(1,2,\ldots,i-1)^{-1}\mbox{ if }i\geqslant 2,

\displaystyle t_{k+1,j}(p*I)

\displaystyle=(j,j+1,\ldots,{k})^{-1}\cdot p\mbox{ if }j\leqslant{k},

\displaystyle t_{i,l+1}(p*I)

\displaystyle=p\cdot(i,i+1,\ldots,{l})\mbox{ if }i\leqslant{l}.

Remark 1.1.1
.

By commutativity of
*
, for any
p\in P(0,0)
, for any
(k,l)\in\mathbb{N}_{0}^{2}
, for any
p^{\prime}\in P(k,l)
:

p*p^{\prime}=p^{\prime}*p,

since
c_{0,k}=\mathrm{Id}_{[k]}
.

Remark 1.1.2
.

The abuse of notation
t_{i,j}
is legitimate since a full notation such as
t_{i,j}^{k,l}
is not necessary in practice. Indeed the indices
k
and
l
in
t_{i,j}(p)
are entirely determined by the element
p
to which
t_{i,j}
is applied.

More so, if
P
is unitary,
t_{i,j}{(p)}
does not strongly depend on
k
and
l
determined by
p
: indeed, let
f:P(k,l)\longrightarrow P(k+1,l+1)
be the map that sends
p
to
p*I

then for
i\in[k]
and
j\in[l]
, we have

t_{i,j}\circ f(p)=f\circ t_{i,j}(p),

which is the axiom 3.(c).

Remark 1.1.3
.

Notice that our TRAPs can be seen as wheeled PROPs without units, or equivalently a wheeled PROP is a TRAP with unit (see Theorem
5.3.1
below). As for wheeled PROPs, TRAPs can be defined in a much more concise way as an algebra over a given monad (see Section
5
). We choose a more pedestrian definition of TRAPs which will allow us in Section
2
to prove that known analytic and geometric spaces carry TRAP structures.

Definition 1.1.2
.

We call
sub-TRAP
of a TRAP
P=(P(k,l))_{k,l\geqslant 0}
, a
\mathfrak{S}\times\mathfrak{S}^{op}
-submodule
Q=(Q(k,l))_{k,l\geqslant 0}
of
P
which contains the unit
I_{0}\in P(0,0)
and is stable under the partial trace map of
P
. If the TRAP
P
is unitary, then the sub-trap
Q
is unitary if it contains the unit
I\in P(1,1)
.

Definition 1.1.3
.

Let
P=(P(k,l))_{k,l{\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}}
and
Q=(Q(k,l))_{k,l\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}
be two TRAPs with partial trace maps
(t_{i,j}^{P})_{i,j{\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}}
and
(t_{i,j}^{Q})_{i,j{\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}}

respectively. A
morphism of TRAPs
is a family
\phi=(\phi(k,l))_{k,l{\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}}

of morphisms of
\mathfrak{S}\times\mathfrak{S}^{op}
-modules
\phi(k,l):P(k,l)\longrightarrow Q(k,l)
which are morphisms for the horizontal concatenation,
and the partial trace maps. More precisely, for any
(k,l,m,n)\in\mathbb{N}_{0}^{4}
:

•

For any
(\sigma,p,\tau)
in
\mathfrak{S}_{l}\times P(k,l)\times\mathfrak{S}_{k}
,
\phi(k,l)(\sigma\cdot p\cdot\tau)=\sigma\cdot\phi(k,l)(p)\cdot\tau
.

•

\phi(0,0)(I_{0})=I_{0}
.

•

\forall(p,q)\in P(k,l)\times P(n,m),\penalty\ \phi(k+n,l+m)(p*q)=\phi(k,l)(p)*\phi(n,m)(q)
,

•

\forall(p,i,j)\in P(k,l)\times[k]\times[l]
,
\phi(k-1,l-1)\circ t^{P}_{i,j}(p)=t^{Q}_{i,j}\circ\phi(k,l)(p)
.

With a slight abuse of notations, we write
\phi(p)
instead of
\phi(k,l)(p)
for
p\in P(k,l)
.
In particular, TRAPs form a category, which we denote by
\mathbf{TRAP}
.

If
P
and
Q
are unitary TRAPs with units
I_{P}
and
I_{Q}
and
\phi:P\longrightarrow Q
is a morphism of TRAPs, this morphism is unitary if

\phi(1,1)(I_{P})=I_{Q}
. Unitary TRAPs form a subcategory of
\mathbf{TRAP}
, denoted by
\mathbf{uTRAP}
.

Remark 1.1.4
.

In Theorem
5.3.1
we shall see that
\mathbf{uTRAP}
coincides with the category of wheeled PROPs.

Let us simplify the axioms:

Lemma 1.1.4
.

Let
P=(P(k,l))_{k,l\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}
be a
\mathfrak{S}\times\mathfrak{S}^{op}
-module, equipped with a horizontal concatenation
*

satisfying axioms 2. (a)-(d), and with maps
t_{i,j}
satisfying axioms 3. (a)-(b).

1.

We assume that for any
k,l,k^{\prime},l^{\prime}\geqslant 1
, for any
p\in P(k,l)
,
p^{\prime}\in P(k^{\prime},l^{\prime})
,

t_{1,1}(p*p^{\prime})=t_{1,1}(p)*p^{\prime}.

Then Axiom 3.(c) is satisfied.

2.

Let
I\in P(1,1)
. We assume for any
k,l\geqslant 1
, for any
p\in P(k,l)
,

t_{1,2}(I*p)=p.

Then
I
is a unit of
P
.

Proof.

1. Let
p\in P(k,l)
and
p^{\prime}\in P(k^{\prime},l^{\prime})
. Let us take
i\in[k+l]
,
j\in[k^{\prime}+l^{\prime}]
, consider the transpositions
\sigma=(1,j)
and
\tau=(1,i)
, with the convention
(1,1)=\mathrm{Id}
.
If
i\leqslant k
and
j\leqslant l
, then:

\displaystyle t_{i,j}(p*p^{\prime})

\displaystyle=t_{i,j}(\sigma^{2}\cdot(p*p^{\prime})\cdot\tau^{2})

\displaystyle=\sigma_{j}\cdot t_{1,1}(\sigma\cdot(p*p^{\prime})\cdot\tau)\cdot\tau_{i}

\displaystyle=\sigma_{j}\cdot(t_{1,1}((\sigma\cdot p\cdot\tau)*p^{\prime})\cdot\tau_{i}

\displaystyle=\sigma_{j}\cdot(t_{1,1}(\sigma\cdot p\cdot\tau)*p^{\prime})\cdot\tau_{i}

\displaystyle=(\sigma_{j}\cdot(t_{1,1}(\sigma\cdot p\cdot\tau)\cdot\tau_{i})*p^{\prime}

\displaystyle=t_{i,j}(p)*p^{\prime}.

If
i>k
and
j>l
, using
c_{m,n}^{-1}=c_{n,m}
:

\displaystyle t_{i,j}(p*p^{\prime})

\displaystyle=t_{i,j}(c_{l^{\prime},l}\cdot(p^{\prime}*p)\cdot c_{k,k^{\prime}})

\displaystyle=(c_{l^{\prime},l})_{j}\cdot t_{i-k,j-l}(p^{\prime}*p)\cdot(c_{k,k^{\prime}})_{i}

\displaystyle=c_{l^{\prime}-1,l}\cdot(t_{i-k,j-l}(p^{\prime})*p)\cdot c_{k,k^{\prime}-1}

\displaystyle=p*t_{i-k,j-l}(p^{\prime}).

2. Let us take
j\geqslant 2
.

\displaystyle t_{1,j}(I*p)

\displaystyle=t_{1,j}((2,j)^{2}\cdot(I*p))

\displaystyle=(2,\ldots,j-1)\cdot t_{1,2}((2,j)\cdot(I*p))

\displaystyle=(2,\ldots,j-1)\cdot t_{1,2}(I*(1,j-1)\cdot p))

\displaystyle=(2,\ldots,j-1)\cdot((1,j-1)\cdot p)

\displaystyle=(2,\ldots,j-1)(1,j-1)\cdot p

\displaystyle=(1,\ldots,j-1)\cdot p.

The other three relations are proved in the same way.
∎

We can also simplify the axioms for morphisms of TRAPs.

Lemma 1.1.5
.

Let
P
and
Q
be two TRAPs and
\phi:P\longrightarrow Q
be a map. We assume that:

1.

For any
(k,l)\in\mathbb{N}_{0}^{2}
, for any
(\sigma,\tau)\in\mathfrak{S}_{l}\times\mathfrak{S}_{k}
, for any
x\in P(k,l)
,

\phi(\sigma\cdot x\cdot\tau)=\sigma\cdot\phi(x)\cdot\tau.

2.

For any
k,l\geqslant 1
, for any
x\in P(k,l)
,

t_{1,1}\circ\phi(x)=\phi\circ t_{1,1}(x).

Then
\phi
is a map of TRAPs.

Proof.

If
i\in[k]
,
j\in[l]
, and
x\in P(k,l)
:

\displaystyle\phi\circ t_{i,j}(x)

\displaystyle=\phi\circ t_{i,j}((1,j)^{2}\cdot x\cdot(1,i)^{2})

\displaystyle=\phi((1,j)\cdot t_{1,1}((1,j)\cdot x\cdot(1,i))\cdot(1,i))

\displaystyle=(1,j)\cdot\phi\circ t_{1,1}((1,j)\cdot x\cdot(1,i))\cdot(1,i)

\displaystyle=(1,j)\cdot t_{1,1}\circ\phi((1,j)\cdot x\cdot(1,i))\cdot(1,i)

\displaystyle=t_{i,j}((1,j)\cdot\phi((1,j)\cdot x\cdot(1,i))\cdot(1,i))

\displaystyle=t_{i,j}\circ\phi(x),

with the convention
(1,1)=\mathrm{Id}
. So
\Phi
is a morphism of TRAPs. ∎

In particular, to show that a collection of linear maps between two TRAPs preserving
the horizontal concatenation and the actions of the symmetry group is a morphism of TRAPs, it is enough to check
the properties of Lemma
1.1.5
.

1.2
Quotient of TRAPs

This paragraph prepares for the construction of an embedding of a TRAP
P
in a unitary TRAP.

Lemma 1.2.1
.

Let
Q
be a TRAP and let
\sim
be an equivalence relation on
Q
which is compatible with the TRAP-structure on
Q
in the following sense.
For any two elements
x,x^{\prime}\in Q
, such that
x\sim x^{\prime}
:

•

(Compatibility with the module-structure). For any
(\sigma,\tau)\in\mathfrak{S}_{k}\times\mathfrak{S}_{l}
,

\tau\cdot x\cdot\sigma\sim\tau\cdot x^{\prime}\cdot\sigma
.

•

(Compatibility with the horizontal concatenation). For any
y\in Q
,
x*y\sim x^{\prime}*y
and
y*x\sim y*x^{\prime}
.

•

(Compatibility with the partial trace maps). For any
(i,j)\in[k]\times[l]
,
t_{i,j}(x)\sim t_{i,j}(x^{\prime})
.

Then the quotient
Q/\sim
is a TRAP with
[I_{0}]
as unit for the concatenation product. If
Q
is unitary with unit
I\in Q(1,1)
for the partial trace maps, then
Q/\sim
is also unitary with
[I]
as unit for the partial trace maps.

Proof.

1. By compatibility with the module structure,
Q/\sim
is a
\mathfrak{S}\times\mathfrak{S}^{op}
-modules.

2. Using twice the compatibility with partial trace maps, we find that if
x\sim x^{\prime}
and
y\sim y^{\prime}
, then
x*y\sim x^{\prime}*y^{\prime}

by transitivity of
\sim
. Thus the horizontal concatenation
[x]*[y]:=[x*y]
on
Q/\sim
is well-defined. It fulfils properties 2.(a) to 2.(d) of Definition
1.1.1
by construction.

3. We defined the partial trace maps on the quotient to be
t_{i,j}([x]):=[t_{i,j}(x)]
. It is well defined by compatibility with the partial trace maps and has properties 3.(a) to 3.(c) of Definition
1.1.1
by construction.

Thus
Q/\sim
is a TRAP. Finally, if
Q
is unitary with unit
I
, then
[I]
endows the quotient
Q/\sim
with a unit by construction and
Q/\sim
is then a unitary TRAP.
∎

The following statement is a direct consequence.

Proposition 1.2.2
.

Let
P
be a TRAP and
\Phi:Q\to P
a TRAP-morphism. The relation

x\sim x^{\prime}\Longleftrightarrow\Phi(x)=\Phi(x^{\prime})

defines an equivalence relation compatible with the TRAP-structure on
Q
and
Q/\sim
defines a TRAP.

Proof.

Let
x
,
x^{\prime}
in
Q
, such that
x\sim x^{\prime}
.

•

For any
(\sigma,\tau)\in\mathfrak{S}_{k}\times\mathfrak{S}_{l}
,

\Phi(\sigma\cdot x\cdot\tau)=\sigma\cdot\Phi(x)\cdot\tau=\sigma\cdot\Phi(x^{\prime})\cdot\tau=\Phi(\sigma\cdot x\cdot\tau),

where the first and last identities follow from the fact that
\Phi
is a morphism of
\mathfrak{S}\times\mathfrak{S}^{op}
-modules.
Thus
\mathfrak{S}
is compatible with the module structure.

•

For any
y
in
Q
we have

\Phi(x*y)=\Phi(x)*\Phi(y)=\Phi(x^{\prime})*\Phi(y)=\Phi(x^{\prime}*y)

where we have used the fact that
\Phi
is a morphism for the horizontal concatenation product (since
\Phi
is a morphism of TRAPs) and the fact that
\Phi(x)=\Phi(x^{\prime})
. Thus
x*y\sim x^{\prime}*y
. Similarly we show that
y*x\sim y^{\prime}*x^{\prime}
and
\sim
is compatible with the horizontal concatenation.

•

For any
(i,j)\in[k]\times[l]
we have

\Phi(t_{i,j}(x))=t_{i,j}(\Phi(x))=t_{i,j}(\Phi(x^{\prime}))=\Phi(t_{i,j}(x^{\prime})),

where the first and last identities follow from the fact that
\Phi
is a morphism of TRAPs.

Thus
\sim
is compatible with the partial trace maps.∎

2
Fundamental examples

2.1
The Hom TRAP

Let us give a fundamental example of unitary TRAP:

Example 2.1.1
.

Let
V
be a finite dimensional vector space and
V^{*}
its algebraic dual. We consider the family

\mathbf{Hom}_{V}=\left(\mathbf{Hom}_{V}(k,l)\right)_{k,l\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}:={(\mathbf{Hom}(V^{\otimes k},V^{\otimes l}))_{(k,l)\in\mathbb{N}_{0}^{2}}},

where for any
(k,l)\in\mathbb{N}_{0}^{2}
,
\mathbf{Hom}(V^{\otimes k},V^{\otimes l})
is the vector space of linear maps from
V^{\otimes k}
to
V^{\otimes l}
.
We shall identify
\mathbf{Hom}_{V}(k,l)
and
{V^{*\otimes k}\otimes V^{\otimes l}}
through the isomorphism

\theta_{k,l}:\left\{\begin{array}[]{rcl}{V^{*\otimes k}\otimes V^{\otimes l}}&\longrightarrow&\mathbf{Hom}_{V}(k,l)\\
{f_{1}\ldots f_{k}\otimes v_{1}\ldots v_{l}}&\longmapsto&\left\{\begin{array}[]{rcl}V^{\otimes k}&\longrightarrow&V^{\otimes l}\\
x_{1}\ldots x_{k}&\longmapsto&f_{1}(x_{1})\ldots f_{k}(x_{k})\,v_{1}\ldots v_{l},\end{array}\right.\end{array}\right.

where with some abuse of notation, we have set
f_{1}\cdots f_{k}:=f_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes f_{k}\in V^{*\otimes k}
and

v_{1}\cdots v_{l}:=v_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes v_{l}\in V^{\otimes l}
. For any vector space
W
, the tensor power
W^{\otimes k}
is a left
\mathfrak{S}_{k}
-module
with the action defined by

\sigma\cdot w_{1}\ldots w_{k}=w_{\sigma^{-1}(1)}\ldots w_{\sigma^{-1}(k)}.

Via the identification
\theta:=\left(\theta_{k,l}\right)_{(k,l)\in\mathbb{N}_{0}^{2}}
, we equip the family
\mathbf{Hom}_{V}=(\mathbf{Hom}(V^{\otimes k},V^{\otimes l}))_{(k,l)\in\mathbb{N}_{0}^{2}}
with a structure
of
\mathfrak{S}_{l}\times\mathfrak{S}_{k}^{op}
by putting, for an
f\in\mathbf{Hom}(V^{\otimes k},V^{\otimes l})
,
for any
(\sigma,\tau)\in\mathfrak{S}_{k}\times\mathfrak{S}_{l}
:

\displaystyle\forall v_{1}\ldots v_{k}\in V^{\otimes l},

\displaystyle\tau\cdot f\cdot\sigma(v_{1}\ldots v_{k})=\tau\cdot f(\sigma\cdot v_{1}\ldots v_{k}).

(4)

The horizontal concatenation is the usual tensor product of linear maps:
if
f\in\mathbf{Hom}_{V}(k,l)
and
g\in\mathbf{Hom}_{V}(k^{\prime},l^{\prime})
, then

f\otimes g:\left\{\begin{array}[]{rcl}V^{\otimes(k+k^{\prime})}&\longrightarrow&V^{\otimes(l+l^{\prime})}\\
v_{1}\ldots v_{k+k^{\prime}}&\longmapsto&f(v_{1}\ldots v_{k})\otimes g(v_{k+1}\ldots v_{k+k^{\prime}}).\end{array}\right.

We define the following partial trace maps:

\displaystyle t_{i,j}(\theta_{k,l}(f_{1}\ldots f_{k}\otimes v_{1}\ldots v_{l}))

\displaystyle=f_{i}(v_{j})\,\theta_{k-1,l-1}(f_{1}\ldots f_{i-1}f_{i+1}\ldots f_{k}\otimes v_{1}\ldots v_{j-1}v_{j+1}\ldots v_{l}).

(5)

Remark 2.1.1
.

Notice that for
p\in\mathbf{Hom}_{V}(1,1)
,
t_{1,1}(p)
coincides with the usual trace of a linear map on
V
. Proposition
4.2.1
generalises the notion of trace to any element of
\mathbf{Hom}_{V}
and to any TRAP.

Proposition 2.1.1
.

For a finite dimensional vector space
V
, the above construction equips
\mathbf{Hom}_{V}
with a TRAP structure which is unitary, with unit given by the identity of
V
. In other words,
\mathbf{Hom}_{V}
is a wheeled PROP.

Proof.

Properties 2.(a)-(d) are trivially satisfied, with
I_{0}=1\in\mathbb{K}
.
Property 3.(a) is direct. Let us prove Property 3.(b).

\displaystyle t_{i,j}(\sigma\cdot\theta_{k,l}(f_{1}\ldots f_{k}\otimes v_{1}\ldots v_{l})\cdot\tau)

\displaystyle=t_{i,j}\circ\theta_{k,l}(f_{\tau(1)}\ldots f_{\tau(k)}\otimes v_{\sigma^{-1}(1)}\ldots v_{\sigma^{-1}(l)})

\displaystyle=f_{\tau(i)}(v_{\sigma^{-1}(j)})\theta_{k-1,l-1}(f_{\tau(1)}\ldots f_{\tau(i-1)}f_{\tau(i+1)}\ldots f_{\tau(k)}

\displaystyle\otimes v_{\sigma^{-1}(1)}\ldots v_{\sigma^{-1}(j-1)}v_{\sigma^{-1}(j+1)}\ldots v_{\sigma^{-1}(l)})

\displaystyle=\sigma_{j}\cdot t_{\tau(i),\sigma^{-1}(j)}\theta_{k,l}(f_{1}\ldots f_{k}\otimes v_{1}\ldots v_{l})\cdot\tau_{i}.

Property 3.(c) is straightforward. Let us prove that
\mathbf{Hom}_{V}
is unitary with the help of Lemma
1.1.4
. Let us fix
(e_{i})_{i\in I}
a basis of
V
, then
(e_{i}^{*})_{i\in I}
is a basis of
V^{*}
and the identity map of
V
is

\displaystyle\mathrm{Id}_{V}=\theta_{1,1}\left(\sum_{i\in I}e_{i}\otimes e_{i}^{*}\right).

(6)

Then for any
p=\theta_{k,l}(f_{1}\ldots f_{k}\otimes v_{1}\ldots v_{l})\in\mathbf{Hom}_{V}(k,l)

\displaystyle t_{1,2}(\mathrm{Id}_{V}*\theta_{k,l}(f_{1}\ldots f_{k}\otimes v_{1}\ldots v_{l}))

\displaystyle=\sum_{i\in I}t_{1,2}\circ\theta_{k+1,l+1}(e_{i}^{*}f_{1}\ldots f_{k}\otimes e_{i}v_{1}\ldots v_{l})

\displaystyle=\sum_{i\in I}\theta_{k,l}(f_{1}\ldots f_{k}\otimes e_{i}\,e_{i}^{*}(v_{1})v_{2}\ldots v_{l})

\displaystyle=\theta_{k,l}(f_{1}\ldots f_{k}\otimes\mathrm{Id}_{V}(v_{1})v_{2}\ldots v_{l})

\displaystyle=\theta_{k,l}(f_{1}\ldots f_{k}\otimes v_{1}\ldots v_{l}).

So
\mathbf{Hom}_{V}
is a unitary TRAP.
∎

When
V
is not finite-dimensional,
\theta
is an injective, non surjective map. Its range is the subpsace
\mathbf{Hom}_{V}^{fr}
of linear
maps from
V^{\otimes k}
to
V^{\otimes l}
of finite rank. We can equip
\mathbf{Hom}_{V}^{fr}
with a similar TRAP structure:

Proposition 2.1.2
.

With the
\mathfrak{S}\times\mathfrak{S}^{op}
action defined by (
4
), the usual tensor product of maps
and the partial trace maps defined by (
5
),
\mathbf{Hom}_{V}^{fr}
is a TRAP. It is unitary if, and only if,
V

is finite-dimensional.

Proof.

We skip the proof that
\mathbf{Hom}_{V}^{fr}
can be equipped with a TRAP structure since it goes as for
\mathbf{Hom}_{V}
when
V
is finite dimensional. Note that when
V
is finite-dimensional, then
\mathbf{Hom}_{V}^{fr}=\mathbf{Hom}_{V}
is a unitary TRAP. We show the second part of the statement.

Let us assume that
\mathbf{Hom}_{V}^{fr}
has a unit
I
. Then
I
has finite rank, let us fix a basis
(e_{1},\ldots,e_{k})
of
\mathrm{Im}({I})
. There exist
\lambda_{1},\ldots,\lambda_{k}\in V^{*}
such that for any
v\in V
,

I(v)=\sum_{i=1}^{k}\lambda_{i}(v)e_{i}.

In other words,

I=\theta_{1,1}\left({\sum_{i=1}^{k}e_{i}\otimes\lambda_{i}}\right).

Let
v\in V
, nonzero, and let
\lambda\in V^{*}
such that
\lambda(v)=1
. We consider
f=\theta_{1,1}(v\otimes\lambda)
.
Then
f(v)=\lambda(v)v=v
. Moreover:

\displaystyle v

\displaystyle=f(v)=t_{1,2}(I*f)(v)

\displaystyle=t_{1,2}\circ\theta_{2,2}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{k}{e_{i}}\,v\otimes\lambda_{i}\lambda\right)(v)

\displaystyle=\theta_{1,1}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{k}{\lambda_{i}}{(v)\,e_{i}}\otimes{\lambda}\right)(v)

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{k}\lambda(v)\lambda_{i}(v)\,{e_{i}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{k}\lambda_{i}(v)\,{e_{i}},

so
v\in\mathrm{Vect}(e_{1},\ldots,e_{k})
. Hence,
V\subseteq\mathrm{Im}(I)
, so
V
is finite-dimensional.
∎

We end this paragraph with an example of a TRAP similar to Example
2.1.1
but of a more geometric nature.

Example 2.1.2

(The TRAP of tensors)
.

Given a finite dimensional smooth manifold
M
and a point
x\in M
, we build the
\mathbf{Hom}
-TRAP
\mathbf{Hom}_{T_{x}M}
where
T_{x}M
is the tangent space to
M
at the point
x
.
Given a pair
(p,q)\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geqslant 0}^{2}
we have

\mathbf{Hom}_{T_{x}M}(p,q)\simeq(T_{x}^{*}M)^{\otimes p}\otimes T_{x}M^{\otimes q},

where we have set
V^{\otimes 0}=\mathbb{R}
. The partial trace maps are built by pairing cotangent and tangent vectors.
We note that if
M
is equipped with a Riemannian metric, thanks to the musical isomorphisms
T_{x}^{*}M\ni\alpha\longmapsto\alpha^{\sharp}\in T_{x}M
and
T_{x}M\ni v\longmapsto v^{\flat}\in T_{x}^{*}M
between
T_{x}^{*}M
and
T_{x}M
, these dual pairings can be seen as contractions via the metric tensor.

This yields a smooth fibration
\mathbf{Hom}_{TM}:=\{\mathbf{Hom}_{T_{x}M},x\in M\}
of TRAPs parametrised by
M
. For any
(p,q)\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geqslant 0}^{2}
, a smooth section of
\mathbf{Hom}_{TM}(p,q)
defines a smooth
(p,q)
tensor on
M
.

2.2
The TRAP of continuous morphisms

We generalise the constructions of the previous paragraph, replacing the finite dimensional spaces
V^{\otimes k}
in
\mathbf{Hom}_{V}
by nuclear spaces.
These nuclear spaces were defined in the seminal work
[
Gro54
]
. Most of the results stated here can be found in
[
Gro52
,
Gro54
]
. We also
refer to the more recent presentation
[
Trè67
]
.

We recall that:

•

A topological vector space is
Fréchet
if it is Hausdorff, has its topology induced by a countable family of semi-norms and is complete with respect
to this family of semi-norms.

•

The topological dual
E^{\prime}
of a locally convex topological vector space
E
can be endowed with various topologies, one of which is
the
strong topology
, namely the topology of uniform convergence on the bounded domains of
E
. It is generated by the family of semi-norms of
E^{\prime}
defined on any
f\in E^{\prime}
by
||f||_{B}:=\sup_{x\in B}|f(x)|

for any bounded set
B
of
E
. The topological dual
E^{\prime}
endowed with this topology is called the
strong dual
.

•

A topological vector space is called
reflexive
if
E^{\prime\prime}=(E^{\prime})^{\prime}=E
, where
E^{\prime}
is the topological dual of
E
endowed with the strong topology.

In the following
E
and
F
are two topological vector spaces and
\mathbf{Hom}^{c}(E,F)
is the set of continuous linear maps from
E
to
F
.

Remark 2.2.1
.

•

When
E
and
F
are finite dimensional, we have
\mathbf{Hom}^{c}(E,F)
=
\mathbf{Hom}(E,F)
.

•

As pointed out to us by Mark Johnson, a natural generalisation to consider in the context of Fréchet spaces are
\sigma

C^{*}
-algebras, defined as inverse limits of
C^{*}
-algebras
[
Phi88
]
, which however lie out of the scope of the present article.

In order to build the TRAP
\mathbf{Hom}_{V}^{c}
for nuclear spaces, we need Grothendieck’s completion of the tensor product, a notion we recall
here in the set-up of locally convex topological
\mathbb{K}
-vector spaces.

Let
E
and
F
be two vector spaces. Recall that there exists a vector space
E\otimes F
, and a bilinear map

\phi:E\times F\longrightarrow E\otimes F
such that for any vector space
V
and bilinear map
f:E\times F\longrightarrow V
, there is a unique
linear map
\tilde{f}:E\otimes F\to V
satisfying
f=\tilde{f}\circ\phi
. The space
E\otimes F
is unique modulo isomorphism and is called the
tensor product
of

E
and
F
.

Given two topological vector spaces
E
and
F
, one can a priori equip
E\otimes F
with several topologies, among which the

\epsilon
-topology
and the
projective topology
whose construction are recalled in Appendix
A
. We
denote by
E\otimes_{\epsilon}F
(resp.
E\otimes_{\pi}F
) the space
E\otimes F
endowed with
the
\epsilon
-topology (resp. the projective topology) and by

E\widehat{\otimes}_{\epsilon}F
(resp.
E\widehat{\otimes}_{\epsilon}F
) of
E\otimes_{\epsilon}F
(resp.
E\otimes_{\epsilon}F
) their completion with respect to the

\epsilon
-topology (resp. projective topology). These two spaces differ in general but coincide for nuclear spaces.

Definition 2.2.1
.

[
Gro54
]

A locally convex topological vector space
E
is
nuclear
if, and only if, for any locally convex topological vector space
F
,

E\widehat{\otimes}_{\epsilon}F=E\widehat{\otimes}_{\pi}F=:E\widehat{\otimes}F

holds, in which case
E\widehat{\otimes}F
is called the
completed tensor product
of
E
and
F
.

There are other equivalent definitions of nuclearity, see for example
[
GV64
,
HS08
]
.

Remark 2.2.2
.

It was pointed out to us by Mark Johnson that such minimal and maximal tensor products, much used in the context of
C^{*}
-algebras, further extend to l.m.c.
C^{*}
-algebras, where l.m.c stands for locally multiplicative convex (see
[
JJ06
]
and references therein).

For Fréchet spaces, nuclearity is preserved under strong duality.

Proposition 2.2.2
.

•

[
Trè67
, Proposition 50.6]

A Fréchet space is nuclear if and only if its strong dual is nuclear.

•

[
Trè67
, Proposition 36.5]
A Fréchet nuclear space is reflexive.

Many spaces relevant to renormalisation issues are Fréchet and nuclear. We list here some examples.

Example 2.2.1
.

Any finite dimensional vector space can be equipped with a norm and for any of these norms, they are trivially Banach, hence Fréchet and nuclear.
If
E
and
F
are finite dimensional vector spaces we have

\mathbf{Hom}^{c}(E,F)=\mathbf{Hom}(E,F)\simeq E^{*}\otimes F
, where
\mathbf{Hom}(E,F)
stands for the space of
F
-valued linear maps on

E
and where the dual
E^{*}
is the
algebraic dual
.

Example 2.2.2
.

Let
U
be an open subset of
\mathbb{R}^{n}
.
Take
E=C^{\infty}(U)=:{\mathcal{E}}(U)
. The topological dual
is the space
{E^{\prime}}={\mathcal{E}}^{\prime}(U)
of distributions
on
U
with compact support.
Then
E
is Fréchet (
[
Trè67
]
, pp. 86-89), and
E^{\prime}
is nuclear (
[
Trè67
]
, Corollary p. 530). By Proposition

•
‣
2.2.2
,
E
is also nuclear.

Remark 2.2.3
.

Note that the dual
E^{\prime}
of a Fréchet space
E
is never a Fréchet space (for any of the natural topologies on
E^{\prime}
), unless
E
is actually a Banach space (see for example

[
Köt69
]
). In particular,
{\mathcal{E}}^{\prime}(U)
is generally not Fréchet.

We now sum up various results of
[
Trè67
]
of importance for later purposes.

Proposition 2.2.3
.

[
Trè67
, Equations (50.17)–(50.19)]

Let
E
and
F
be two Fréchet spaces, with
E
nuclear. The following isomorphisms of topological vector spaces hold.

\displaystyle E^{\prime}\widehat{\otimes}F\simeq\mathbf{Hom}^{c}(E,F)

(7)

\displaystyle E\widehat{\otimes}F\simeq\mathbf{Hom}^{c}(E^{\prime},F)

(8)

\displaystyle E^{\prime}\widehat{\otimes}F^{\prime}\simeq(E\widehat{\otimes}F)^{\prime}\simeq{\mathcal{B}}^{c}(E\times F,\mathbb{K}).

(9)

with
{\mathcal{B}}^{c}(E\times F,\mathbb{K})
the set of continuous bilinear maps

K:E\times F\longrightarrow\mathbb{K}
. Here the duals are endowed with the strong dual topology,

\mathbf{Hom}^{c}(E,F)\simeq E^{\prime}\otimes F
with the topology of uniform convergence on the bounded subsets of
E
6
6

6

It is defined by a family of semi-norms
p_{B,\pi_{i}}
of the form
p_{B,\pi_{i}}(f)=\sup_{x\in B}\pi_{i}(f(x))
when applied to some
f
in
{\rm Hom}^{c}(E,F)
, where
B
runs through the sets of all bounded subsets of
E
and
\pi_{i}
runs through a countable family of semi-norms which generate the topology of
F
. It gives back the strong topology on
E^{\prime}
if
F
is the underlying field. It also carries other names such as ”topology of bounded convergence”
[
Bou03
, page III.14]
,
[
Sch71
, p.81]
and
{\mathcal{B}}^{c}(E\times F,\mathbb{K})
with the topology of uniform convergence on products of bounded sets.

The stability of Fréchet nuclear spaces under completed tensor products, It follows from combining and the definition of the completed tensor product with the fact that if
E
and
F
are two nuclear spaces then
E\widehat{\otimes}F
is a nuclear space (
[
Trè67
, Equation (50.9)]
). A stronger version of this Lemma is mentioned in
[
BB03
, §6.f, page 182]
which quotes
[
Gro54
]
.

Lemma 2.2.4
.

The completed tensor product
E\widehat{\otimes}F
of two Fréchet nuclear spaces is a Fréchet nuclear space.

Proposition 2.2.5
.

Let
V
be a Fréchet nuclear space. Then

\left(V^{\widehat{\otimes}k}\right)^{\prime}\simeq\left(V^{\prime}\right)^{\widehat{\otimes}k}

(10)

holds for any
k\geqslant 1
, where the duals are endowed with their strong topologies.

Proof.

Let
V
be a Fréchet nuclear space. The case
k=1
is trivial. Then Equation (
10
) with
k=2
holds by Equation (
9
)
with
E=F=V
. The cases
k\geqslant 2
are proved by induction, using
E=V^{\widehat{\otimes}k-1}
and
F=V
. The induction holds by Lemma

2.2.4
.
∎

We denote by
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}({M})
the set of distributions on
{X}
and by
\mathcal{E}^{\prime}(M)
, the set of distributions with
compact support on a finite dimensional smooth manifold
M
, see e.g.
[
Hö89
, Definition 6.3.3]
.
It is well-known (see e.g.
[
vdBC13
, Exercise 2.3.2]
,
[
BDLGR17
, p. 4]
) that
\mathcal{E}(M)
is a Fréchet nuclear space.
It then follows from Proposition
•
‣
2.2.2
, that the space
\mathcal{E}^{\prime}(M)
is also nuclear.

Remark 2.2.4
.

(Compare with Remark
2.2.3
). Note that the
space
\mathcal{E}^{\prime}(M)
is
not
Fréchet since the dual of a Fréchet space
F
is Fréchet if and only if
F
is Banach (see for example

[
Köt69
]
) which is not the
case of
\mathcal{E}(M)
.

One further useful result is:

Proposition 2.2.6
.

Let
M
and
{N}
be two finite dimensional smooth manifolds. Then

\mathbf{Hom}^{c}(\mathcal{E}^{\prime}(M),\mathcal{E}(N))\simeq\,\mathcal{E}(M)\,\widehat{\otimes}\,\mathcal{E}(N)\simeq\mathcal{E}(M\times N)

holds.

The second isomorphism
[
Gro52
, Chap. 5, p. 105]
can be proved using a version of the Schwartz kernel theorem for smoothing operators

[
vdBC13
, Theorem 2.4.5]
by means of the identification
\mathbf{Hom}^{c}(\mathcal{E}^{\prime}(M),\mathcal{E}(N))\simeq\mathcal{E}(M\times N)
. The result then follows from
(
8
) applied to
\mathcal{E}(X)
and
\mathcal{E}(Y)
which are Fréchet nuclear spaces.

Definition 2.2.7
.

Let
V
be a Fréchet nuclear space. For any
k,l\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
, we set

\mathbf{Hom}_{V}^{c}(k,l)=\mathbf{Hom}^{c}(V^{\hat{\otimes}k},V^{\hat{\otimes}l})\simeq(V^{\prime})^{\widehat{\otimes}k}\widehat{\otimes}V^{\widehat{\otimes}l},

where, as before
V^{\prime}
stands for the strong topological dual. Furthermore we set
\mathbf{Hom}^{c}_{V}:=(\mathbf{Hom}_{V}^{c}(k,l))_{k,l\geqslant 0}
.

For any
\sigma\in\mathfrak{S}_{n}
, let
\Theta_{\sigma}
be the endomorphism of
V^{\otimes n}
defined by

\Theta_{\sigma}(v_{1}\otimes\ldots\otimes v_{n})=v_{\sigma^{-1}(1)}\otimes\ldots\otimes v_{\sigma^{-1}(n)}.

It extends to a continuous linear map

\overline{\Theta_{\sigma}}
on the closure

V^{\widehat{\otimes}n}
.
For any
f\in\mathbf{Hom}_{V}^{c}(k,l)
,
\sigma\in\mathfrak{S}_{l}
,
\tau\in\mathfrak{S}_{k}
, we set:

\displaystyle\sigma\cdot f

\displaystyle=\overline{\Theta_{\sigma}}\circ f,

\displaystyle f\cdot\tau

\displaystyle=f\circ\overline{\Theta_{\tau}}.

In the above definition, the superscript “c” stands for continuous. The family
\mathbf{Hom}_{V}^{c}
carries a TRAP structure.

Theorem 2.2.8
.

Let
V
be a Fréchet nuclear space.
\mathbf{Hom}_{V}^{c}
, with the action of
\mathfrak{S}\times\mathfrak{S}^{\mathrm{op}}
described above, is a TRAP. Its horizontal
concatenation is the usual (topological) tensor product of maps with
I_{0}:\mathbb{K}\longrightarrow\mathbb{K}
given by the
identity map of
\mathbb{K}
, its partial trace maps coincide with those of the TRAP
\mathbf{Hom}_{V}
on elements of
(V^{\prime})^{\widehat{\otimes}k}\widehat{\otimes}V^{\widehat{\otimes}l}

t_{i,j}(f_{1}\cdots f_{k}\otimes v_{1}\cdots v_{l})=f_{i}(v_{j})f_{1}\cdots f_{i-1}f_{i+1}\cdots f_{k}\otimes v_{1}\cdots v_{j-1}v_{j+1}\cdots v_{l}

(with the same notations than the ones of Subsection
2.1
).
It is unitary if and only if
V
is finite dimensional, in which case
I_{1}:V\longrightarrow V
is the identity map of
V
.

Proof.

The proof of the TRAP structure of
\mathbf{Hom}^{c}_{V}
goes as in Proposition
2.1.1
. The proof of the unital case is the same than the proof of Proposition
2.1.2
.
∎

Example 2.2.3
.

For a finite dimensional vector space
V
, the TRAP
\mathbf{Hom}_{V}^{c}
coincides with the TRAP
\mathbf{Hom}_{V}
.

Example 2.2.4
.

Let
M
be a smooth finite dimensional manifold.
From Proposition
2.2.6
and Equation (
10
), it follows that
the family
({\mathcal{K}}_{M}(k,l))_{k,l\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}
,
with
{\mathcal{K}}_{M}(k,l)=\left({\mathcal{E}}^{\prime}(M)\right)^{\widehat{\otimes}k}\,\widehat{\otimes}\,{\mathcal{E}}(M)^{\widehat{\otimes}l}

defines a TRAP.

2.3
The TRAP
\mathcal{K}_{M}^{\infty}
of smoothing pseudo-differential operators

We apply our results on TRAPs to tensor products
of Fréchet spaces
{\mathcal{E}}(M)
of smooth functions on a given smooth finite dimensional orientable closed manifold
M
and
\mu(z)
a volume form on
M
. Recall from Proposition

2.2.6
that such spaces are stable under tensor products and morphisms in
\mathrm{Hom}^{c}({\mathcal{E}}^{\prime}(M),{\mathcal{E}}(N))
are determined by smoothing kernels in
{\mathcal{E}}(M\times N)
.

We consider smooth kernels
which stabilise
{\mathcal{E}}(M)
and set, for
(k,l)\neq(0,0)
:

{\mathcal{K}}_{M}^{\infty}(k,l):={\mathcal{E}}({M}^{k}\times{M}^{l})\simeq{\mathcal{E}}({M})^{\widehat{\otimes}k}\,\hat{\otimes}\,{\mathcal{E}}({M})^{\widehat{\otimes}l},

(11)

whose elements we refer to as smooth generalised kernels.

Theorem 2.3.1
.

The family of topological vector spaces
\left({\mathcal{K}}_{M}^{\infty}(k,l)\right)_{k,l\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}
can be equipped with the partial trace maps

\displaystyle t_{i,j}

\displaystyle:\left\{\begin{array}[]{rcl}{\mathcal{K}}_{M}^{\infty}(k,l)&\longrightarrow&{\mathcal{K}}_{M}^{\infty}(k-1,l-1)\\
K_{1}\otimes K_{2}&\longmapsto&{t_{i,j}(K_{1}\otimes K_{2})}\end{array}\right.

with

t_{i,j}(K_{1}\otimes K_{2})
defined by

\displaystyle t_{i,j}(K_{1}\otimes K_{2})

\displaystyle(x_{1},\cdots,x_{k-1},y_{1},\cdots,y_{l-1}):=

(12)

\displaystyle\int_{M}K_{1}(x_{1},\cdots,x_{i-1},z,x_{i}\cdots,x_{k-1})\,K_{2}(y_{1},\cdots,y_{j-1},z,y_{j}\cdots,y_{k})\,d\mu(z).

Together with the horizontal concatenation given by the tensor product of maps
(K_{1}\otimes K_{2})*(K^{\prime}_{1}\otimes K^{\prime}_{2})=K_{a}\otimes K_{b}
with
K_{a}:=K_{1}\otimes K^{\prime}_{1}
and
K_{b}:=K_{2}\otimes K^{\prime}_{2}

this defines a TRAP, written
\mathcal{K}_{M}^{\infty}
, which we call the TRAP of
generalised smooth kernels
on
M
.

Remark 2.3.1
.

Note that the partial trace amounts to what one could call a partial convolution.

Proof.

The unit
I_{0}\in\mathcal{K}_{M}^{\infty}(0,0)\simeq\mathbb{C}\otimes\mathbb{C}
of the horizontal concatenation
*
is the constant map
f:\mathbb{C}\to\mathbb{C}
defined by

f(x)=1
. It is the unit of
*
by bilinearity of the tensor product.The horizontal concatenation on the
\mathfrak{S}\times\mathfrak{S}^{op}
-module
\left({\mathcal{K}}_{M}^{\infty}(k,l)\right)_{k,l\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}
satisfies axioms 2. (a)-(d) of Definition
1.1.1
. We want to check that the maps
t_{i,j}
are well-defined and satisfy axioms 3. (a)-(c).

The existence of the integral follows from the smoothness of
K_{1}
and
K_{2}
and the
closedness of
M
. It is enough to show that the function

t_{i,j}(K_{1}\otimes K_{2}):M^{k-1}\times M^{l-1}\longrightarrow\mathbb{C}
is smooth. Since

K_{1}
and
K_{2}
are smooth, the map

(x_{1},\cdots,x_{k-1},y_{1},\cdots,y_{k})\longmapsto K_{1}(x_{1},\cdots,x_{i-1},z,x_{i}\cdots,x_{k-1})\,K_{2}(y_{1},\cdots,y_{j-1},z,y_{j}\cdots,y_{k})

is infinitely differentiable for any
z\in M
. Since
M
is compact, the partial derivatives

\partial_{\vec{x}}^{\vec{\alpha}}\partial_{\vec{y}}^{\vec{\beta}}K_{1}(x_{1},\cdots,x_{i-1},z,x_{i}\cdots,x_{k-1})\,K_{2}(y_{1},\cdots,y_{j-1},z,y_{j}\cdots,y_{k})

are bounded uniformly in
z
and hence

\displaystyle\int_{M}\partial_{\vec{x}}^{\vec{\alpha}}\partial_{\vec{y}}^{\vec{\beta}}K_{1}(x_{1},\cdots,x_{i-1},z,x_{i}\cdots,x_{k-1})\,K_{2}(y_{1},\cdots,y_{j-1},z,y_{j}\cdots,y_{k})\,d\mu(z)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\partial_{\vec{x}}^{\vec{\alpha}}\partial_{\vec{y}}^{\vec{\beta}}\int_{M}K_{1}(x_{1},\cdots,x_{i-1},z,x_{i}\cdots,x_{k-1})\,K_{2}(y_{1},\cdots,y_{j-1},z,y_{j}\cdots,y_{k})\,d\mu(z)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\partial_{\vec{x}}^{\vec{\alpha}}\partial_{\vec{y}}^{\vec{\beta}}t_{i,j}(K_{1}\otimes K_{2})(x_{1},\cdots,x_{k-1},y_{1},\cdots,y_{l-1}).

Therefore the map
t_{i,j}(K_{1}\otimes K_{2})(x_{1},\cdots,x_{k-1},y_{1},\cdots,y_{l-1})
is smooth.

Finally, to check Axiom 3.(c), by the first item of Lemma
1.1.4
it is enough to check the compatibility of the horizontal concatenation with the partial trace to show that
t_{1,1}(p*p^{\prime})=t_{1,1}(p)*p^{\prime}
for any pair
(p,p^{\prime})\in\mathcal{K}_{M}^{\infty}(k,l)\times\mathcal{K}_{M}^{\infty}(k^{\prime},l^{\prime})
with
k,k^{\prime},l,l^{\prime}\geqslant 1
. Setting
p=K_{1}\otimes K_{2}
and
p^{\prime}=K^{\prime}_{1}\otimes K^{\prime}_{2}
we have, by definition of the partial trace maps and the horizontal concatenation

\displaystyle t_{1,1}(p*p^{\prime})(x_{1},\cdots,x_{k+k^{\prime}-1},y_{1},\cdots,y_{l+l^{\prime}-1})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int K_{1}(z,x_{1},\cdots,x_{k-1})K^{\prime}_{1}(x_{k},\cdots,x_{k+k^{\prime}-1})K_{2}(z,y_{1},\cdots,y_{l-1})K^{\prime}_{2}(x_{l},\cdots,x_{l+l^{\prime}-1})dz

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(\int K_{1}(z,x_{1},\cdots,x_{k-1})K_{2}(z,y_{1},\cdots,y_{l-1})dz\right)K^{\prime}_{1}(x_{k},\cdots,x_{k+k^{\prime}-1})K^{\prime}_{2}(x_{l},\cdots,x_{l+l^{\prime}-1})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(t_{1,1}(p)*p^{\prime})(x_{1},\cdots,x_{k+k^{\prime}-1},y_{1},\cdots,y_{l+l^{\prime}-1}).\qed

Remark 2.3.2
.

Notice that
\mathcal{K}_{M}^{\infty}
is non unitary, since the map
f:M\times M\longmapsto\mathbb{C}
which could play the role of a vertical unity is a
\delta
distribution supported on the diagonal. The simple examples considered here, namely
\mathcal{K}_{M}^{\infty}
and the TRAP Hom
{}^{c}_{V}
speak for the fact that non-unitary TRAPs is an appropriate framework to host infinite dimensional spaces. We hope non-unitary TRAPs to host more general distributions.

3
Free TRAPs

3.1
Various families of graphs

Here, we consider oriented multigraphs endowed with extra structures: indexed input and output edges, loops, ordering
\ldots

These extra structures make it difficult to implement the usual definition of multigraphs
[
Har69
]
, where the edges
form a multiset of pairs of vertices. In the literature on PROPs
[
Mar08
,
MMS09
]
,
graphs are defined as a set of half-edges (or flags), with an involution which tells us how to glue them together
in order to obtain edges, and with a partition which defines the vertices. This definition does not take loops in account
that is to say edges with no ends, yet loops enter our constructions in an essential way. Instead we borrow a definition from the theory of quivers
[
CB90
,
DW17
]
, where two maps (called source and target,
or alternatively tail and head) are given from the set of edges to the set of vertices.
In our approach, we allow edges without source, or without target, or with neither source nor target. Among these are the inputs and outputs
in the graph we consider, which we then index.

Definition 3.1.1
.

A
graph
is a family
G=(V(G),E(G),I(G),O(G),IO(G),L(G),s,t,\alpha,\beta)
, where:

1.

V(G)
(set of vertices),
E(G)
(set of internal edges),
I(G)
(set of input edges),

O(G)
(set of output edges),
IO(G)
(set of input-output edges) and
L(G)
(set of loops, that is to say
edges with no endpoints) are finite (possibly empty) sets.

2.

s:E(G)\sqcup O(G)\longrightarrow V(G)
is a map (source map).

3.

t:E(G)\sqcup I(G)\longrightarrow V(G)
is a map (target map).

4.

\alpha:I(G)\sqcup IO(G)\longrightarrow[i(G)]
is a bijection, with
i(G)=|I(G)|+|IO(G)|

(indexation of the input edges).

5.

\beta:O(G)\sqcup IO(G)\longrightarrow[o(G)]
is a bijection, with
o(G)=|O(G)|+|IO(G)|

(indexation of the output edges).

A
corolla ordered graph
is a graph
G
such that for any vertex
v
, the set of incoming edges
I(v)
of
v

and the set of outgoing edges
O(v)
of
v
are totally ordered and we shall denote both order relations by
\leqslant_{v}
.

A graph
G
is
solar
7
7

7

The terminology solar refers to its radiating aspect with rays around a central body. In
[
YJ15
]
such graphs are called ordinary.
if
IO(G)=L(G)=\emptyset
.

Example 3.1.1
.

Here is a graph
G
:

\displaystyle V(G)

\displaystyle=\{x,y\},

\displaystyle E(G)

\displaystyle=\{a,b\},

\displaystyle I(G)

\displaystyle=\{c,d\},

\displaystyle O(G)

\displaystyle=\{e,f\},

\displaystyle IO(G)

\displaystyle=\{g\},

\displaystyle L(G)

\displaystyle=\{h,k\},

and:

\displaystyle s

\displaystyle:\left\{\begin{array}[]{rcl}a&\longmapsto&y\\
b&\longmapsto&x\\
e&\longmapsto&y\\
f&\longmapsto&y,\end{array}\right.

\displaystyle t

\displaystyle:\left\{\begin{array}[]{rcl}a&\longmapsto&x\\
b&\longmapsto&y\\
c&\longmapsto&x\\
d&\longmapsto&x,\end{array}\right.

\displaystyle\alpha

\displaystyle:\left\{\begin{array}[]{rcl}c&\longmapsto&1\\
d&\longmapsto&2\\
g&\longmapsto&3,\end{array}\right.

\displaystyle\beta

\displaystyle:\left\{\begin{array}[]{rcl}e&\longmapsto&3\\
f&\longmapsto&1\\
g&\longmapsto&2.\end{array}\right.

This is graphically represented as follows:

\textstyle{1}
\textstyle{3}
\textstyle{2}
\textstyle{y}
\scriptstyle{e}
\scriptstyle{f}
\scriptstyle{a}
\scriptstyle{h}
\scriptstyle{k}
\textstyle{x}
\scriptstyle{b}
\textstyle{1\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{c}
\textstyle{2\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{d}
\textstyle{3\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{g}

Note that this graph contains two loops, represented by
\scriptstyle{h}

and
\scriptstyle{k}
.

Graphically, if
G
is a corolla ordered graph, we shall represent the orders on the incoming and outgoing edges of a vertex by drawing box-shaped vertices, with the incoming and outgoing edges of any vertex ordered from left to right. For example, we distinguish the following two situations:

We note that the graph of Example
3.1.1
can be made corolla ordered in
3!\times 3!=36
ways,
corresponding to the total orderings of the three incoming edges of
x
and of the three outgoing edges of
y
.
Here are three of them:

Definition 3.1.2
.

Let
G
and
G^{\prime}
be two graphs. A
(resp. iso-)morphism
of graphs from
G
to
G^{\prime}
is a family of (resp. bijections) maps
f=(f_{V},f_{E},f_{I},f_{O},f_{IO},f_{L})
with:

\displaystyle f_{V}

\displaystyle:V(G)\longrightarrow V(G^{\prime}),

\displaystyle f_{E}

\displaystyle:E(G)\longrightarrow E(G^{\prime}),

\displaystyle f_{I}

\displaystyle:I(G)\longrightarrow I(G^{\prime}),

\displaystyle f_{O}

\displaystyle:O(G)\longrightarrow O(G^{\prime}),

\displaystyle f_{IO}

\displaystyle:IO(G)\longrightarrow IO(G^{\prime}),

\displaystyle f_{L}

\displaystyle:L(G)\longrightarrow L(G^{\prime}),

such that:

\displaystyle s^{\prime}\circ f_{E}

\displaystyle=f_{V}\circ s_{\mid E(G)},

\displaystyle s^{\prime}\circ f_{O}

\displaystyle=f_{V}\circ s_{\mid O(G)},

\displaystyle t^{\prime}\circ f_{E}

\displaystyle=f_{V}\circ t_{\mid E(G)},

\displaystyle t^{\prime}\circ f_{I}

\displaystyle=f_{V}\circ t_{\mid I(G)},

\displaystyle\alpha^{\prime}\circ f_{I}

\displaystyle=\alpha_{\mid I(G)},

\displaystyle\alpha^{\prime}\circ f_{IO}

\displaystyle=\alpha_{\mid IO(G)},

\displaystyle\beta^{\prime}\circ f_{O}

\displaystyle=\beta_{\mid O(G)},

\displaystyle\beta^{\prime}\circ f_{IO}

\displaystyle=\beta_{\mid IO(G)}.

Let
G
and
G^{\prime}
be two corolla ordered graphs. A
(resp. iso-)morphism
of corolla ordered graphs from
G
to
G^{\prime}
is an
(resp. iso-)morphism
of graphs

f
from
G
to
G^{\prime}
which preserves the order of incoming and outgoing edges i.e., for any vertex of
v
:

•

For any incoming edges
e
,
e^{\prime}
of
v
,
e\leqslant_{v}e^{\prime}
in
G
if, and only if,
f(e)\leqslant_{f(v)}f(e^{\prime})
in
G^{\prime}
.

•

For any outgoing edges
e
,
e^{\prime}
of
v
,
e\leqslant_{v}e^{\prime}
in
G
if, and only if,
f(e)\leqslant_{f(v)}f(e^{\prime})
in
G^{\prime}
.

For any
k,l\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
, we denote by
\mathbf{Gr^{\circlearrowright}}(k,l)
the set of the isoclasses of graphs
G
such that

i(G)=k
and
o(G)=l
, i.e.
\mathbf{Gr^{\circlearrowright}}(k,l)
is the quotient space of graphs with
k
input edges and
l
output edges by the equivalence relation given by isomorphism. Similarly, we denote by
\mathbf{{CGr}^{\circlearrowright}}(k,l)
the set of isoclasses of corolla ordered
graphs
G
such that
i(G)=k
and
o(G)=l
.

The subset of
\mathbf{Gr^{\circlearrowright}}(k,l)
formed by isoclasses of solar graphs is denoted by
\mathbf{{sol}Gr^{\circlearrowright}}(k,l)

and the subset of
\mathbf{{CGr}^{\circlearrowright}}(k,l)
formed by isoclasses of solar corolla ordered graphs is denoted by
\mathbf{{solCGr}^{\circlearrowright}}(k,l)
.

In what follows, we shall write
graphs
for
isoclasses of graphs
.

Example 3.1.2
.

The isoclass of the graph of Example
3.1.1
is represented by:

\textstyle{1}
\textstyle{3}
\textstyle{2}
\textstyle{1\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{2\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{3\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

We will later define a monad structure on graphs and corolla ordered graphs (Proposition
5.2.4
).

Throughout the paper,
X=(X(k,l))_{k,l\geqslant 0}
is a family of sets.

Definition 3.1.3
.

A graph decorated by
X=(X(k,l))_{k,l\geqslant 0}
(or
X
-decorated graph, or simply decorated graph) is a couple
(G,d_{G})
with
G
a graph as in Definition
3.1.1
and
\displaystyle d_{G}:V(G)\longrightarrow\bigsqcup_{k,l\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}X(k,l)
a map, such that for any vertex
v\in V(G)
,
d_{G}(v)\in X{(i(v),o(v))}
. We denote by
\mathbf{Gr^{\circlearrowright}}(X)
the set of graphs decorated by
X
. Similarly, we define
X
-decorated corolla ordered graphs which we denote by
\mathbf{{CGr}^{\circlearrowright}}(X)
.

We further write
\mathbf{Gr^{\circlearrowright}}(X)(k,l)
(resp.
\mathbf{{CGr}^{\circlearrowright}}(X)(k,l)
,
\mathbf{{sol}Gr^{\circlearrowright}}(X)(k,l)
and
\mathbf{{solCGr}^{\circlearrowright}}(X)(k,l)
) the set of graphs (resp. of corolla ordered graphs, solar graphs, solar corolla ordered graphs)
decorated by
X
with
k
inputs (i.e.
|I(G)|=k
) and
l
ouputs (i.e.
|O(G)|=l
).

3.2
TRAPs of graphs

As before,
X=(X(k,l))_{k,l\geqslant 0}
is a family of sets. We equip the set of graphs (possibly decorated by
X
) with a structure of TRAP.
Let us first define an action of
\mathfrak{S}\times\mathfrak{S}^{op}
on graphs.
Let
G=(V(G),E(G),I(G),O(G),IO(G),L(G),s,t,\alpha,\beta)\in\mathbf{Gr^{\circlearrowright}}(k,l)
,
\sigma\in\mathfrak{S}_{k}
and
\tau\in\mathfrak{S}_{l}
.
Then:

\tau\cdot G\cdot\sigma=(V(G),E(G),I(G),O(G),IO(G),L(G),s,t,\sigma^{-1}\circ\alpha,\tau\circ\beta).

If
G
is corolla ordered, then
\tau\cdot G\cdot\sigma
is naturally corolla ordered; if
G
is
X
-decorated,
then
\tau\cdot G\cdot\sigma
is also
X
-decorated. Hence, this defines a structure of
\mathfrak{S}\times\mathfrak{S}^{op}
-module
on
\mathbf{Gr^{\circlearrowright}}
,
\mathbf{{CGr}^{\circlearrowright}}
,
\mathbf{Gr^{\circlearrowright}}(X)
and
\mathbf{{CGr}^{\circlearrowright}}(X)
for any
X
.

We now define the
horizontal concatenation
. If
G
and
G^{\prime}
are two graphs,
we define a graph
G*G^{\prime}
in the following way:

\displaystyle V(G*G^{\prime})

\displaystyle=V(G)\sqcup V(G^{\prime}),

\displaystyle E(G*G^{\prime})

\displaystyle=E(G)\sqcup E(G^{\prime}),

\displaystyle L(G*G^{\prime})

\displaystyle=L(G)\sqcup L(G^{\prime}),

\displaystyle I(G*G^{\prime})

\displaystyle=I(G)\sqcup I(G^{\prime}),

\displaystyle O(G*G^{\prime})

\displaystyle=O(G)\sqcup O(G^{\prime}),

\displaystyle IO(G*G^{\prime})

\displaystyle=IO(G)\sqcup IO(G^{\prime}).

The source and target maps are given by:

\displaystyle s^{\prime\prime}_{\mid E(G)\sqcup O(G)}

\displaystyle=s,

\displaystyle s^{\prime\prime}_{\mid E(G^{\prime})\sqcup O(G^{\prime})}

\displaystyle=s^{\prime},

\displaystyle t^{\prime\prime}_{\mid E(G)\sqcup I(G)}

\displaystyle=t,

\displaystyle t^{\prime\prime}_{\mid E(G^{\prime})\sqcup I(G^{\prime})}

\displaystyle=t^{\prime}.

The indexations of the input and output edges are given by:

\displaystyle\alpha^{\prime\prime}_{\mid I(G)\sqcup IO(G)}

\displaystyle=\alpha,

\displaystyle\alpha^{\prime\prime}_{\mid I(G^{\prime})\sqcup IO(G^{\prime})}

\displaystyle=i(G)+\alpha^{\prime},

\displaystyle\beta^{\prime\prime}_{\mid O(G)\sqcup IO(G)}

\displaystyle=\beta,

\displaystyle\beta^{\prime\prime}_{\mid O(G^{\prime})\sqcup IO(G^{\prime})}

\displaystyle=o(G)+\beta^{\prime}

with an obvious abuse of notation in the definition of the second column.
Notice that this product is not commutative in the usual sense for
G*G^{\prime}
and
G^{\prime}*G
might differ by the indexation of their input and output
edges. However, it is commutative in the sense of Axiom 2.(d) of TRAPs.
Roughly speaking,
G*G^{\prime}
is the disjoint union of
G
and
G^{\prime}
, the input and output edges of
G^{\prime}
being indexed
after the input and output edges of
G
.
G
1
k
\ldots
1
l
\ldots

\begin{subarray}{c}\displaystyle*\\
\vskip 85.35826pt\end{subarray}

G^{\prime}
1
k^{\prime}
\ldots
1
l^{\prime}
\ldots

\begin{subarray}{c}\displaystyle=\\
\vskip 85.35826pt\end{subarray}

G
1
k
\ldots
1
l
\ldots

G^{\prime}
k+1
k+k^{\prime}
\ldots
l+1
l+l^{\prime}
\ldots

Moreover, if
G
and
H
are corolla ordered graphs, then
G*H
is naturally a corolla ordered graph.
If
G
and
H
are
X
-decorated graphs, then
G*H
is also naturally an
X
-decorated graph.

Let us finally define the partial trace maps. We only define the outline of their definition, and refer the reader to Appendix
B
for a rigorous definition.
Let
G\in\mathbf{Gr^{\circlearrowright}}(k,l)
,
1\leqslant i\leqslant k
and
1\leqslant j\leqslant l
.
We set
e_{i}=\alpha_{G}^{-1}(i)
,
f_{j}=\beta_{G}^{-1}(j)
and define
t_{i,j}(G)

as the graph obtained by identifying the input of
e_{i}
with the output
j
of
f_{j}
.
If
e_{i}\in I(G)
and
f_{j}\in O(G)
, this creates
an edge in
E(G)
. This case is illustrated in the figure below. Otherwise, we create an edge in
I(G)
, or
O(G)
or
IO(G)
or in
L(G)
. In all these cases, we then reindex in non-decreasing order, the inputs and the outputs of the obtained graph.

Graphically:
G
1
i
k
\ldots
\ldots
1
j
l
\ldots
\ldots

\begin{subarray}{c}\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle t_{i,j}}}{{\longmapsto}}\\
\vskip 113.81102pt\end{subarray}

G
1
k-1
\ldots
\ldots
1
l-1
\ldots
\ldots

In particular,
t_{1,1}(I)
is the graph
\mathcal{O}
.
As before, if
G
is corolla ordered, or if it is
X
-decorated, then
t_{i,j}(G)
is corolla ordered, or
X
-decorated.

Example 3.2.2
.

Let
G
be the following graph:

\textstyle{2}
\textstyle{1}
\textstyle{1\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{2\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{3\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

Then:

\displaystyle\begin{subarray}{c}\vskip 56.9055pt\\
\displaystyle t_{1,2}(G)=\hskip 8.5359pt\end{subarray}

\textstyle{1}
\textstyle{1\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{2\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

\displaystyle\begin{subarray}{c}\vskip 56.9055pt\\
\displaystyle t_{1,1}(G)=t_{2,2}(G)=t_{3,2}(G)=\hskip 8.5359pt\end{subarray}

\textstyle{1}
\textstyle{1\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{2\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

\displaystyle\begin{subarray}{c}\vskip 56.9055pt\\
\displaystyle t_{2,1}(G)=t_{3,1}(G)=\hskip 8.5359pt\end{subarray}

\textstyle{1}
\textstyle{1\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{2\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

Note that
t_{1,2}
creates a loop when applied on
G
.

Proposition 3.2.1
.

With these data,
\mathbf{Gr^{\circlearrowright}}
,
\mathbf{{CGr}^{\circlearrowright}}
,
\mathbf{Gr^{\circlearrowright}}(X)
and
\mathbf{{CGr}^{\circlearrowright}}(X)
are unitary TRAPs.

Proof.

We carry out the proof for
{\mathbf{{CGr}^{\circlearrowright}}}
. The proof is similar for the three other cases.
Properties 1. and 2. are trivial.
Let us give a graphical interpretation of the proof of Property 3.(a), when
i^{\prime}<i
and
j^{\prime}<j
.

G
1
i^{\prime}
i
k
\ldots
\ldots
\ldots
1
j^{\prime}
j
l
\ldots
\ldots
\ldots

\begin{subarray}{c}\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle t_{i,j}}}{{\longmapsto}}\\
\vskip 113.81102pt\end{subarray}

G
1
i^{\prime}
k-1
\ldots
\ldots
\ldots
1
j^{\prime}
l-1
\ldots
\ldots
\ldots

\begin{subarray}{c}\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle t_{i^{\prime},j^{\prime}}}}{{\longmapsto}}\\
\vskip 113.81102pt\end{subarray}

G
1
k-2
\ldots
\ldots
\ldots
1
l-2
\ldots
\ldots
\ldots

G
1

i

\ldots
\ldots
\ldots
1
j^{\prime}
j
l
\ldots
\ldots
\ldots

\begin{subarray}{c}\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle t_{i^{\prime},j^{\prime}}}}{{\longmapsto}}\\
\vskip 113.81102pt\end{subarray}

G

\begin{subarray}{c}\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle t_{i-1,j-1}}}{{\longmapsto}}\\
\vskip 113.81102pt\end{subarray}

G

\ldots
\ldots
\ldots
1
l-2
\ldots
\ldots
\ldots

For Property 3.(b), let us consider a graph
p=G
.
As the input edge indexed by
i
in
\sigma\cdot G\cdot\tau
is the input edge of
G
indexed by
\tau(i)

and the output edge indexed by
j
in
\sigma\cdot G\cdot\tau
is the output edge of
G
indexed by
\sigma^{-1}(j)
,

G_{1}=t_{i,j}(\sigma\cdot G\cdot\tau)
is the graph obtained by gluing together
the input indexed by
\tau(j)
and the output indexed by
\sigma^{-1}(j)
, reindexing the input
according to
\sigma_{i}
and the output edges by
\tau_{j}
, so
G_{1}=\sigma_{i}\cdot t_{\tau(i),\sigma^{-1}(j)}(G)\cdot\tau_{j}
.

Let us prove Property 3.(c). By Lemma
1.1.4
, it is enough to prove it for

(p,p^{\prime})=(G,G^{\prime})
a pair of graphs and
(i,j)=(1,1)
. In this case,

e_{i}
and
f_{j}
are both edges of
G
, so
t_{1,1}(G*G^{\prime})=t_{1,1}(G)*G^{\prime}
.

Let us consider the graph
I
such that

V(I)=E(I)=O(I)=I(I)=L(I)=\emptyset,

with
IO(I)
reduced to a single element. Then for any graph
G
with
|O(G)|\geqslant 1
,

t_{1,2}(I*G)=G.

By Lemma
1.1.4
,
I
is a unit of
\mathbf{Gr^{\circlearrowright}}
.
∎

If
G
and
H
are solar, then
G*H
is clearly also solar. If
G\in{\mathbf{{CGr}^{\circlearrowright}}}(k,l)
is solar, then for any
i\in[k]
and
j\in[l]
,

t_{i,j}(G)
is solar. Indeed, as
IO(G)=\emptyset
,
IO(t_{i,j}(G))=\emptyset
; as
IO(G)=L(G)=\emptyset
,

L(t_{i,j}(G))=\emptyset
. Hence:

Corollary 3.2.2
.

\mathbf{{sol}Gr^{\circlearrowright}}
,
\mathbf{{solCGr}^{\circlearrowright}}
,
\mathbf{{sol}Gr^{\circlearrowright}}(X)
and
\mathbf{{solCGr}^{\circlearrowright}}(X)
are subTRAPs of

\mathbf{Gr^{\circlearrowright}}
,
\mathbf{{CGr}^{\circlearrowright}}
,
\mathbf{Gr^{\circlearrowright}}(X)
and
\mathbf{{CGr}^{\circlearrowright}}(X)
in the sense of Definition
1.1.2
. They are non unitary.

Remark 3.2.1
.

\mathbf{Gr^{\circlearrowright}}
,
\mathbf{{CGr}^{\circlearrowright}}
,
\mathbf{Gr^{\circlearrowright}}(X)
and
\mathbf{{CGr}^{\circlearrowright}}(X)
admit other sub-TRAPs, for example with vertices with only a prescribed number of possible vertices. These sub-TRAPs might be of importance in question of renormalisability of QFTs, but this question is far from the scope of this work and we therefore do not define rigorously these other objects.

3.3
Morphisms of TRAPs and free TRAPs

As before,
X=(X(k,l))_{k,l\geqslant 0}
is a family of sets. It turns out that
\mathbf{{solCGr}^{\circlearrowright}}(X)
is the free TRAP generated by
X
.
For any
x\in X(k,l)
, we identify
x
with the graph in
\mathbf{{solCGr}^{\circlearrowright}}(k,l)(X)
with one vertex decorated by
x
,

k
incoming edges, totally ordered according to their indices, and
l
outgoing edges, totally ordered according to their indices.
For example,
x\in X(3,2)
is identified with the corolla ordered graph

(13)

Theorem 3.3.1
.

Let
P
be a TRAP and
\phi=(\phi(k,l))_{k,l{\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}}
be a map from
X
to
P
i.e.,
for any
k,l\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
,
\phi(k,l):X(k,l)\longrightarrow P(k,l)
is a map.
Then there exists a unique TRAP morphism
\Phi:\mathbf{{solCGr}^{\circlearrowright}}(X)\longrightarrow P
, sending
x
to
\phi(x)
for any
x\in X
. If moreover
P
is unitary, this morphism
\Phi
is uniquely extended as a unitary TRAP morphism from
\mathbf{{CGr}^{\circlearrowright}}(X)
to
P
.

In other words,
\mathbf{{solCGr}^{\circlearrowright}}(X)
(resp.
\mathbf{{CGr}^{\circlearrowright}}(X)
) is the free TRAP (resp. the free unitary TRAP, i.e. the free wheeled PROP) generated by
X
.

Remark 3.3.1
.

In practice we often have
P=X
and
\phi=\mathrm{Id}
which yields a map

\Phi:\mathbf{{solCGr}^{\circlearrowright}}(X)\longrightarrow X

(14)

from decorated graphs to the space
X
of decorations.

Example 3.3.1
.

Here is a trivial yet enlightening example of how
\Phi
acts on graphs: for
G=\mathcal{O}
, we have
G=t_{1,1}(I)
and hence
\Phi(G)=t_{1,1}(I_{P})
.

Proof.

We provide here a sketch of the proof, and refer the reader to the appendix for a full proof. Since
\mathbf{{solCGr}^{\circlearrowright}}(X)\subseteq\mathbf{{CGr}^{\circlearrowright}}(X)
, we take
G\in\mathbf{{CGr}^{\circlearrowright}}(k,l)(X)
and treat simultaneously the case of solar graphs and the other. We define
\Phi(G)
for any graph

G\in\mathbf{{CGr}^{\circlearrowright}}(k,l)(X)
by induction on the number
N
of internal edges of
G
.

If
N=0
, then
G
can be written as

G=\mathcal{O}^{*p}*\sigma\cdot(I^{*q}*x_{1}*\ldots*x_{r})\cdot\tau,

(recall that
\mathcal{O}
is the graph with no vertex, with only one edge in
L(G)
) where
p,q,r\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
,
(k_{i},{l_{i}})\in\mathbb{N}_{0}^{2}
for any
i
,
x_{i}\in X{({k_{i},l_{i}})}
and
\sigma\in\mathfrak{S}_{q+k_{1}+\ldots+k_{r}}
,
\tau\in\mathfrak{S}_{q+l_{1}+\ldots+l_{r}}
.
If
G
is solar, then
p=q=0
and this reduces to

G=\sigma\cdot(x_{1}*\ldots*x_{r})\cdot\tau.

We then set

\Phi(G)=\sigma\cdot(\phi(x_{1})*\ldots*\phi(x_{k}))\cdot\tau.

(15)

If
G
is not solar and if
P
is unitary, we denote by
I_{P}
the identity of
P
, and we put

\Phi(G)={t_{1,1}(I_{P})^{*p}}*\sigma\cdot(I_{P}^{*q}*\phi(x_{1})*\ldots*\phi(x_{r}))\cdot\tau.

We can prove that this does not depend on the choice of the decomposition of
G
, with the help of the TRAP axioms applied to
P
.
Let us now assume that
\Phi(G^{\prime})
is defined for any graph with
N-1
internal edges, for a given
N\geqslant 1
. Let
G
be a graph with
N
internal edges and let
e
be one of these edges. Let
G_{e}
be a graph obtained by cutting this edge in two, such that
G=t_{1,1}(G_{e})
. We then set:

\Phi(G)=t_{1,1}\circ\Phi(G_{e}).

One can prove that this does not depend on the choice of
e
. It can then be shown that
\Phi
defined as above is compatible with the partial trace maps.
∎

Since the ingoing and outgoing edges of each vertex of a corolla ordered graph are totally ordered, each corolla ordered graph
\mathbf{{CGr}^{\circlearrowright}}
is naturally acted upon by
\mathfrak{S}\times\mathfrak{S}^{op}
.

Definition 3.3.2
.

For any corolla ordered graph
G\in\mathbf{{CGr}^{\circlearrowright}}
and any vertex
v\in V(G)
, there is a natural action of
\mathfrak{S}_{o(v)}\times\mathfrak{S}_{i(v)}^{op}
induced by the action on the totally ordered edges in
O(v)
and
I(v)
. The corolla ordered graph obtained from
G
by the action of
(\sigma,\tau)
on the vertex
v
is denoted by

\sigma\cdot_{v}G\cdot_{v}\tau.

A similar action can be built on a corolla ordered graph
G
decorated by a family of sets
X
:

\sigma\cdot_{v}(G,d_{G})\cdot_{v}\tau:=(\sigma\cdot_{v}G\cdot_{v}\tau,d_{G}).

Example 3.3.2
.
