In these pictures, the labelling of the edges outgoing (resp. ingoing to) from the vertex
v
(resp.
w
) are labelled from left to right.

Note that
\mathbf{Gr^{\circlearrowright}}(X)
is obtained from
\mathbf{{CGr}^{\circlearrowright}}(X)
by forgetting the total orders on the edges, which in fact is equivalent to the trivialisation of this action of symmetric groups on incoming and outgoing edges of any vertex. Hence:

Corollary 3.3.3
.

Let
P
be a TRAP and
\phi=(\phi(k,l))_{k,l\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}
be a map from
X
to
P
.
We assume that for any
x\in X(k,l)
, for any
(\sigma,\tau)\in\mathfrak{S}_{k}\otimes\mathfrak{S}_{l}
,

\tau\cdot\phi(x)\cdot\sigma=\phi(x).

There exists a unique TRAP morphism
\Phi:\mathbf{{sol}Gr^{\circlearrowright}}(X)\longrightarrow P
, sending
x
to
\phi(x)
for any
x\in X
.
If moreover
P
is unitary, this morphism
\Phi
is uniquely extended as a unitary TRAP morphism
from
\mathbf{Gr^{\circlearrowright}}(X)
to
P
.

We end this paragraph with the non corolla ordered counterpart of Remark
3.3.1
:

Remark 3.3.2
.

In practice we often have
P=X
and
\phi=\mathrm{Id}_{P}
which yields a map

\Phi:\mathbf{{solCGr}^{\circlearrowright}}(X)\longrightarrow X

(16)

from decorated corolla ordered graphs to the space
X
of decorations.

3.4
Extending non-unitary TRAPs

In this section, we embed any TRAP
P
in a unitary TRAP denoted by
\mathbf{uPGr^{\circlearrowright}}(P)
. We proceed in the following way:

•

We start with the canonical TRAP morphism from the free TRAP
\mathbf{{solCGr}^{\circlearrowright}}(P)
generated by
P
to
P
.

•

By Proposition
1.2.2
, this defines an equivalence
\sim
on
\mathbf{{solCGr}^{\circlearrowright}}(P)
,
compatible with the TRAP structure of
\mathbf{{solCGr}^{\circlearrowright}}(P)
.

•

We then extend this equivalence to
\mathbf{{CGr}^{\circlearrowright}}(P)
, in such a way that it is compatible with the unitary TRAP structure
of
\mathbf{{CGr}^{\circlearrowright}}(P)
, as required in Lemma
1.2.1
.

•

Consequently, the quotient
\mathbf{{CGr}^{\circlearrowright}}(P)/\sim
is a unitary TRAP which contains
P
.

For this, we shall need the solar part of any corolla ordered graph
G
, which we now define:

Notations 3.4.1
.

Let
G\in\mathbf{{CGr}^{\circlearrowright}}(P)(k,l)
. Then there exist a unique
(p,k^{\prime},l^{\prime})\in\mathbb{N}_{0}^{2}
,
k^{\prime}\leqslant k
,
l^{\prime}\leqslant l
, a unique solar graph
G^{\prime}\in\mathbf{{solCGr}^{\circlearrowright}}(P)(k^{\prime},l^{\prime})
,
a unique pair of permutations
(\sigma,\tau)\in\mathfrak{S}_{k}\times\mathfrak{S}_{l}
such that:

•

\sigma(1)<\ldots<\sigma(k^{\prime})
and
\sigma(k^{\prime}+1)<\ldots<\sigma(k)
(that is to say
\sigma
is a
(k^{\prime},k-k^{\prime})
-shuffle);

•

\tau(1)<\ldots<\tau(l^{\prime})
and
\tau(l^{\prime}+1)<\ldots<\tau(l)
(that is to say
\tau
is a
(l^{\prime},l-l^{\prime})
-shuffle);

•

G=\mathcal{O}^{p}*\tau^{-1}\cdot(G^{\prime}*I^{k-k^{\prime}})\cdot\sigma
.

The graph
G^{\prime}
is the
solar part
of
G
and is denoted by
\mathrm{sol}(G)
. We also set

\displaystyle\sigma

\displaystyle:=\mathrm{sh}_{{in}}(G),

\displaystyle\tau

\displaystyle:=\mathrm{sh}_{out}(G),

\displaystyle p

\displaystyle:=\mathrm{val}_{\mathcal{O}}(G).

Here
\mathrm{sh}
stands for shuffle and
\mathrm{val}
for valuation.

Remark 3.4.1
.

As the subsequent example will show, a reindexation of ingoing and outgoing edges
is useful to write the graph as a horizontal product of a solar graph, loops and
I
.

Example 3.4.1
.

Let
G
be the graph:

Moreover:

\displaystyle\mathrm{sh}_{in}(G)

\displaystyle=(231),

\displaystyle\mathrm{sh}_{out}(G)

\displaystyle=(132),

\displaystyle\mathrm{val}_{\mathcal{O}}(G)

\displaystyle=2.

Let
P
be a TRAP and let us consider the unique TRAP morphism
\Phi:{\mathbf{{solCGr}^{\circlearrowright}}}(P)\longrightarrow P
,
extending the identity of
P
. We define a relation
\sim
on
{\mathbf{{CGr}^{\circlearrowright}}}(P)
as follows: for
G,G^{\prime}\in{\mathbf{{CGr}^{\circlearrowright}}}(P)
,

G\sim G^{\prime}\Longleftrightarrow\begin{cases}\Phi(\mathrm{sol}(G))=\Phi(\mathrm{sol}(G^{\prime})),\\
\mathrm{sh}_{in}(G)=\mathrm{sh}_{in}(G^{\prime}),\\
\mathrm{sh}_{out}(G)=\mathrm{sh}_{out}(G^{\prime}),\\
\mathrm{val}_{\mathcal{O}}(G)=\mathrm{val}_{\mathcal{O}}(G^{\prime}).\end{cases}

This is clearly an equivalence.
Roughly speaking, this equivalence identifies graphs with the same input-output edges and loops and which coincide after contraction of their components obtained from deleting input-output edges and loops.

Theorem 3.4.1
.

The quotient
\mathbf{uPGr^{\circlearrowright}}(P):=\mathbf{{CGr}^{\circlearrowright}}(P)/\sim
is a unitary TRAP, containing a sub-TRAP isomorphic to
P
.

Proof.

•

We first show the compatibility of the equivalence relation with the left and right actions of the symmetric group.

Let
G,G^{\prime}\in{\mathbf{{CGr}^{\circlearrowright}}}(P)
, such that
G\sim G^{\prime}
.
If
\sigma\in\mathfrak{S}_{k+l}
, there exists a unique triple
(\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2},\sigma^{\prime})\in\mathfrak{S}_{k^{\prime}}\otimes\mathfrak{S}_{k-k^{\prime}}\times\mathfrak{S}_{k}

such that:

–

\mathrm{sh}_{in}(G)\circ\sigma=\sigma^{\prime}\circ(\sigma_{1}\otimes\sigma_{2})
.

–

\sigma^{\prime}(1)<\ldots<\sigma^{\prime}(k^{\prime})
and
\sigma^{\prime}(k^{\prime}+1)<\ldots<\sigma^{\prime}(k)
.

Then
\mathrm{sol}(G\cdot\sigma)=\mathrm{sol}(G)\cdot\sigma_{1}
,
\mathrm{sh}_{in}(G\cdot\sigma)=\sigma^{\prime}
and
\mathrm{sh}_{out}(G\cdot\sigma)=sh_{{out}}(G)
.
Obviously,
\mathrm{val}_{\mathcal{O}}(G\cdot\sigma)=\mathrm{val}_{\mathcal{O}}(G^{\prime})
. A similar result holds for
G^{\prime}
. We immediately obtain that:

\displaystyle\mathrm{sh}_{in}(G\cdot\sigma)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{sh}_{in}(G^{\prime}\cdot\sigma),

\displaystyle sh_{o}(G\cdot\sigma)

\displaystyle=sh_{o}(G^{\prime}\cdot\sigma),

\displaystyle\mathrm{val}_{\mathcal{O}}(G\cdot\sigma)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{val}_{\mathcal{O}}(G^{\prime}\cdot\sigma).

Moreover, as
\Phi
is a TRAP morphism:

\Phi(\mathrm{sol}(G\cdot\sigma))=\Phi(\mathrm{sol}(G)\cdot\sigma_{1})=\Phi(\mathrm{sol}(G))\cdot\sigma_{1}=\Phi(\mathrm{sol}(G^{\prime}))\cdot\sigma_{1}=\Phi(\mathrm{sol}(G^{\prime}\cdot\sigma)),

So
G\cdot\sigma\sim G^{\prime}\cdot\sigma
. Similarly, if
\tau\in\mathfrak{S}_{l}
,

\tau\cdot G\sim\tau\cdot G^{\prime}
.

•

Let us show the compatibility of the equivalence relation with the horizontal concatenation
*
on the left and on the right.

Let
H\in{\mathbf{{CGr}^{\circlearrowright}}}(P)
. Then:

\displaystyle\mathrm{sol}(G*H)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{sol}(G)*\mathrm{sol}(H),

\displaystyle\mathrm{sh}_{in}(G*H)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{sh}_{in}(G)*\mathrm{sh}_{in}(H),

\displaystyle\mathrm{sh}_{out}(G*H)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{sh}_{out}(G)*\mathrm{sh}_{out}(H),

\displaystyle\mathrm{val}_{\mathcal{O}}(G*H)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{val}_{\mathcal{O}}(G)+\mathrm{val}_{\mathcal{O}}(H).

A similar result holds for
G^{\prime}*H
. As
G\sim G^{\prime}
,

\displaystyle\mathrm{sh}_{in}(G*H)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{sh}_{in}(G^{\prime}*H),

\displaystyle sh_{{out}}(G*H)

\displaystyle=sh_{{out}}(G^{\prime}*H),

\displaystyle\mathrm{val}_{\mathcal{O}}(G*H)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{val}_{\mathcal{O}}(G^{\prime}*H).

Moreover, as
\Phi
is a TRAP morphism:

\displaystyle\Phi(\mathrm{sol}(G*H))

\displaystyle=\Phi(\mathrm{sol}(G)*\mathrm{sol}(H))

\displaystyle=\Phi(\mathrm{sol}(G))*\Phi(\mathrm{sol}(H))

\displaystyle=\Phi(\mathrm{sol}(G^{\prime}))*\Phi(\mathrm{sol}(H))=\Phi(\mathrm{sol}(G^{\prime}*H)).

Hence,
G*H\sim G^{\prime}*H
. Similarly,
H*G\sim H*G^{\prime}
.

•

We now check the compatibility of the equivalence relation with the partial trace maps.

Let
i\in[k]
and
j\in[l]
. We denote by
e_{i}
(respectively
e^{\prime}_{i}
) the
i
-th input of
G
(respectively of
G^{\prime}
)
and by
f_{j}
(respectively
f^{\prime}_{j}
) the
j
-th output of
G
(respectively of
G^{\prime}
). There are five possible cases:

1.

If
e_{i}=f_{j}\in IO(G)
, then
e^{\prime}_{i}=f^{\prime}_{j}\in IO(G)
. Moreover:

\displaystyle\mathrm{sh}_{in}(t_{i,j}(G))

\displaystyle=\mathrm{sh}_{in}(t_{i,j}(G^{\prime})),

\displaystyle sh_{o}(t_{i,j}(G))

\displaystyle=sh_{o}(t_{i,j}(G^{\prime})),

\displaystyle\mathrm{val}_{\mathcal{O}}(t_{i,j}(G))

\displaystyle=\mathrm{val}_{\mathcal{O}}(t_{i,j}(G^{\prime}))=\mathrm{val}_{\mathcal{O}}(G)+1,

\displaystyle\mathrm{sol}(t_{i,j}(G))

\displaystyle=\mathrm{sol}(G),

\displaystyle\mathrm{sol}(t_{i,j}(G^{\prime}))

\displaystyle=\mathrm{sol}(G^{\prime}).

As
G\sim G^{\prime}
,
\Phi(\mathrm{sol}(G))=\Phi(\mathrm{sol}(G^{\prime}))
, so
t_{i,j}(G)\sim t^{\prime}_{i,j}(G)
.

2.

If
e_{i},f_{j}\in IO(G)
, with
e_{i}\neq f_{j}
, then
e^{\prime}_{i},f^{\prime}_{j}\in IO(G^{\prime})
, with
e^{\prime}_{i}\neq f^{\prime}_{j}
. Moreover:

\displaystyle\mathrm{sh}_{in}(t_{i,j}(G))

\displaystyle=\mathrm{sh}_{in}(t_{i,j}(G^{\prime})),

\displaystyle\mathrm{sh}_{o}(t_{i,j}(G))

\displaystyle=\mathrm{sh}_{o}(t_{i,j}(G^{\prime})),

\displaystyle\mathrm{val}_{\mathcal{O}}(t_{i,j}(G))

\displaystyle=\mathrm{val}_{\mathcal{O}}(t_{i,j}(G^{\prime}))=\mathrm{val}_{\mathcal{O}}(G),

\displaystyle\mathrm{sol}(t_{i,j}(G))

\displaystyle=\mathrm{sol}(G),

\displaystyle\mathrm{sol}(t_{i,j}(G^{\prime}))

\displaystyle=\mathrm{sol}(G^{\prime}).

So
t_{i,j}(G)\sim t^{\prime}_{i,j}(G)
.

3.

If
e_{i}\in IO(G)
and
f_{j}\notin IO(G)
, then
e^{\prime}_{i}\in IO(G^{\prime})
and
f_{j}\notin IO(G^{\prime})
. Moreover:

\displaystyle\mathrm{sh}_{in}(t_{i,j}(G))

\displaystyle=\mathrm{sh}_{in}(t_{i,j}(G^{\prime})),

\displaystyle\mathrm{sh}_{o}(t_{i,j}(G))

\displaystyle=\mathrm{sh}_{o}(t_{i,j}(G^{\prime})),

\displaystyle\mathrm{val}_{\mathcal{O}}(t_{i,j}(G))

\displaystyle=\mathrm{val}_{\mathcal{O}}(t_{i,j}(G^{\prime}))=\mathrm{val}_{\mathcal{O}}(G),

and there exists a permutation
\alpha\in\mathfrak{S}_{k^{\prime}}
such that

\displaystyle\mathrm{sol}(t_{i,j}(G))

\displaystyle=\mathrm{sol}(G)\cdot\alpha,

\displaystyle\mathrm{sol}(t_{i,j}(G^{\prime}))

\displaystyle=\mathrm{sol}(G^{\prime})\cdot\alpha.

As
\Phi
is a TRAP morphism:

\Phi(\mathrm{sol}(t_{i,j}(G))=\Phi(\mathrm{sol}(G))\cdot\alpha=\Phi(\mathrm{sol}(G^{\prime}))\cdot\alpha=\Phi(\mathrm{sol}(G^{\prime})\cdot\alpha)=\Phi(\mathrm{sol}(t_{i,j}(G^{\prime})),

so
t_{i,j}(G)\sim t_{i,j}(G^{\prime})
.

4.

The case where
e_{i}\notin IO(G)
and
f_{j}\in IO(G)
is treated similarly.

5.

If
e_{i},f_{j}\notin IO(G)
, then
e_{i},f_{j}\notin IO(G^{\prime})
. Moreover:

\displaystyle\mathrm{sh}_{in}(t_{i,j}(G))

\displaystyle=\mathrm{sh}_{in}(t_{i,j}(G^{\prime})),

\displaystyle\mathrm{sh}_{{out}}(t_{i,j}(G))

\displaystyle=\mathrm{sh}_{{out}}(t_{i,j}(G^{\prime})),

\displaystyle\mathrm{val}_{\mathcal{O}}(t_{i,j}(G))

\displaystyle=\mathrm{val}_{\mathcal{O}}(t_{i,j}(G^{\prime}))=\mathrm{val}_{\mathcal{O}}(G),

and there exist
i^{\prime}\in[k^{\prime}]
,
j^{\prime}\in[l^{\prime}]
, such that

\displaystyle\mathrm{sol}(t_{i,j}(G))

\displaystyle=t_{i^{\prime},j^{\prime}}(\mathrm{sol}(G)),

\displaystyle\mathrm{sol}(t_{i,j}(G^{\prime}))

\displaystyle=t_{i^{\prime},j^{\prime}}(\mathrm{sol}(G^{\prime})).

As
\Phi
is a TRAP morphism:

\displaystyle\Phi(\mathrm{sol}(t_{i,j}(G)))

\displaystyle=\Phi\circ t_{i^{\prime},j^{\prime}}(\mathrm{sol}(G))

\displaystyle=t_{i^{\prime},j^{\prime}}\circ\Phi(\mathrm{sol}(G))

\displaystyle=t_{i^{\prime},j^{\prime}}\circ\Phi(\mathrm{sol}(G^{\prime}))=\Phi(\mathrm{sol}(t_{i,j}(G^{\prime}))),

so
t_{i,j}(G)\sim t_{i,j}(G^{\prime})
.

By Lemma
1.2.1
,
\mathbf{uPGr^{\circlearrowright}}(P)
is a unitary TRAP.

The canonical injection
\iota:P\longrightarrow\mathbf{{CGr}^{\circlearrowright}}(P)
induces a map
\iota^{\prime}:P\longrightarrow\mathbf{uPGr^{\circlearrowright}}(P)
.
If
p,q
lie in
P
, then in
\mathbf{{CGr}^{\circlearrowright}}(P)
,
\iota(p)*\iota(q)
and
\iota(p*q)
are solar graphs, and:

\Phi(\iota(p)*\iota(q))=\Phi\circ\iota(p)*\Phi\circ\iota(q)=p*q=\Phi\circ\iota(p*q),

so
\iota(p)*\iota(q)\sim\iota(p*q)
. Hence,
\iota^{\prime}(p)*\iota^{\prime}(q)=\iota(p*q)
.
If
p\in P(k,l)
,
i\in[k]
and
j\in[l]
, then
t_{i,j}\circ\iota(p)
and
\iota\circ t_{i,j}(p)
are solar graphs in
\mathbf{{CGr}^{\circlearrowright}}(P)
, and:

\Phi\circ t_{i,j}\circ\iota(p)=t_{i,j}\circ\Phi\circ\iota(p)=t_{i,j}(p)=\Phi\circ\iota\circ t_{i,j}(p),

so
t_{i,j}\circ\iota(p)\sim\iota\circ t_{i,j}(p)
, which implies that
t_{i,j}\circ\iota^{\prime}(p)=\iota^{\prime}\circ t_{i,j}(p)
:
the map
\iota^{\prime}
is a TRAP morphism.

Let
p,q\in P
, such that
\iota^{\prime}(p)=\iota^{\prime}(q)
. Then
\iota(p)\sim\iota(q)
. As
\iota(p)
and
\iota(q)
are solar graphs,

p=\Phi\circ\iota(p)=\Phi\circ\iota(q)=q,

so
\iota^{\prime}
is injective. We have proved that the unitary TRAP
\mathbf{uPGr^{\circlearrowright}}(P)
contains a (non unitary) sub-TRAP isomorphic to
P
.
∎

3.5
A functor from TRAPs to unitary TRAPs

We now identify the sub-TRAP
\iota^{\prime}(P)
of
\mathbf{uPGr^{\circlearrowright}}(P)
and
P
.
We obtain a description the
\mathfrak{S}_{l}\times\mathfrak{S}_{k}^{op}
-module
\mathbf{uPGr^{\circlearrowright}}(P)(k,l)
:

\mathbf{uPGr^{\circlearrowright}}(P)(k,l)=\left(\bigsqcup_{{i=0}}^{\min(k,l)}\mathrm{ind}_{(\mathfrak{S}_{i}\otimes\mathfrak{S}_{l-i})\times(\mathfrak{S}_{i}\otimes\mathfrak{S}_{k-i})^{op}}^{\mathfrak{S}_{l}\times\mathfrak{S}_{k}^{op}}\mathfrak{S}_{i}\times P(k-i,l-i)\right)\times\{\mathcal{O}^{i},i\in\mathbb{N}_{0}\},

where
\mathrm{ind}
is the induction of modules and
\mathfrak{S}_{i}
is equipped with its canonical
\mathfrak{S}_{i}\times\mathfrak{S}_{i}^{op}
-action.
The elements of
\mathfrak{S}_{i}
correspond to input-output edges and
\mathcal{O}
is the trace of the identity.
The partial trace maps can be computed with the help of the unitary TRAP axioms. For example, if
p\in P(k-1,l-1)
,
if
j>1
, then:

t_{1,j}(\mathrm{Id}_{[1]},p,\mathcal{O}^{i})=(\mathrm{Id}_{[0]},(1\ldots j-1)\cdot p,\mathcal{O}^{i}),

which is graphically represented by

\vskip 113.81102pt\end{subarray}

The unitary TRAP
\mathbf{uPGr^{\circlearrowright}}(P)
satisfies the following universal property:

Proposition 3.5.1
.

Let
P
be a TRAP,
Q
a unitary TRAP and
\theta:P\longrightarrow Q
be a TRAP morphism. There exists a unique unitary TRAP
morphism
\Theta:\mathbf{uPGr^{\circlearrowright}}(P)\longrightarrow Q
extending
\theta
.

Proof.

Uniqueness
. Let
\Theta
be such a morphism. For any
G\in\mathbf{{CGr}^{\circlearrowright}}(P)
, we denote by
[G]
its class in
\mathbf{uPGr^{\circlearrowright}}(P)
. Then:

G=\mathrm{sh}_{out}(G)\cdot(\mathrm{sol}(G)*I^{*k^{\prime\prime}})\cdot\mathrm{sh}_{in}(G),

so

\Theta([G])=\mathrm{sh}_{out}(G)\cdot(\theta(\mathrm{sol}(G))*I_{Q}^{*k^{\prime\prime}})\cdot\mathrm{sh}_{out}(G),

which entirely determines
\Theta
.

Existence
. Let
\overline{\Theta}:\mathbf{{CGr}^{\circlearrowright}}(P)\longrightarrow Q
be the unique unitary TRAP morphism such that

\Theta(p)=\theta(p)
for any
p\in P
. Let
G,G^{\prime}\in\mathbf{{CGr}^{\circlearrowright}}(P)
, such that
G\sim G^{\prime}
. Then:

\displaystyle G

\displaystyle=\mathrm{sh}_{out}(G)\cdot(\mathrm{sol}(G)*I^{*k^{\prime\prime}})\cdot\mathrm{sh}_{in}(G),

\displaystyle G^{\prime}

\displaystyle=\mathrm{sh}_{out}(G)\cdot(\mathrm{sol}(G^{\prime})*I^{*k^{\prime\prime}})\cdot\mathrm{sh}_{in}(G),

and
\Phi(\mathrm{sol}(G))=\Phi(\mathrm{sol}(G^{\prime}))
in
P
, so:

\displaystyle\overline{\Theta}(G)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{sh}_{out}(G)\cdot(\overline{\Theta}(\mathrm{sol}(G))*I_{Q}^{*k^{\prime\prime}})\cdot\mathrm{sh}_{in}(G)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{sh}_{out}(G)\cdot(\theta\circ\Phi(\mathrm{sol}(G))*I_{Q}^{*k^{\prime\prime}})\cdot\mathrm{sh}_{in}(G)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{sh}_{out}(G)\cdot(\theta\circ\Phi(\mathrm{sol}(G^{\prime}))*I_{Q}^{*k^{\prime\prime}})\cdot\mathrm{sh}_{in}(G)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{sh}_{out}(G)\cdot(\overline{\Theta}(\mathrm{sol}(G^{\prime}))*I_{Q}^{*k^{\prime\prime}})\cdot\mathrm{sh}_{in}(G)

\displaystyle=\overline{\Theta}(G^{\prime}).

Hence,
\overline{\Theta}
induces a unitary TRAP morphism
\Theta:\mathbf{uPGr^{\circlearrowright}}(P)\longrightarrow Q
,
extending
\theta
.
∎

In other words,
\mathbf{uPGr^{\circlearrowright}}
is a functor from the category of TRAPs to the category of unitary TRAPs,
left adjoint of the forgetful functor from the category of unitary TRAPs to the category of TRAPs. This functor is the functor
L
of
[
YJ15
, Theorem 12.1]
(with the difference that in
[
YJ15
]
, one works in the coloured setup). Notice that we have a more explicit and straightforward construction of this tensor than the one of
[
YJ15
]
.

4
Compositions, generalised trace and convolution

4.1
Vertical concatenation in a TRAP

In the same way as wheeled PROPs are PROPs and are equipped with a second associative product
[
YJ15
]
,
TRAPs can be equipped with a natural operation, called the
vertical concatenation
.
We start with the various TRAPs of graphs we introduced.

Let
G
and
G^{\prime}
be two graphs such that
o(G)=i(G^{\prime})
. We define a graph

G^{\prime\prime}=G^{\prime}\circ G

in the following way:

\displaystyle V(G^{\prime\prime})

\displaystyle=V(G)\sqcup V(G^{\prime}),

\displaystyle E(G^{\prime\prime})

\displaystyle=E(G)\sqcup E(G^{\prime})\sqcup\{(f,e)\in O(G)\times I(G^{\prime}):\beta(f)=\alpha^{\prime}(e)\},

\displaystyle I(G^{\prime\prime})

\displaystyle=I(G)\sqcup\{(f,e)\in IO(G)\times I(G^{\prime}):\beta(f)=\alpha^{\prime}(e)\},

\displaystyle O(G^{\prime\prime})

\displaystyle=O(G)\sqcup\{(f,e)\in O(G)\times IO(G^{\prime}):\beta(f)=\alpha^{\prime}(e)\},

\displaystyle IO(G^{\prime\prime})

\displaystyle=\{(f,e)\in IO(G)\times IO(G^{\prime}):\beta(f)=\alpha^{\prime}(e)\},

\displaystyle L(G^{\prime\prime})

\displaystyle=L(G)\sqcup L(G^{\prime}).

Its
source
and
target
maps are given by:

\displaystyle s^{\prime\prime}_{\mid E(G)}

\displaystyle=s_{\mid E(G)},

\displaystyle s^{\prime\prime}_{\mid E(G^{\prime})}

\displaystyle=s^{\prime}_{\mid E(G^{\prime})},

\displaystyle s^{\prime\prime}_{\mid O(G^{\prime})}

\displaystyle=s^{\prime}_{\mid O(G^{\prime})},

\displaystyle s^{\prime\prime}((f,e))

\displaystyle=s(f),

\displaystyle t^{\prime\prime}_{\mid E(G)}

\displaystyle=t_{\mid E(G)},

\displaystyle t^{\prime\prime}_{\mid E(G^{\prime})}

\displaystyle=s^{\prime}_{\mid E(G^{\prime})},

\displaystyle t^{\prime\prime}_{\mid I(G)}

\displaystyle=s_{\mid I(G)},

\displaystyle s^{\prime\prime}((f,e))

\displaystyle=t^{\prime}(e).

The indexations of its input and output edges are given by:

\displaystyle\alpha^{\prime\prime}_{\mid I(G)}

\displaystyle=\alpha_{\mid I(G)},

\displaystyle\alpha^{\prime\prime}((f,e))

\displaystyle=\alpha(f),

\displaystyle\beta^{\prime\prime}_{\mid O(G^{\prime})}

\displaystyle=\beta^{\prime}_{\mid O(G^{\prime})},

\displaystyle\beta^{\prime\prime}((f,e))

\displaystyle=\beta^{\prime}(e).

Roughly speaking,
G^{\prime}\circ G
is obtained by gluing together the outgoing edges of
G
and the incoming
edges of
G^{\prime}
according to their indexation.
G^{\prime}
1
l
\ldots
1
m
\ldots

\begin{subarray}{c}\displaystyle\circ\\
\vskip 85.35826pt\end{subarray}

G
1
k
\ldots
1
l
\ldots

\begin{subarray}{c}\displaystyle=\\
\vskip 85.35826pt\end{subarray}

G
1
k
\ldots
G^{\prime}
1
m
\ldots

Example 4.1.1
.

If
G
and
G^{\prime}
are corolla ordered (respectively
X
-decorated) graphs, then
G
and
G^{\prime}
are naturally corolla ordered (respectively
X
-decorated).
This operation
\circ
is clearly associative. Moreover, denoting by
I
the identity graph, for any
k,l\in\mathbb{N}
,
for any graph
G
with
k
inputs and
l
outputs,

I^{*l}\circ G=G\circ I^{*k}=G.

The vertical concatenation can be described in terms of the horizontal concatenation and of the partial trace maps:
If
G
is a graph with
k
inputs and
l
outputs, and
G^{\prime}
a graph with
l
inputs and
m
outputs, then:

\displaystyle t_{k+1,1}\circ\ldots\circ t_{k+l-1,l-1}\circ t_{k+l,l}(G*G^{\prime})

\displaystyle=G\circ G^{\prime},

or, graphically:
G
1
k
\ldots
\ldots
G^{\prime}
1
m
\ldots
\ldots

\begin{subarray}{c}\displaystyle=\\
\vskip 85.35826pt\end{subarray}

G
1
k
\ldots
\ldots
G^{\prime}
1
m
\ldots

This construction can be generalised from TRAPs of graphs to an arbitrary TRAP:

Proposition 4.1.1
.

Let
P
be a TRAP. We define a vertical concatenation
8
8

8

When there is a risk of confusion, we will write
\circ_{P}
for the vertical concatenation of a given TRAP
P
.

\circ
in the following way:

\displaystyle\forall(k,l,m)\in\mathbb{N}_{0}^{3},\>\forall p\in P(k,l),\>\forall q\in P(l,m),

\displaystyle q\circ p

\displaystyle=t_{k+1,1}\circ\ldots\circ t_{k+l-1,l-1}\circ t_{k+l,l}(p*q).

This operation is associative: for any
(k,l,m,n)\in\mathbb{N}^{4}
, for any
(p,q,r)\in P(k,l)\times P(l,m)\times P(l,n)
,

r\circ(q\circ p)=(r\circ q)\circ p.

(17)

If the TRAP is unitary, then for any
(k,l)\in\mathbb{N}_{0}^{2}
, for any
p\in P(k,l)
, if
I_{P}
is the unit of
P
, then

I_{P}^{*l}\circ p=p\circ I_{P}^{*k}=p.

Proof.

Recall that in Subsection
3.3
we identified any element
p
of the decorating set and the solar graph with one vertex decorated
p
(see Equation (
13
).
Let
\alpha:\mathbf{{solCGr}^{\circlearrowright}}(P)\longrightarrow P
be the unique TRAP morphism such that
\alpha(p)=p
for any
p\in P
.
This a surjective TRAP morphism. As
\alpha
respects the horizontal concatenation and the partial trace maps,
for any graphs
G,G^{\prime}\in\mathbf{{solCGr}^{\circlearrowright}}(P)
,

\alpha(G)\circ\alpha(G^{\prime})=\alpha(G\circ G^{\prime}).

Since the vertical concatenation is clearly associative in
\mathbf{{solCGr}^{\circlearrowright}}(P)
, the vertical concatenation is associative in
P
.
If
P
is unitary, this morphism is extended as a unitary TRAP morphism from
\mathbf{{CGr}^{\circlearrowright}}(P)
to
P
, which we also denote by
\alpha
.
For any
p\in P(k,l)
, in
\mathbf{{CGr}^{\circlearrowright}}(P)
:

I^{*l}\circ p=p\circ I^{*k}=p.

As
\alpha(I)=I_{P}
, in
P
:

\alpha(I^{*l}\circ p)=I_{P}^{*l}\circ p=p=\alpha(p\circ I^{*k})=p\circ I_{P}^{*k}.\qed

Remark 4.1.1
.

One could also define
partial vertical concatenations
, where only a
subset of the outputs are glued to the inputs with the partial trace maps, in the spirit of
[
YJ15
, Paragraph 3.3.3]
. We do not pursue this course here since such partial vertical concatenations will play no role in the rest of the paper.

Example 4.1.2
.

Let
V
be a vector space and let
f=\theta(v_{1}\ldots v_{l}\otimes f_{1}\ldots f_{k})\in\mathbf{Hom}_{V}^{fr}(k,l)
,

g=\theta(w_{1}\ldots w_{m}\otimes g_{1}\ldots g_{l})\in\mathbf{Hom}_{V}^{fr}(l,m)
. Then, denoting by
\bullet
the vertical
concatenation of
\mathbf{Hom}_{V}^{fr}
:

\displaystyle g\bullet f

\displaystyle=g_{1}(v_{1})\ldots g_{l}(v_{l})\theta(w_{1}\ldots w_{m}\otimes f_{1}\ldots f_{k})

\displaystyle=\theta(w_{1}\ldots w_{m}\otimes g_{1}\ldots g_{l})\circ\theta(v_{1}\ldots v_{l}\otimes f_{1}\ldots f_{k})

\displaystyle=f\circ g.

Hence, the vertical concatenation induced by the TRAP structure is the usual composition of linear maps.
If
V
is not finite-dimensional, this composition does not have a unit, as
\mathrm{Id}_{V}
is not of finite rank.

We end this subsection with a simple yet important Proposition.

Proposition 4.1.2
.

For any two TRAPs
P=(P(k,l))_{k,l\in\mathbb{N}^{2}}
and
Q
, any TRAP morphism
\phi=(\phi(k,l))_{k,l\in\mathbb{N}^{2}}:P\longrightarrow Q
yields a morphism for the vertical concatenations of
P
and
Q
.

Proof.

We need to show that for any TRAPs
P
and
Q
and any TRAP morphism
\phi:P\longrightarrow Q
as in the statement of the proposition, for any
(k,l,m)\in\mathbb{N}^{3}
,
p_{1}\in P(k,l)
and
p_{2}\in P(l,m)
we have

\phi(k,m)(p_{2}\circ p_{1})=\phi(k,l)(p_{1})\circ\phi(l,m)(p_{2}).

Using the definition of the vertical concatenation in the TRAP
P
and the third property of the Definition
1.1.3
of morphisms of TRAPs we have

\phi(k,m)(p_{2}\circ p_{1})=t_{k+1,1}^{Q}\circ\cdots\circ t_{k+l,l}^{Q}[\phi(k+l,m+l)(p_{1}*p_{2})]

with
t_{i,j}^{Q}
the partial trace maps of the TRAP
Q
. Then using the second property of Definition
1.1.3

we obtain:

\phi(k,m)(p_{2}\circ p_{1})=t_{k+1,1}^{Q}\circ\cdots\circ t_{k+l,l}^{Q}[\phi(k,l)(p_{1})*\phi(l,m)(p_{2})]=\phi(k,l)(p_{1})\circ\phi(l,m)(p_{2}).\qed

4.2
The generalised trace on a TRAP

If
G
is a graph with the same number of inputs and outputs, we define its generalised trace by, roughly speaking, grafting
any of its input to the output with the same index:
G
1
k
\ldots
1
k
\ldots

\begin{subarray}{c}\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle Tr}}{{\longrightarrow}}\\
\vskip 85.35826pt\end{subarray}

G
\ldots
\ldots

In particular, in
\mathbf{{CGr}^{\circlearrowright}}(X)
,
\mathcal{O}=\mathrm{Tr}(I)
. Note that if
G
is an solar graph, then
\mathrm{Tr}(G)
is solar:

\mathrm{Tr}({\mathbf{{solCGr}^{\circlearrowright}}}(k,k))\subseteq{\mathbf{{solCGr}^{\circlearrowright}}}(0,0)
for any
k
.
If
G
is a corolla ordered graph (respectively an
X
-decorated graph), then
\mathrm{Tr}(G)
is corolla ordered (respectively
X
-decorated).
This operation can be described in terms of the partial trace maps: if
G\in{\mathbf{{solCGr}^{\circlearrowright}}}(k,k)
, then

\mathrm{Tr}(G)=t_{1,1}\circ\ldots\circ t_{k,k}(G)=t_{1,1}\circ\ldots\circ t_{1,1}(G).

Similarly, we can define this generalised trace on any TRAP:

Proposition 4.2.1
.

Let
P
be a TRAP. For any
p\in P(k,k)
,
k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
, the generalised trace on
P
is defined as:

\mathrm{Tr}_{P}(p){:=}t_{1,1}\circ\ldots\circ t_{k,k}(p)\in P(0,0).

1.

For any
(k,l)\in\mathbb{N}_{0}^{2}
, for any
(p,q)\in P(k,l)\times P(l,k)
,

\displaystyle\mathrm{Tr}_{P}(p\circ q)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{Tr}_{P}(q\circ p),

which justifies the terminology "trace".

2.

For any
(k,l)\in\mathbb{N}_{0}^{2}
, for any
(p,q)\in P(k,k)\times P(l,l)
,

\displaystyle\mathrm{Tr}_{P}(p*q)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{Tr}_{P}(p)*\mathrm{Tr}_{P}(q).

Proof.

Let
\alpha:{\mathbf{{solCGr}^{\circlearrowright}}}(P)\longrightarrow P
be, as before, the unique TRAP morphism which extends the identity map on
P
.
Since
\alpha
respects the partial trace maps, for any graph
G\in{\mathbf{{solCGr}^{\circlearrowright}}}(P)(k,k)
,

\alpha\circ\mathrm{Tr}(G)=\mathrm{Tr}_{P}\circ\alpha(G).

Let
p,q\in P(k,k)
. In
\mathbf{{sol}Gr^{\circlearrowright}}(P)
,
\mathrm{Tr}(q\circ p)
and
\mathrm{Tr}(p\circ q)
are represented by the graphs

p
\ldots
\ldots
q
\ldots

q
\ldots
\ldots
p
\ldots

which are the same. Applying
\alpha
, we obtain
\mathrm{Tr}_{P}(p\circ q)=\mathrm{Tr}(q\circ p)
.
Moreover, the graph
\mathrm{Tr}(p*q)
is represented by

p
\ldots
\ldots
q
\ldots
\ldots

which is also a graphical representation of
\mathrm{Tr}(p)*\mathrm{Tr}(q)
. Applying
\alpha
,
we obtain
\mathrm{Tr}_{P}(p*q)=\mathrm{Tr}_{P}(p)*Tr_{P}(q)
.
∎

Example 4.2.1
.

Let
V
be a finite dimensional vector space and
f=\Theta(v_{1}\ldots v_{k}\otimes f_{1}\ldots f_{k})\in\mathbf{Hom}_{V}^{fr}(k,k)
.
Identifying
\mathbf{Hom}_{V}(0,0)
with
\mathbb{R}
, we obtain that

\mathrm{Tr}_{\mathbf{Hom}_{V}}(f)=f_{1}(v_{1})\ldots f_{k}(v_{k}),

which is the usual trace of linear endomorphisms of a finite-dimensional vector space.
If
V
is not finite-dimensional,
\mathrm{Tr}_{\mathbf{Hom}_{V}^{fr}}
is a direct generalisation of this trace for linear endomorphisms
of finite rank.

4.3
Amplitudes and generalised convolutions

By Theorem
3.3.1
applied to
\phi=\mathrm{Id}_{P}
,
we know that for any TRAP
P
there exists a canonical TRAP map
\Phi_{P}:\mathbf{{solCGr}^{\circlearrowright}}(P)\longrightarrow P
(see Remark
3.3.1
).

Definition 4.3.1
.

Let
G
be a graph decorated by a TRAP
P
.
The

P
-amplitude
associated to
G
is the image
\Phi_{P}(G)
of
G
under
\Phi_{P}
.

When
P=\mathcal{K}_{M}^{\infty}
is the TRAP of smooth generalised kernel over a smooth finite dimensional closed Riemannian manifold
M
of §
2.3
(i.e.
P(k,l):=\mathcal{K}_{M}^{\infty}(k,l)
with the r.h.s defined in (
11
)),
we simply write
\Phi
for
\Phi_{P}
and call
\Phi(G)
the
smooth amplitudes

associated to
G\in\mathbf{{solCGr}^{\circlearrowright}}(\mathcal{K}_{M}^{\infty})
.

Remark 4.3.1
.

The terminology
P
-amplitude is justified in so far as it associates to a graph an expression in
P
depending on the ingoing and outgoing edges of the graph in a similar way as an amplitude associated to a Feynman depend on the external ingoing and outgoing momenta.

Remark 4.3.2
.

If we specialise to spaces
{\mathcal{E}}({M})^{\widehat{\odot}k}\,\hat{\otimes}\,{\mathcal{E}}({M})^{\widehat{\odot}l}
which are symmetric in both sets of input and output variables, then
\phi
can be extended to
\mathbf{{sol}Gr^{\circlearrowright}}(X)

(see Corollary
3.3.3
).

The case of a ladder graph relates amplitudes to convolutions:

Remark 4.3.3
.

Let
G
be a ladder graph decorated by
X=(\mathcal{K}_{X}^{\infty}(k,l))_{kl,\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}
i.e., a graph such that
I(G)=O(G)=[1]
,

IO(G)=L(G)=\emptyset
,
V(G)=\{v_{1},\cdots,v_{n}\}
,
E(G)=\{e_{1},\cdots,e_{n-1}\}
and the source and target maps defined by

\displaystyle s_{G}(1)

\displaystyle=v_{n},

\displaystyle t_{G}(1)

\displaystyle=v_{1},

\displaystyle\forall i\in[n-1],

\displaystyle s_{G}(e_{i})

\displaystyle=v_{i},

\displaystyle t_{G}(e_{i})

\displaystyle=v_{i+1}.

Here is a graphical representation of this graph:

\textstyle{1\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{v_{1}}
\textstyle{\ldots\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{v_{n}}
\textstyle{1}

Let
P_{i},i=1,\cdots,n
be smoothing pseudo-differential operator each of which is defined by the kernel
K_{i}
that decorates the vertex
v_{i}
, namely

K_{i}:=\mathrm{dec}(v_{i})
for any

v_{i},i\in[n]
. Then the generalised convolution associated to the graph
G
is the convolution
K_{1}\star\cdots\star K_{n}
of the kernels
{K_{1}},\cdots,K_{n}
, which is the
kernel of the smoothing pseudo-differential
operator
P_{1}\circ\cdots\circ P_{n}
. In this sense,
P
-amplitudes can be seen as a generalisation of the convolution of multiple smooth kernels.

Proposition 4.3.2
.

For any TRAP
P
, the
P
-amplitude associated to a horizontal concatenation of graphs is the horizontal concatenation of their
P
-amplitudes: for any
G_{1},G_{2}\in\mathbf{{solCGr}^{\circlearrowright}}(P)
,

\Phi_{P}(G_{1}*G_{2})=\Phi_{P}(G_{1})*\Phi_{P}(G_{2}),

and the same holds for the vertical concatenation: if
G_{1}\circ G_{2}
exists, then

\Phi_{P}(G_{1}\circ G_{2})=\Phi_{P}(G_{1})\circ_{P}\Phi_{P}(G_{2})

with
\circ_{P}
the vertical concatenation of
P
.

Proof.

First, notice that since
G_{2}\circ G_{2}
exists and since
\Phi_{P}
is a TRAP morphism,
\Phi_{P}(G_{1})\circ\Phi_{P}(G_{2})
exists.
The results then follows directly from the fact that
\Phi_{P}
is a TRAP morphism and from Proposition
4.1.2
.
∎

For any TRAP
P
, let
\iota_{P}:P\longrightarrow\mathbf{{solCGr}^{\circlearrowright}}(P)
be the canonical embedding of
P
into the TRAP of
P
-decorated graphs i.e.,
\iota_{P}(p)
is the solar graph with only one vertex decorated by
p
. We have the following simple yet useful Lemma.

Lemma 4.3.3
.

For any TRAP
P
the following diagram commutes:

\textstyle{P\times P\penalty\ \ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\circ_{P}}
\textstyle{\mathbf{{solCGr}^{\circlearrowright}}(P)\times\mathbf{{solCGr}^{\circlearrowright}}(P)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\circ}
\textstyle{P}
\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\mathbf{{solCGr}^{\circlearrowright}}(P)}
\scriptstyle{\Phi_{P}}

with
\circ_{p}
the vertical concatenation of the TRAP
P
and the obvious abuse of notation that vertical concatenations, if seen as maps, are not defined on the whole of
P\times P
.

In words, the horizontal concatenation of two elements
p_{1}

p_{2}
of
P
is the
P
-amplitude associated with the graph given by the vertical concatenation of two graphs with exactly one vertex, each decorated by one
p_{i}
.
Graphically, if
p\in P(k,l)
and
q\in P(l,m)
:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-3.15735pt}{109.68675pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}

{}{{}}{}{{}}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -27.56 118.11}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{

{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-17.38373pt}{75.82516pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\end{subarray}\hskip 8.53581pt\right)=\Phi_{P}(p)\circ_{P}\Phi_{P}(q).

Proof.

Let
P
be a TRAP. Then for any
p_{1}
,
p_{2}
in
P
such that
p_{1}\circ_{P}p_{2}
is well defined,
\iota_{P}(p_{1})\circ\iota_{P}(p_{2})
is well-defined since
\iota_{P}
respects the gradings and we have

\displaystyle\Phi_{P}(\iota_{P}(p_{1})\circ\iota_{P}(p_{2}))

\displaystyle=\Phi_{P}(\iota_{P}(p_{1}))\circ_{P}\Phi(\iota_{P}(p_{2}))\quad\text{by Proposition \ref{prop:gen_conv_vert_conc}}

\displaystyle=p_{1}\circ_{P}p_{2}

since for any TRAP
P
,
\Phi_{P}\circ\iota_{P}=\mathrm{Id}_{P}
by definition of
\Phi_{P}
(Equation (
15
) with
k=1
and
\phi=\mathrm{Id}_{P}
).
∎

Remark 4.3.4
.

Note that the vertical concatenation is
not
the same as the
P
-amplitude:
the latter has a much larger domain.

Applying the above constructions to the TRAP of smooth kernels described in Theorem
2.3.1
whose partial traces
12
are given by integrations on the underlying manifold, easily yields the following statement (we use the notations of
Paragraph
2.3
:
M
is a smooth, finite dimensional orientable closed manifold and
\mu(z)
is a volume form on
M
).

Theorem 4.3.4
.

The family
\left(X_{k,l}:=\mathcal{K}_{M}^{\infty}(k,l)\right)_{k,l\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}
is a TRAP and:

1.

The vertical concatenation of two kernels corresponds to their
generalised convolution
:

\displaystyle\forall(k,l,m)\in\mathbb{N}_{0}^{3},\>\forall K_{1}\in{\mathcal{K}}_{M}^{\infty}(k,l),\>\forall K_{2}\in{\mathcal{K}}_{M}^{\infty}(l,m),\forall(x_{1},\cdots,x_{k},z_{1},\cdots,z_{m})\in M^{k+m},

\displaystyle K_{2}\circ K_{1}(x_{1},\cdots,x_{k},z_{1},\cdots,z_{m})

\displaystyle=t_{k+1,1}\circ\ldots\circ t_{k+l-1,l-1}\circ t_{k+l,l}(K_{1}\otimes K_{2})(x_{1},\cdots,x_{k},z_{1},\cdots,z_{m})

\displaystyle=\int_{M^{l}}K_{1}(x_{1},\cdots,x_{k},y_{1},\cdots,y_{l})\,K_{2}(y_{1},\cdots,y_{l},z_{1},\cdots,z_{m})\,{d\mu(y_{1})\,\cdots d\mu(y_{l})},

obtained by integrating along the diagonal
\Delta_{M}^{l}:=\{(y_{1},\cdots,y_{l},y_{1},\cdots,y_{l}),y_{i}\in M\}\subset M^{2l}
.

2.

The associativity property
K_{3}\circ(K_{2}\circ K_{1})=(K_{3}\circ K_{2})\circ K_{1}
(cfr. (
17
)) for any
K_{3}\in{\mathcal{K}}_{M}^{\infty}(m,n)
,
amounts to the Fubini property for the corresponding multiple integrals:

\int_{M^{m}}\left(\int_{M^{l}}K_{1}(\vec{x},\vec{y}_{1})K_{2}(\vec{y}_{1},\vec{y}_{2})d\vec{\mu}(\vec{y}_{1})\right)K_{3}(\vec{y}_{2},\vec{z})d\vec{\mu}(\vec{y}_{2})=\int_{M^{l}}K_{1}(\vec{x},\vec{y}_{1})\left(\int_{M^{m}}K_{2}(\vec{y}_{1},\vec{y}_{2})K_{3}(\vec{y}_{2},\vec{z})d\vec{\mu}(\vec{y}_{2})\right)d\vec{\mu}(\vec{y}_{1})

for any
\vec{x}\in M^{k}
and
\vec{z}\in M^{n}
, where we use the short-hand notations
d\vec{\mu}(\vec{y}_{i}):=d\mu(y_{1})\cdots d\mu(y_{l_{i}})
.

3.

The generalised trace of a generalised kernel
K\in\mathcal{K}_{M}^{\infty}(k,k)
is given by the integral along the small diagonal of
M^{k}
:

\mathrm{Tr}_{{K}^{\infty}}(K)=\int_{M^{k}}K(x_{1},\cdots,x_{k},x_{1},\cdots,x_{k})\,dx_{1}\cdots dx_{k}

and obeys the following cyclicity property:

\mathrm{Tr}_{{K}^{\infty}}(K_{2}\circ K_{1})=\mathrm{Tr}_{{K}^{\infty}}(K_{1}\circ K_{2})

for
K_{1}\in\mathcal{K}_{M}^{\infty}(k,l)
and
K_{2}\in\mathcal{K}_{M}^{\infty}(l,k)
.

4.

The
\mathcal{K}_{M}^{\infty}
-amplitude is compatible with the horizontal and vertical concatenations
in
\mathcal{K}_{M}^{\infty}
.

5
Categorical interpretation

We describe the categories of (resp. unitary) TRAPs as (resp.
{\bf sol}\Gamma^{\circlearrowright}
-)
\Gamma^{\circlearrowright}
-algebras, where
\Gamma^{\circlearrowright}
is an endofunctor on the category of
\mathfrak{S}\times\mathfrak{S}^{op}-
modules, which sends a (resp. unitary) TRAP
X
to the free (resp. unitary) TRAP of graphs (resp.
{\bf sol}\Gamma^{\circlearrowright}(X)
)
\Gamma^{\circlearrowright}(X)
generated by
X
, thus extending known results of
[
YJ15
]
on the categorial aspects of wheeled PROPs.

5.1
Two endofunctors in the category of
\mathfrak{S}\times\mathfrak{S}^{op}
modules

We consider graphs decorated by a
\mathfrak{S}\times\mathfrak{S}^{op}
-module
X=(X(k,l))_{k,l\geqslant 0}

and use the action of the symmetric groups on the vertices of Definition
3.3.2
to define
an endofunctor
\Gamma^{\circlearrowright}
.

Definition 5.1.1
.

We define a relation on
\mathbf{{CGr}^{\circlearrowright}}(X)(k,l)
by
(G,d_{G})\mathcal{R}_{k,l}(G^{\prime},d_{G^{\prime}})
for
(G,d_{G})
and
(G^{\prime},d_{G^{\prime}})
if there exists a vertex
v
of
G
and permutations

\sigma\in\mathfrak{S}_{o(v)}
,
\tau\in\mathfrak{S}_{i(v)}
such that

\sigma\cdot_{v}(G,d_{G})\cdot_{v}\tau{=}(G,d_{G}^{\sigma,v,\tau})

with

\displaystyle d_{G}^{\sigma,v,\tau}(v^{\prime})=\begin{cases}&d_{G}(v^{\prime})\quad\text{for }v^{\prime}\neq v\\
&\sigma\cdot d_{G}(v)\cdot\tau\mbox{ otherwise}.\end{cases}

We denote by
\sim_{k,l}
the transitive closure of
\mathcal{R}_{k,l}
which defines an equivalence. We further define

\displaystyle\Gamma^{\circlearrowright}(X)(k,l)

\displaystyle:=\dfrac{\mathbf{{CGr}^{\circlearrowright}}(X)(k,l)}{\sim_{k,l}},

\displaystyle{\bf sol}\Gamma^{\circlearrowright}(X)(k,l)

\displaystyle:=\dfrac{\mathbf{{solCGr}^{\circlearrowright}}(X)(k,l)}{\sim_{k,l}}.

Here is the type of relations we obtain graphically:

where
x\in X_{3,2}
and
y\in X_{2,2}
.

It is easy to show that the family of equivalences
\sim=(\sim_{k,l})_{(k,l)\in\mathbb{N}_{0}^{2}}

is compatible with the action of
\mathfrak{S}\times\mathfrak{S}^{op}
,
the partial trace maps and the horizontal concatenation in the sense of Lemma
1.2.1
. The subsequent useful statement follows from Lemma
1.2.1
.

Lemma 5.1.2
.

Let
X
be a
\mathfrak{S}\times\mathfrak{S}^{op}
-module.
\Gamma^{\circlearrowright}{(X)}
is a unitary TRAP
and
{\bf sol}\Gamma^{\circlearrowright}{(X)}
is a TRAP.

Moreover, we know from Theorem
3.3.1
that
\mathbf{{solCGr}^{\circlearrowright}}(X)
is the free TRAP generated by the set
X
.
Now, by its very definition
{\bf sol}\Gamma^{\circlearrowright}(X)
is the free TRAP
generated by the
\mathfrak{S}\times\mathfrak{S}^{op}
-module
X
:

Proposition 5.1.3
.

Let
X
be
\mathfrak{S}\times\mathfrak{S}^{op}
module,
P
be a TRAP and
\phi:X\longrightarrow P

be a morphism of
\mathfrak{S}\times\mathfrak{S}^{op}
-modules. There exists a unique extension
\phi
to a TRAP morphism
\Phi:{\bf sol}\Gamma^{\circlearrowright}(X)\longrightarrow P
. If moreover
P
is unitary, this morphism extends to
\Gamma^{\circlearrowright}(X)
.

Example 5.1.1
.

If
X
is a trivial
\mathfrak{S}\times\mathfrak{S}^{op}
-module, then
\Gamma^{\circlearrowright}(X)=\mathbf{Gr^{\circlearrowright}}(X)
and
{\bf sol}\Gamma^{\circlearrowright}(X)=\mathbf{{sol}Gr^{\circlearrowright}}(X)
as TRAPs.
More generally, choosing for any graph
G
a corolla orientation
\overline{G}
, we can prove that for any
(k,l)\in\mathbb{N}_{0}^{2}
,
the sets
\Gamma^{\circlearrowright}(X)(k,l)
and
\mathbf{Gr^{\circlearrowright}}(X)(k,l)
are in bijection, as well as
{\bf sol}\Gamma^{\circlearrowright}(X)(k,l)
and
\mathbf{{sol}Gr^{\circlearrowright}}(X)(k,l)

(but not in a canonical way), through the map sending the equivalence class of
\overline{G}
to
G
.

The correspondence
P\to\Gamma^{\circlearrowright}(P)
defined above induces an endofunctor in the category
\mathbf{Mod}_{\mathfrak{S}}
of
\mathfrak{S}\times\mathfrak{S}^{op}
-modules which we now introduce.

Definition 5.1.4
.

Let
\mathbf{Mod}_{\mathfrak{S}}
denote the category of
\mathfrak{S}\times\mathfrak{S}^{op}
-modules: its objects are families
P=(P(k,l))_{(k,l)\in\mathbb{N}_{0}^{2}}
, such that for any
(k,l)\in\mathbb{N}_{0}^{2}
,
P(k,l)
is a
\mathfrak{S}_{l}\times\mathfrak{S}_{k}^{op}
-module; a morphism
\phi:P\longrightarrow Q
is a family
(\phi(k,l))_{(k,l)\in\mathbb{N}^{2}}
, where for any
(k,l)\in\mathbb{N}_{0}^{2}
,
\phi(k,l):P(k,l)\longrightarrow Q(k,l)
is a morphism of
\mathfrak{S}_{l}\times\mathfrak{S}_{k}^{op}
-modules.

We can now state the main result of this subsection:

Proposition 5.1.5
.

The correspondences
{\bf sol}\Gamma^{\circlearrowright},\Gamma^{\circlearrowright}:\mathbf{Mod}_{\mathfrak{S}}\longrightarrow\mathbf{Mod}_{\mathfrak{S}}
define endofunctors of the category
\mathbf{Mod}_{\mathfrak{S}}
.

Proof.

We have already seen that the correspondences
P\to{\bf sol}\Gamma^{\circlearrowright}(P)
and
P\to\Gamma^{\circlearrowright}(P)
send
\mathfrak{S}\times\mathfrak{S}^{op}
-modules to
\mathfrak{S}\times\mathfrak{S}^{op}
-modules. So we only need to specify the action of the functors on morphisms of
\mathfrak{S}\times\mathfrak{S}^{op}
-modules:

Let
X
and
Y
be two
\mathfrak{S}\times\mathfrak{S}^{op}
-modules and
\varphi:X\longrightarrow Y
a morphism of
\mathfrak{S}\times\mathfrak{S}^{op}
-modules and let
\mathbf{{solCGr}^{\circlearrowright}}(\varphi):\mathbf{{solCGr}^{\circlearrowright}}(P)\longrightarrow\mathbf{{solCGr}^{\circlearrowright}}(Q)
(resp.
\mathbf{{CGr}^{\circlearrowright}}(\varphi):\mathbf{{CGr}^{\circlearrowright}}(P)\longrightarrow\mathbf{{CGr}^{\circlearrowright}}(Q)
) be its pullback defined by

\mathbf{{solCGr}^{\circlearrowright}}(\varphi)(G,d_{G}):=(G,\varphi\circ d_{G})

(18)

(resp.
\mathbf{{CGr}^{\circlearrowright}}(\varphi)(G,d_{G}):=(G,\varphi\circ d_{G})
) for any
G\in\mathbf{{solCGr}^{\circlearrowright}}(P)
(resp. in
\mathbf{{CGr}^{\circlearrowright}}(P)
).

Is is easy to check that the induced morphisms
{\bf sol}\Gamma^{\circlearrowright}(\varphi):{\bf sol}\Gamma^{\circlearrowright}(P)\longrightarrow{\bf sol}\Gamma^{\circlearrowright}(Q)
and
\Gamma^{\circlearrowright}(\varphi):\Gamma^{\circlearrowright}(P)\longrightarrow\Gamma^{\circlearrowright}(Q)
are indeed morphisms of
\mathfrak{S}\times\mathfrak{S}^{op}
-modules turning
{\bf sol}\Gamma^{\circlearrowright}
and
\Gamma^{\circlearrowright}
into endofunctors of
\mathbf{Mod}_{\mathfrak{S}}
.
∎

5.2
Monad of graphs

We now endow the functor
\Gamma^{\circlearrowright}
with a monad structure.

We first recall basic definitions of category theory. In particular, for two functors
F,G:\mathcal{C}\longrightarrow\mathcal{D}
, a

natural transformation

\eta:F\longrightarrow G
between these two functors is given by maps
\eta_{X}:F(X)\longmapsto G(X)
for each object
X
of
\mathcal{C}
such that for any pair of objects
X,Y\in\mathrm{Obj}(\mathcal{C})
and morphism
f:X\longmapsto Y\in\mathrm{Mor}(\mathcal{C})
the following diagram commutes:

\textstyle{F(X)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{F(f)}
\scriptstyle{\eta_{X}}
\textstyle{F(Y)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\eta_{Y}}
\textstyle{G(X)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{G(f)}
\textstyle{G(Y).}

Let us now introduce the structure of monad, a terminology we borrow from
[
ML98
]
. A monad is the categorical equivalent of monoids.

Definition 5.2.1
.

A
monad
(also called a triple) on a category
\mathcal{C}
is given by an endofunctor
\Gamma\in\mathrm{End}(\mathcal{C})
and two natural transformations
\mu:\Gamma\circ\Gamma\longrightarrow\Gamma
and
\nu:\mathrm{Id}_{\mathcal{C}}\longrightarrow\Gamma
which form an associative and unital monoid
(\Gamma,\mu,\nu)
in the unital monoid
9
9

9

The terminology monoid is used in this definition with an obvious abuse of vocabulary since
\Gamma
and
\mathrm{End}(\mathcal{C})
are not necessarily sets.

\mathrm{End}(\mathcal{C})
of endofunctors of
\mathcal{C}
, where are two natural transformations. This means that the multiplication
\mu:\Gamma\circ\Gamma\longrightarrow\Gamma
and the unit morphism
\nu:\mathrm{Id}_{\mathcal{C}}\longrightarrow\Gamma
should satisfy the axioms given by commutativity of the diagrams below for any object
P
of the category
\mathcal{C}
.

\textstyle{\Gamma\circ\Gamma\circ\Gamma(P)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Gamma(\mu_{P})}
\scriptstyle{\mu_{\Gamma(P)}}

We now recall the notion of
\Gamma
-algebra (see e.g.
[
MMS09
, Definition 2.1.4]
).

Definition 5.2.2
.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be a category. An algebra over a monad
\Gamma\in\mathrm{End}(\mathcal{C})
or a
\Gamma
-algebra
is an object
P
of
\mathcal{C}
together with a
structure morphism
\alpha:\Gamma(P)\to P
such that
the following diagrams commute:

\textstyle{\Gamma\circ\Gamma(P)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Gamma(\alpha)}
\scriptstyle{\mu_{P}}
\scriptstyle{\mu_{P}}
\textstyle{\Gamma(P)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\alpha}
\textstyle{\Gamma(P)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\alpha}
\textstyle{P}

\textstyle{P\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\nu_{P}}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{Id}}
\textstyle{\Gamma(P)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\alpha}
\textstyle{P}

Let
(P,\alpha)
and
(Q,\beta)
be two algebras over a fixed monad
\Gamma
. A morphism of
\Gamma
-algebras
from
P
to
Q
is a morphism
\phi:P\longrightarrow Q
in the category
\mathcal{C}
such that the following diagram commutes:

\textstyle{\Gamma(P)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Gamma(\phi)}
\scriptstyle{\alpha}
\textstyle{P\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\phi}
\textstyle{\Gamma(Q)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\beta}
\textstyle{Q}

(39)

We now define the natural transformations
\nu
and
\mu
in the case
\mathcal{C}=\mathbf{Mod}_{\mathfrak{S}}
and
\Gamma=\Gamma^{\circlearrowright}
. In this case, for any
\mathfrak{S}\times\mathfrak{S}^{op}
-module
P
, elements of
\Gamma^{\circlearrowright}\circ\Gamma^{\circlearrowright}(P)
are graphs
G
whose vertices
v
are decorated by graphs
G_{v}
, consistently with the number of incoming and outgoing edges.

Definition 5.2.3
.

1.

For any
\mathfrak{S}\times\mathfrak{S}^{op}
-module
P
, let
\eta_{P}:P\longrightarrow\Gamma^{\circlearrowright}(P)
be the morphism of
\mathfrak{S}\times\mathfrak{S}^{op}
-modules which sends an element
p\in P(k,l)
to the class of the graph
G(k,l)(p)
with one vertex
v
decorated by
p
,
k
incoming edges indexed from left to right by
1,\ldots,k
and
l
outgoing edges indexed from left to right by
1,\ldots,l
.

2.

For any
\mathfrak{S}\times\mathfrak{S}^{op}
-module
P
, let
\mu_{P}:\Gamma^{\circlearrowright}\circ\Gamma^{\circlearrowright}(P)\longrightarrow\Gamma^{\circlearrowright}(P)
be the morphism
\mathfrak{S}\times\mathfrak{S}^{op}
-modules which sends a graph
G\in\Gamma^{\circlearrowright}\circ\Gamma^{\circlearrowright}(P)
to the graph
H\in\Gamma^{\circlearrowright}(P)
with
V(H)=\bigsqcup_{v\in V(G)}V(G_{v})
and whose edges are obtained by identifying, for any vertex
v
, the
i
-th incoming edges of
v
with the
i
-th incoming edge of
G_{v}
, and the
j
-th outgoing edge of
v
with the
j
-th outgoing edge of
G_{v}
.

In simpler words, the map
\eta_{P}
sends an element
p\in P
to the graph with one vertex, which is decorated by
p
and has the same numbers of input and output edges as
p
has inputs and outputs. In picture:

where
p\in P(2,3)
,
q\in P(2,2)
and
r\in P(2,3)
.

The families of morphisms
\eta_{P}
and
\mu_{P}
define two natural tranformations and we further obtain:

Proposition 5.2.4
.

The triple
\Gamma^{\circlearrowright}=(\Gamma^{\circlearrowright},\mu,\nu)
is a monad in the category
\mathbf{Mod}_{\mathfrak{S}}
.
Moreover,
{\bf sol}\Gamma^{\circlearrowright}=({\bf sol}\Gamma^{\circlearrowright},\mu_{\mid{\bf sol}\Gamma^{\circlearrowright}},\mu_{\mid{\bf sol}\Gamma^{\circlearrowright}})
is a sub-monad of
\Gamma^{\circlearrowright}
.

5.3
TRAPs versus wheeled PROPs

We can now state the main result of this section, which relates TRAPs and various known objects.

Theorem 5.3.1
.

The categories of
\Gamma^{\circlearrowright}
-algebras (i.e of wheeled PROPs) and of unitary TRAPs are isomorphic.
Similarly, the categories of
{\bf sol}\Gamma^{\circlearrowright}
-algebras and of TRAPs are isomorphic.

Remark 5.3.1
.

Wheeled PROPs are defined (for example in
[
MMS09
]
) as
\Gamma^{\circlearrowright}
-algebras. Thus Theorem
5.3.1
precisely says that wheeled PROPs and unitary TRAPs coincide, and that TRAPs can be viewed as non-unitary wheeled PROPs.

Proof.

Let us start with the TRAP case, i.e. the non-unitary case.

From Proposition
5.1.3
, we know that
{\bf sol}\Gamma^{\circlearrowright}(P)
is the free TRAP
generated by the
\mathfrak{S}\times\mathfrak{S}^{op}
-module
P
.
Let
\nu_{P}
be the canonical injection from
P
to
{\bf sol}\Gamma^{\circlearrowright}(P)
. If
P
is a TRAP, then the canonical TRAP morphism

{\alpha_{P}}:\Gamma^{\circlearrowright}(P)\longrightarrow P
makes it a
\Gamma^{\circlearrowright}
-algebra, thus defining a functor from the category
of TRAPs to the category of
{\bf sol}\Gamma^{\circlearrowright}
-algebras.

Conversely, if
(P,\alpha)
is a
{\bf sol}\Gamma^{\circlearrowright}
-algebra:

•

For any
(p,p^{\prime})\in P(k,l)\times P(k^{\prime},l^{\prime})
,we define
p*p^{\prime}
by applying
\alpha
to the following graph:

p
1
\ldots
k
1
\ldots
l
p^{\prime}
k+1
\ldots
k+k^{\prime}
l+1
\ldots
l+l^{\prime}

•

For any
p\in P(k,l)
, for any
(i,j)\in[k]\times[l]
, we define
t_{i,j}(p)
by the application of
\alpha
to the following graph:

p
1
\ldots
i-1
i
\ldots
k-1
1
\ldots
j-1
j
\ldots
l-1

Let us prove some of the axioms of TRAPs for
P
. The others can be proved in the same way and are left to the reader.

1. holds by Proposition
5.1.5
.

2. (a): let
(p,p^{\prime},p^{\prime\prime})\in P(k,l)\times P(k^{\prime},l^{\prime})\times P(k^{\prime\prime},l^{\prime\prime})
. Then
(p*p^{\prime})*p^{\prime\prime}
is obtained by the application
of
\alpha_{P}
to the graph:

p*p^{\prime}
\ldots
\ldots
p^{\prime\prime}
\ldots
\ldots

(For the sake of simplicity, we delete the indices of the input and output edges of this graph: they are always indexed from left to right).
Hence,
(p*p^{\prime})*p^{\prime\prime}
is obtained by application of
\alpha\circ\Gamma^{\circlearrowright}(\alpha)
to the graph:

p
\ldots
\ldots
p^{\prime}
\ldots
\ldots
p^{\prime\prime}
\ldots
\ldots
\ldots
\ldots
\ldots
\ldots

Note that for the second connected component of this graph, this comes from:

\alpha\circ\Gamma^{\circlearrowright}(\alpha)\circ\Gamma^{\circlearrowright}(\nu_{P})(p^{\prime\prime})=\alpha\circ\Gamma^{\circlearrowright}(\alpha\circ\nu_{P})(p^{\prime\prime})=\alpha\circ\Gamma^{\circlearrowright}(\mathrm{Id}_{P})(p^{\prime\prime})=\alpha(p^{\prime\prime}).

As
\alpha\circ\Gamma^{\circlearrowright}(\alpha)=\alpha\circ\mu_{P}
,
(p*p^{\prime})*p^{\prime\prime}
is obtained by applying
\alpha
to the graph:

p
\ldots
\ldots
p^{\prime}
\ldots
\ldots
p^{\prime\prime}
\ldots
\ldots

The same computation can be carried out for
p*(p^{\prime}*p^{\prime\prime})
, which gives the associativity of
*
.

2. (b): the unity
I_{0}
of the concatenation product of graph is the empty graph, which is the image of the unity of
P
for the horizontal concatenation under
\alpha
.

2. (c) holds trivially by definition of the horizontal concatenation product on
P
, the
\mathfrak{S}\times\mathfrak{S}^{op}
-module structure of
P
, and the fact that
\mathbf{{solCGr}^{\circlearrowright}}(X)
is a TRAP.

3. (c): for any
k,l,k^{\prime},l^{\prime}\geqslant 1
, for any
i\in[k]
,
j\in[l]
, for any
p\in P(k,l)
,
p^{\prime}\in P(k^{\prime},l^{\prime})
,
t_{i,j}(p*p^{\prime})
is the image under
\alpha_{P}
of the graph

\displaystyle\begin{subarray}{c}\hskip 28.45274pt\\

(41)

with
\tilde{p}
the image under
\alpha_{P}
of the graph

p
1
\ldots
i-1
i
\ldots
k-1
1
\ldots
j-1
j
\ldots
l-1

The images of the graphs (
40
) and (
41
) under
\alpha_{P}
are identical by the commutativity of the first diagram of (
5.2.2
). The case
l+1\leqslant j\leqslant l+l^{\prime}
and
k+1\leqslant i\leqslant k+k^{\prime}
holds by the same argument.

Let us now focus on the unitary case.

First if
P
is a unitary TRAP, then by Theorem
3.3.1
,
(P,\mu_{P})
is a
\Gamma^{\circlearrowright}
-algebra with exactly the same argument as in the non unitary case.

Conversely, let
(P,\alpha_{P})
be a
\Gamma^{\circlearrowright}
-algebra. We then set

I:=\alpha_{P}(I_{1})

where
I_{1}
is the graph with only one input-output edge.

Let
p\in P(k,l)
and
2\leqslant j\leqslant l+1
. Then
t_{1,j}(I*p)
is obtained by applying

\alpha\circ\Gamma^{\circlearrowright}(\alpha)
to the graph:

p
\ldots
\ldots
\ldots
\ldots
\ldots

where the curved edge relate the first edge at the bottom to the
j
-th edge on the top.
As
\alpha\circ\Gamma^{\circlearrowright}(\alpha)=\alpha\circ\mu_{P}
,
t_{1,j}(I*p)
is obtained by application
of
\alpha
to the graph:

p
\ldots
\ldots
\ldots

where the curved edge relate the first edge on the bottom to the
j
-th edge on the top (note that this edge is also
the
(j-1)
-th outgoing the vertex decorated by
p
). As
\alpha
is a
\mathfrak{S}\times\mathfrak{S}^{op}
morphism,
we obtain that this is
(1,\ldots,j-1)\cdot\alpha\circ\nu_{P}(p)
, that is to say
(1,\ldots,j-1)\cdot p
.

In this way, we define a functor from the category of
r\Gamma^{\circlearrowright}
-algebras to the category of TRAPs.
In the same way, we define a functor from the category of
\Gamma^{\circlearrowright}
-algebras to the category of unitary TRAPs.

We obtain in this way two functors

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}

\displaystyle:\mathbf{TRAP}\longrightarrow{\bf sol}\Gamma^{\circlearrowright}-\mathbf{Alg},

\displaystyle\mathcal{G}

\displaystyle:{\bf sol}\Gamma^{\circlearrowright}-\mathbf{Alg}\longrightarrow\mathbf{TRAP}.

Let
P
be a TRAP and
P^{\prime}
the TRAP
\mathcal{G}\circ\mathcal{F}(P)
, with concatenation
*^{\prime}

and trace operators
t^{\prime}_{i,j}
.
We set
\mathcal{F}(P):=(P,\alpha_{P})
: in other words,
\alpha_{P}
is the TRAP morphism from
{\bf sol}\Gamma^{\circlearrowright}(P)
to
P

which is the identity on
P
. For any
p,q\in P
:

p*^{\prime}q=\alpha_{P}(\nu_{P}(p)*\nu_{P}(q))={p*q},

where in the middle term
*
is the concatenation in the TRAP
{\bf sol}\Gamma^{\circlearrowright}(P)
and where we used that
\alpha_{P}
is a TRAP morphism by Proposition
5.1.3
. Therefore,
*=*^{\prime}
.
If
p\in P(k,l)
,
(i,j)\in[k]\times[l]
, then
t^{\prime}_{i,j}
is obtained by the application of
\alpha_{P}
to the graph:

p
1
\ldots
i-1
i
\ldots
k-1
1
\ldots
j-1
j
\ldots
l-1

which is
t_{i,j}(\nu_{P}(p))
, where here
t_{i,j}
is the trace operator of
{\bf sol}\Gamma^{\circlearrowright}(P)
. As
\alpha_{P}
is a TRAP morphism:

t^{\prime}_{i,j}(p)=\alpha_{P}\circ t_{i,j}\circ\nu_{P}(p)=t_{i,j}\circ\alpha_{P}\circ\nu_{P}(p)=t_{i,j}(p),

so
P^{\prime}=P
and
\mathcal{G}\circ\mathcal{F}
is the identity functor of
\mathbf{TRAP}
.

Let now
(P,\alpha)
be a
{\bf sol}\Gamma^{\circlearrowright}
-algebra and let us consider
(P^{\prime},\alpha^{\prime})
be the
{\bf sol}\Gamma^{\circlearrowright}
-algebra

\mathcal{F}\circ\mathcal{G}(P)
. Both
\alpha
and
\alpha^{\prime}
are TRAP morphisms from

{\bf sol}\Gamma^{\circlearrowright}(P)
to
\mathcal{G}(P)
; for any
p\in P
,

\alpha\circ\nu_{P}(p)=\alpha^{\prime}\circ\nu_{P}(p)=p.

Since
{\bf sol}\Gamma^{\circlearrowright}(P)
viewed as a TRAP is generated by the elements
\nu_{P}(p)
,
\alpha=\alpha^{\prime}
,
it follows that
\mathcal{F}\circ\mathcal{G}
is the identity functor of
{\bf sol}\Gamma^{\circlearrowright}-\mathbf{Alg}
.

The proof is similar in the unitary case. ∎

Remark
5.3.1
gives a straightforward Corollary of Theorems
5.3.1
and
3.3.1
,
thus confirming previous statements.

Corollary 5.3.2
.

\mathbf{{CGr}^{\circlearrowright}}(X)
is the free wheeled PROP generated by
X
.

Remark 5.3.2
.

The monad
\Gamma^{\circlearrowright}
contains an interesting sub-monad, formed by graphs without any oriented cycle (which includes also loops).
This submonad is denoted by
\Gamma^{\uparrow}
. It is well-known that
\Gamma^{\uparrow}
is the monad of PROPs
[
Mar08
]
.
Hence, unitary TRAPs are PROPS. In particular, a unitary TRAP
P
inherits a vertical composition denoted by
\circ
,
which is the one described in Proposition
4.1.1
in the more general frame of (non unitary) TRAPs.

Appendix A
Appendix: topologies on tensor products

Tensor products ot topological spaces can be equipped with various topologies. A first possibility is the so-called
\epsilon
-topology
;
[
Trè67
, Definition 43.1]
. For two topological vector spaces
E
and
F
, one
can show (
[
Trè67
, Proposition 42.4]
) the isomorphism of vector spaces

E\otimes F\simeq\mathcal{B}^{c}(E^{\prime}_{\sigma}\times F^{\prime}_{\sigma},\mathbb{K})
where

\mathcal{B}^{c}(E^{\prime}_{\sigma}\times F^{\prime}_{\sigma},\mathbb{K})
denotes the space of continuous bilinear maps from
E^{\prime}_{\sigma}\times F^{\prime}_{\sigma}
to
\mathbb{K}
and
E^{\prime}_{\sigma}
(resp.

F^{\prime}_{\sigma}
) the topological dual of
E
(resp.
F
) for
\sigma
, the weak topology.

Recall that a bilinear map
f:E\times F\longrightarrow K
is called separately continuous if,
for any pair
(x,y)\in E\times F
, the maps
z\to f(x,z)
and
z\to f(z,y)
are continuous. We then clearly have that continuous bilinear maps
build a linear subspace of the space
\mathcal{B}^{sc}(E\times F,\mathbb{K})
of separately continuous bilinear maps.

The space
\mathcal{B}^{sc}(E\times F,\mathbb{K})
can be equipped with the topology of uniform convergence on products of equicontinuous subsets of
E^{\prime}_{\sigma}
with
equicontinuous subsets of
F^{\prime}_{\sigma}
. Recall that, for a topological space
X
and a topological vector space
G
, a set
S
of maps from
X
to
G
is
said to be equicontinuous at
x_{0}\in X
if, for any
V\subseteq G
neighbourhood of zero, there is some neighbourhood
V(x_{0})\subseteq X
of
x_{0}
, such
that

\forall f\in S,\penalty\ x\in V(x_{0})\Rightarrow f(x)-f(x_{0})\in V.

In our case,
G
is
\mathbb{K}
and
X
is
E_{\sigma}
(resp.
F_{\sigma}
). This topology induces a topology on the subspace

\mathcal{B}^{c}(E^{\prime}_{\sigma}\times F^{\prime}_{\sigma},\mathbb{K})
and thus on
E\otimes F
. We denote by
E\otimes_{\epsilon}F
the topological vector space obtained by
endowing
E\otimes F
with this topology.

There is another topology on
E\otimes F
called the
projective topology
;

[
Trè67
, Definition 43.2]
. The projective topology is defined as the strongest
locally convex topology on
E\otimes F
such that the canonical map
\phi:E\times F\longrightarrow E\otimes F
is continuous.
We write
E\otimes_{\pi}F
the topological vector space obtained by
endowing
E\otimes F
with this topology.

The neighbourhoods of zero of the projective topology can be simply described in terms of neighbourhoods of zero in
E
and
V
. A convex subset
S
of

E\otimes F
containing zero is a neighbourhood of zero if it exist a neighbourhood
U
(resp. V) of zero in
E
(resp.
F
) such that

U\otimes V:=\{u\otimes v|u\in U\wedge v\in V\}\subseteq S
.

Various topologies can be defined on the vector space
E\otimes F
for
E
and
F
two topological vector spaces. However the projective topology and the
\epsilon
-topology play an important special role since they allow to define nuclear spaces (see Definition
2.2.1
).

Appendix B
Appendix: definition of the partial trace maps on
\mathbf{Gr^{\circlearrowright}}

We give a rigorous definition of the partial trace maps on the space of graphs
\mathbf{Gr^{\circlearrowright}}
, which were only loosely defined in the bulk of the article.

Let
G\in\mathbf{Gr^{\circlearrowright}}(k,l)
with
k,l\geqslant 1
,
i\in[k]
and
j\in[l]
. We put
e_{i}=\alpha_{G}^{-1}(i)

and
f_{j}=\beta_{G}^{-1}(j)
. We define the graph
G^{\prime}=t_{i,j}(G)
in the following way:

1.

If
e_{i}\in I(G)
and
f_{j}\in O(G)
, then:

\displaystyle V(G^{\prime})

\displaystyle=V(G),

\displaystyle E(G^{\prime})

\displaystyle=E(G)\sqcup\{(e_{i},f_{j})\},

\displaystyle I(G^{\prime})

\displaystyle=I(G)\setminus\{e_{i}\},

\displaystyle O(G^{\prime})

\displaystyle=O(G)\setminus\{f_{j}\},

\displaystyle IO(G^{\prime})

\displaystyle=IO(G),

\displaystyle L(G^{\prime})

\displaystyle=L(G),

\displaystyle s_{G^{\prime}}(e)

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}s_{G}(f_{j})\mbox{ if }e=(e_{i},f_{j}),\\
s_{G}(e)\mbox{ otherwise},\end{cases}

\displaystyle t_{G^{\prime}}(e)

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}t_{G}(e_{i})\mbox{ if }e=(e_{i},f_{j}),\\
t_{G}(e)\mbox{ otherwise},\end{cases}

\displaystyle\alpha_{G^{\prime}}(e)

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}\alpha_{G}(e)\mbox{ if }\alpha_{G}(e)<i,\\
\alpha_{G}(e)-1\mbox{ if }\alpha_{G}(e)\geqslant i,\end{cases}

\displaystyle\beta_{G^{\prime}}(e)

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}\beta_{G}(e)\mbox{ if }\beta_{G}(e)<j,\\
\beta_{G}(e)-1\mbox{ if }\beta_{G}(e)\geqslant j.\end{cases}

2.

If
e_{i}\in IO(G)
and
f_{j}\in O(G)
, then:

\displaystyle V(G^{\prime})

\displaystyle=V(G),

\displaystyle E(G^{\prime})

\displaystyle=E(G),

\displaystyle I(G^{\prime})

\displaystyle=I(G),

\displaystyle O(G^{\prime})

\displaystyle=O(G)\setminus\{f_{j}\}\sqcup\{(e_{i},f_{j},)\},

\displaystyle IO(G^{\prime})

\displaystyle=IO(G)\setminus\{e_{i}\},

\displaystyle L(G^{\prime})

\displaystyle=L(G),

\displaystyle s_{G^{\prime}}(e)

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}s_{G}(f_{j})\mbox{ if }e=(e_{i},f_{j}),\\
s_{G}(e)\mbox{ otherwise},\end{cases}

\displaystyle t_{G^{\prime}}(e)

\displaystyle=t_{G}(e),

\displaystyle\alpha_{G^{\prime}}(e)

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}\alpha_{G}(e)\mbox{ if }\alpha_{G}(e)<i,\\
\alpha_{G}(e)-1\mbox{ if }\alpha_{G}(e)\geqslant i,\end{cases}

\displaystyle\beta_{G^{\prime}}(e)

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}\beta_{G}(e_{i})\mbox{ if }e=(e_{i},f_{j})\mbox{ and }\beta_{G}(e_{i})<j,\\
\beta_{G}(e_{i})-1\mbox{ if }e=(e_{i},f_{j})\mbox{ and }\beta_{G}(e_{i})\geqslant j,\\
\beta_{G}(e)\mbox{ if }e\neq(e_{i},f_{j})\mbox{ and }\beta_{G}(e)<j,\\
\beta_{G}(e)-1\mbox{ if }e\neq(e_{i},f_{j})\mbox{ and }\beta_{G}(e)\geqslant j.\end{cases}

3.

If
e_{i}\in I(G)
and
f_{j}\in IO(G)
, then:

\displaystyle V(G^{\prime})

\displaystyle=V(G),

\displaystyle E(G^{\prime})

\displaystyle=E(G),

\displaystyle I(G^{\prime})

\displaystyle=I(G)\setminus\{e_{i}\}\sqcup\{(e_{i},f_{j})\},

\displaystyle O(G^{\prime})

\displaystyle=O(G),

\displaystyle IO(G^{\prime})

\displaystyle=IO(G)\setminus\{f_{j}\},

\displaystyle L(G^{\prime})

\displaystyle=L(G),

\displaystyle s_{G^{\prime}}(e)

\displaystyle=s_{G}(e),

\displaystyle t_{G^{\prime}}(e)

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}t_{G}(e_{i})\mbox{ if }e=(e_{i},f_{j}),\\
t_{G}(e)\mbox{ otherwise},\end{cases}

\displaystyle\alpha_{G^{\prime}}(e)

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}\alpha_{G}(f_{i})\mbox{ if }e=(e_{i},f_{j})\mbox{ and }\alpha_{G}(f_{j})<i,\\
\alpha_{G}(f_{i})-1\mbox{ if }e=(e_{i},f_{j})\mbox{ and }\alpha_{G}(f_{j})\geqslant i,\\
\alpha_{G}(e)\mbox{ if }e\neq(e_{i},f_{j})\mbox{ and }\alpha_{G}(e)<i,\\
\alpha_{G}(e)-1\mbox{ if }e\neq(e_{i},f_{j})\mbox{ and }\alpha_{G}(e)\geqslant i,\end{cases}

\displaystyle\beta_{G^{\prime}}(e)

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}\beta_{G}(e)\mbox{ if }\beta_{G}(e)<j,\\
\beta_{G}(e)-1\mbox{ if }\beta_{G}(e)\geqslant j.\end{cases}

4.

If
e_{i}\in IO(G)
,
f_{j}\in IO(G)
and
e_{i}\neq f_{j}
, then:

\displaystyle V(G^{\prime})

\displaystyle=V(G),

\displaystyle E(G^{\prime})

\displaystyle=E(G),

\displaystyle I(G^{\prime})

\displaystyle=I(G),

\displaystyle O(G^{\prime})

\displaystyle=O(G),

\displaystyle IO(G^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\{(e_{i},f_{j})\}\sqcup IO(G)\setminus\{e_{i},f_{j}\},

\displaystyle L(G^{\prime})

\displaystyle=L(G),

\displaystyle s_{G^{\prime}}(e)

\displaystyle=s_{G}(e),

\displaystyle t_{G^{\prime}}(e)

\displaystyle=t_{G}(e),

\displaystyle\alpha_{G^{\prime}}(e)

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}\alpha_{G}(f_{i})\mbox{ if }e=(e_{i},f_{j})\mbox{ and }\alpha_{G}(f_{j})<i,\\
\alpha_{G}(f_{i})-1\mbox{ if }e=(e_{i},f_{j})\mbox{ and }\alpha_{G}(f_{j})\geqslant i,\\
\alpha_{G}(e)\mbox{ if }e\neq(e_{i},f_{j})\mbox{ and }\alpha_{G}(e)<i,\\
\beta_{G}(e)-1\mbox{ if }e\neq(e_{i},f_{j})\mbox{ and }\alpha_{G}(e)\geqslant i,\end{cases}

\displaystyle\beta_{G^{\prime}}(e)

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}\beta_{G}(e_{i})\mbox{ if }e=(e_{i},f_{j})\mbox{ and }\beta_{G}(e_{i})<j,\\
\beta_{G}(e_{i})-1\mbox{ if }e=(e_{i},f_{j})\mbox{ and }\beta_{G}(e_{i})\geqslant j,\\
\beta_{G}(e)\mbox{ if }e\neq(e_{i},f_{j})\mbox{ and }\beta_{G}(e)<j,\\
\beta_{G}(e)-1\mbox{ if }e\neq(e_{i},f_{j})\mbox{ and }\beta_{G}(e)\geqslant j.\end{cases}

5.

If
e_{i}\in IO(G)
,
f_{j}\in IO(G)
and
e_{i}=f_{j}
, then:

\displaystyle V(G^{\prime})

\displaystyle=V(G),

\displaystyle E(G^{\prime})

\displaystyle=E(G),

\displaystyle I(G^{\prime})

\displaystyle=I(G),

\displaystyle O(G^{\prime})

\displaystyle=O(G),

\displaystyle IO(G^{\prime})

\displaystyle=IO(G)\setminus\{e_{i},f_{j}\},

\displaystyle L(G^{\prime})

\displaystyle=L(G)\sqcup\{(e_{i},f_{j})\},

\displaystyle s_{G^{\prime}}(e)

\displaystyle=s_{G}(e),

\displaystyle t_{G^{\prime}}(e)

\displaystyle=t_{G}(e),

\displaystyle\alpha_{G^{\prime}}(e)

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}\alpha_{G}(e)\mbox{ if }\alpha_{G}(e)<i,\\
\alpha_{G}(e)-1\mbox{ if }\alpha_{G}(e)\geqslant i,\end{cases}

\displaystyle\beta_{G^{\prime}}(e)

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}\beta_{G}(e)\mbox{ if }\beta_{G}(e)<j,\\
\beta_{G}(e)-1\mbox{ if }\beta_{G}(e)\geqslant j.\end{cases}

Appendix C
Appendix: Freeness of
\mathbf{{CGr}^{\circlearrowright}}(X)

We now give a detailed proof of Theorem
3.3.1
.

Proof.

We simultaneously prove the unitary and non-unitary cases.

We first define
\Phi:\mathbf{{CGr}^{\circlearrowright}}(X)\longrightarrow P
by assigning to
any graph
G\in\mathbf{{CGr}^{\circlearrowright}}(X)(k,l)
(or
G\in\mathbf{{solCGr}^{\circlearrowright}}(X)(k,l)
for the non-unitary case)
such that
\Phi(\sigma\cdot G\cdot\tau)=\sigma\cdot\Phi(G)\cdot\tau
any
(\sigma,\tau)\in\mathfrak{S}_{l}\times\mathfrak{S}_{k}
. We proceed by induction on the number
N
of internal edges of
G
. If
N=0
, then
G
can be written (non uniquely) as

G=\mathcal{O}^{*p}*\sigma\cdot(I^{*q}*G_{k_{1},l_{1}}*\ldots*G_{k_{r},l_{r}})\cdot\tau,

where
p,q,r\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
are unique,
(k_{i},k_{i})\in\mathbb{N}_{0}^{2}
for any
i
, unique up to a permutation, and
\sigma\in\mathfrak{S}_{q+k_{1}+\ldots+k_{r}}
,
\tau\in\mathfrak{S}_{q+l_{1}+\ldots+l_{r}}
. Notice that in the non-unitary case we necessarily have
p=q=0
, and we have set in this case
g^{*0}=I_{0}=\emptyset
, the empty graph, for any graph
g
.

We then put:

\Phi(G)=t_{1,1}(I)^{*p}*\sigma\cdot(I^{*q}*x_{k_{1},l_{1}}*\ldots*x_{k_{r},l_{r}})\cdot\tau,

where as before
x^{*0}=I_{0}
(the unit for horizontal concatenation in the image
P
of
\Phi
) for any
x\in P
; and
I
is now the unit of
P
in the unitary case.

Let us prove that this does not depend of the choice of the decomposition of
G
. Such a decomposition is determined modulo a permutation of the vertices
and of the choice of
\sigma
and
\tau
. Thus, we can go from one decomposition of
G
to any other one by means of a finite number of steps among the following two types:

1.

We consider two decompositions of
G
of the form

\displaystyle G

\displaystyle=\mathcal{O}^{*p}*\sigma\cdot(I^{*q}*G_{k_{1},l_{1}}*\ldots*G_{k_{i},l_{i}}*G_{k_{i+1},l_{i+1}}*\ldots*G_{k_{r},l_{r}})\cdot\tau,

\displaystyle G

\displaystyle=\mathcal{O}^{*p}*\sigma^{\prime}\cdot(I^{*q}*G_{k_{1},l_{1}}*\ldots*G_{k_{i+1},l_{i+1}}*G_{k_{i},l_{i}}*\ldots*G_{k_{r},l_{r}})\cdot\tau^{\prime},

with

\displaystyle\sigma^{\prime}

\displaystyle=\sigma(\mathrm{Id}_{q+l_{1}+\ldots+l_{i-1}}\otimes c_{l_{i},l_{i+1}}\otimes\mathrm{Id}_{l_{i+2}+\ldots+l_{r}}),

\displaystyle\tau^{\prime}

\displaystyle=(\mathrm{Id}_{q+k_{1}+\ldots+k_{i-1}}\otimes c_{k_{i+1},k_{i}}\otimes\mathrm{Id}_{k_{i+2}+\ldots+k_{r}})\tau.

Then, by commutativity of
*
:

\displaystyle\sigma^{\prime}\cdot(I^{*q}*x_{k_{1},l_{1}}*\ldots*x_{k_{r},l_{r}})\cdot\tau^{\prime}

\displaystyle=\sigma\cdot(I^{*q}*x_{k_{1},l_{1}}*\ldots*c_{l_{i},l_{i+1}}\cdot(x_{k_{i+1},l_{i+1}}*x_{k_{i},l_{i}})\cdot c_{k_{i+1},k_{i}}*\ldots*x_{k_{r},l_{r}})\cdot\tau

\displaystyle=\sigma\cdot(I^{*q}*x_{k_{1},l_{1}}*\ldots*x_{k_{i},l_{i}}*x_{k_{i+1},l_{i+1}}*\ldots*x_{k_{r},l_{r}})\cdot\tau.

2.

We consider two decompositions of
G
of the form

\displaystyle G

\displaystyle=\mathcal{O}^{*p}*\sigma\cdot(I^{*q}*G_{k_{1},l_{1}}*\ldots*G_{k_{r},l_{r}})\cdot\tau,

\displaystyle G

\displaystyle=\mathcal{O}^{*p}*\sigma^{\prime}\cdot(I^{*q}*G_{k_{1},l_{1}}*\ldots*G_{k_{r},l_{r}})\cdot\tau^{\prime},

with

\displaystyle\sigma^{\prime}

\displaystyle=\sigma(\sigma_{0}\otimes\sigma_{1}\otimes\ldots\otimes\sigma_{r}),

\displaystyle\tau^{\prime}

\displaystyle=(\sigma_{0}^{-1}\otimes\tau_{1}\otimes\ldots\otimes\tau_{r})\tau^{\prime},

with
\sigma_{0}\in\mathfrak{S}_{q}
,
\sigma_{i}\in\mathfrak{S}_{k_{i}}
and
\tau_{i}\in\mathfrak{S}_{l_{i}}
if
i\geqslant 1
.
Using the commutativity of
*
and the invariance of the
x_{k,l}
, we find

\displaystyle\sigma^{\prime}\cdot(I^{*q}*x_{k_{1},l_{1}}*\ldots*x_{k_{r},l_{r}})\cdot\tau^{\prime}

\displaystyle=\sigma\cdot(\sigma_{0}\cdot I^{*q}\cdot\sigma_{0}^{-1}*\sigma_{1}\cdot x_{k_{1},l_{1}}\cdot\tau_{1}*\ldots*\sigma_{r}\cdot x_{k_{r},l_{r}}\cdot\tau_{r})\cdot\tau

\displaystyle=\sigma\cdot(I^{*q}*x_{k_{1},l_{1}}*\ldots*x_{k_{r},l_{r}})\cdot\tau.

Notice that setting
p=q=0
in these computations does not change the result.

Hence,
\Phi(G)
is well-defined. Moreover, for any
\tau^{\prime}\in\mathfrak{S}_{k}
,
\sigma^{\prime}\in\mathfrak{S}_{l}
, a decomposition of
G
of the form

G=\mathcal{O}^{*p}*\sigma\cdot(I^{*q}*G_{k_{1},l_{1}}*\ldots*G_{k_{i},l_{i}}*G_{k_{i+1},l_{i+1}}*\ldots*G_{k_{r},l_{r}})\cdot\tau,

give rise to a decomposition of
G^{\prime}=\sigma^{\prime}\cdot G\cdot\tau^{\prime}
given by

\mathcal{O}^{*p}*\sigma^{\prime}\sigma\cdot(I^{*q}*G_{k_{1},l_{1}}*\ldots*G_{k_{r},l_{r}})\cdot\tau\tau^{\prime},

and, by definition of
\Phi(G^{\prime})
:

\displaystyle\Phi(G^{\prime})

\displaystyle=t_{1,1}(I)^{*p}*\sigma^{\prime}\sigma\cdot(I^{*q}*x_{k_{1},l_{1}}*\ldots*x_{k_{r},l_{r}})\cdot\tau\tau^{\prime}

\displaystyle=\sigma^{\prime}\cdot(t_{1,1}(I)^{*}p*\sigma\cdot(I^{*q}*x_{k_{1},l_{1}}*\ldots*x_{k_{r},l_{r}})\cdot\tau)*\tau^{\prime}

\displaystyle=\sigma^{\prime}\cdot\Phi(G)\cdot\tau^{\prime}.

Here again, the computations are valid in particular in the case
p=q=0
, so for the non-unitary case.

Let us assume now that
\Phi(G^{\prime})
is defined for any graph with
N-1
internal edges, for a given
N\geqslant 1
.
Let
G
be a graph with
N
internal edges and let
e
be one of these edges.
Let
G_{e}
be a graph obtained by cutting this edge in two:

1.

V(G_{e})=V(G)
.

2.

E(G_{e})=E(G)\setminus\{e\}
,
I(G_{e})=I(G)\sqcup\{e\}
,
O(G_{e})=O(G)\sqcup\{e\}
,
IO(G_{e})=IO(G)
,
L(G_{e})=L(G)
.

3.

s_{G_{e}}=s_{G}
and
t_{G_{e}}=t_{G}
.

4.

For any
e^{\prime}\in I(G_{e})\sqcup IO(G_{e})
, for any
f^{\prime}\in O(G_{e})\sqcup IO(G_{e})
:

\displaystyle\alpha_{G_{e}}(e^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}1\mbox{ if }e^{\prime}=e,\\
\alpha_{G}(e^{\prime})+1\mbox{ if }e^{\prime}\neq e,\end{cases}

\displaystyle\beta_{G_{e}}(f^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}1\mbox{ if }f^{\prime}=e,\\
\beta_{G}(f^{\prime})+1\mbox{ if }f^{\prime}\neq e.\end{cases}

Notice that if
G\in\mathbf{{solCGr}^{\circlearrowright}}(X)
(i.e.
IO(G)=L(G)=\emptyset
) then
G_{e}
also lies in
\mathbf{{solCGr}^{\circlearrowright}}(X)
. Then, as before, we can treat the unitary and non-unitary cases simultaneously. In both cases we have
G=t_{1,1}(G_{e})
and
G_{e}
has
N-1
internal edges. We then put:

\Phi(G)=t_{1,1}\circ\Phi(G_{e}).

Let us prove that this does not depend of the choice of
e
. If
e^{\prime}
is another internal edge of
G
,
then:

(G_{e})_{e^{\prime}}=(12)\cdot(G_{e^{\prime}})_{e}\cdot(12),

which implies, by definition of
\Phi(G_{e})
and
\Phi(G_{e^{\prime}})
:

\displaystyle t_{1,1}\circ\Phi(G_{e})

\displaystyle=t_{1,1}\circ t_{1,1}\circ\Phi((G_{e})_{e^{\prime}})

\displaystyle=t_{1,1}\circ t_{1,1}\circ((12)\cdot\Phi((G_{e^{\prime}})_{e})\cdot(12))

\displaystyle=t_{1,1}\circ t_{2,2}\circ\Phi((G_{e^{\prime}})_{e})

\displaystyle=t_{1,1}\circ t_{1,1}\circ\Phi((G_{e^{\prime}})_{e})

\displaystyle=t_{1,1}\circ\Phi(G_{e^{\prime}}).

So
\Phi(G)
is well-defined. Let
\sigma\in\mathfrak{S}_{k}
and
\tau\in\mathfrak{S}_{l}
. Then:

(\sigma\cdot G\cdot\tau)_{e}=((1)\otimes\sigma)\cdot(G_{e})\cdot((1)\otimes\tau),

so:

\displaystyle\Phi(\sigma\cdot G\cdot\tau)

\displaystyle=t_{1,1}\circ\Phi((\sigma\cdot G\cdot\tau)_{e})

\displaystyle=t_{1,1}((1)\otimes\sigma)\cdot\Phi(G_{e})\cdot((1)\otimes\tau)

\displaystyle=((1)\otimes\sigma)_{1}\cdot t_{1,1}\circ\Phi(G_{e})\cdot((1)\otimes\tau)_{1}

\displaystyle=\sigma\cdot\Phi(G)\cdot\tau.

where, for
\sigma\in\mathfrak{S}_{k}
we use
\sigma_{i}
for the permutation in
\mathfrak{S}_{k-1}
defined by

\sigma_{i}(j)=\begin{cases}&\sigma(j)\quad\text{if }j\leqslant i-1,\\
&\sigma(j-1)\quad\text{if }j\geqslant i.\end{cases}

where
((1)\otimes\tau)_{1}
is defined by (
3
).

We have therefore defined a map
\Phi:\mathbf{{CGr}^{\circlearrowright}}(X)\longrightarrow P
(resp.
\Phi:\mathbf{{solCGr}^{\circlearrowright}}(X)\longrightarrow P
in the non-unitary case) compatible with the action of the symmetric groups. Let us prove that for any graphs
G
,
G^{\prime}
,

\Phi(G*G^{\prime})=\Phi(G)*\Phi(G^{\prime}).

We proceed by induction on the number
N
of internal edges of
G*G^{\prime}
. If
N=0
, we put:

\displaystyle G

\displaystyle=\mathcal{O}^{*p}*\sigma\cdot(I^{*q}*G_{k_{1},l_{1}}*\ldots*G_{k_{r},l_{r}})\cdot\tau,

\displaystyle G^{\prime}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{O}^{*p^{\prime}}*\sigma^{\prime}\cdots(I^{*q^{\prime}}*G_{k^{\prime}_{1},l^{\prime}_{1}}*\ldots*G_{k^{\prime}_{r^{\prime}},l^{\prime}_{r^{\prime}}})\cdot\tau^{\prime}.

As before, if we are in the non-unitary case we set
p=q=p^{\prime}=q^{\prime}=0
and the whole discussion still holds. We obtain:

\displaystyle G*G^{\prime}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{O}^{*(p+p^{\prime})}*(\sigma\otimes\sigma^{\prime})*(\mathrm{Id}_{q}\otimes c_{k_{1}+\ldots+k_{r},q^{\prime}}\otimes\mathrm{Id}_{k^{\prime}_{1}+\ldots+k^{\prime}_{r^{\prime}}})

\displaystyle\cdot(I^{q+q^{\prime}}*G_{k_{1},l_{1}}*\ldots*G_{k^{\prime}_{r^{\prime}},l^{\prime}_{r^{\prime}}})\cdot(\mathrm{Id}_{q}\otimes c_{q^{\prime},l_{1}+\ldots+l_{r}}\otimes\mathrm{Id}_{l^{\prime}_{1}+\ldots+l^{\prime}_{r^{\prime}}}),

which gives, by commutativity of
*
:

\displaystyle\Phi(G*G^{\prime})

\displaystyle=t_{1,1}(I)^{*(p+p^{\prime})}*(\sigma\otimes\sigma^{\prime})*(\mathrm{Id}_{q}\otimes c_{l_{1}+\ldots+l_{r},q^{\prime}}\otimes\mathrm{Id}_{l^{\prime}_{1}+\ldots+l^{\prime}_{r^{\prime}}})

\displaystyle\cdot(I^{q+q^{\prime}}*x_{k_{1},l_{1}}*\ldots*x_{k^{\prime}_{r^{\prime}},l^{\prime}_{r^{\prime}}})\cdot(\mathrm{Id}_{q}\otimes c_{q^{\prime},k_{1}+\ldots+k_{r}}\otimes\mathrm{Id}_{k^{\prime}_{1}+\ldots+k^{\prime}_{r^{\prime}}})

\displaystyle=t_{1,1}(I)^{*p}*\sigma\cdot(I^{*q}*x_{k_{1},l_{1}}*\ldots*x_{k_{r},l_{r}})\cdot\tau

\displaystyle*t_{1,1}(I)^{*p^{\prime}}*\sigma^{\prime}\cdot(I^{*q^{\prime}}*x_{k^{\prime}_{1},l^{\prime}_{1}}*\ldots*x_{k^{\prime}_{r^{\prime}},l^{\prime}_{r^{\prime}}})\cdot\tau^{\prime}

\displaystyle=\Phi(G)*\Phi(G^{\prime}).

In the non-unitary case, the TRAP
P
has no unit
P
and one simply removes the terms with the identity
I
in the above computation and sets
p=q=p^{\prime}=q=0
. In this case, the result is the same as in the unitary case:
\Phi(G*G^{\prime})=\Phi(G)*\Phi(G^{\prime})
.

If
N\geqslant 1
, let us take an internal edge
e
of
G*G^{\prime}
. If
e
is an internal edge of
G
, then

(G*G^{\prime})_{e}=G_{e}*G^{\prime}
, and:

\displaystyle\Phi(G*G^{\prime})

\displaystyle=t_{1,1}\circ\Phi((G*G^{\prime})_{e})

\displaystyle=t_{1,1}\circ\Phi(G_{e}*G^{\prime})

\displaystyle=t_{1,1}(\Phi(G_{e})*G^{\prime})

\displaystyle=t_{1,1}\circ\Phi(G_{e})*\Phi(G^{\prime})=\Phi(G)*\Phi(G^{\prime}).

If
e
is an internal edge of
G^{\prime}
, we obtain similarly that
\Phi(G^{\prime}*G)=\Phi(G^{\prime})*\Phi(G)
.
The result then
follows from the commutativity of
*
(axiom
2.(d)
of Definition
1.1.1
).
So
\Phi
is compatible with
*
.

We still need to prove the compatibility of
\Phi
with the partial trace maps. By Lemma
1.1.5
, it is enough to prove that
\Phi
is compatible with
t_{1,1}
. Let
G\in\mathbf{{CGr}^{\circlearrowright}}(X)(k,l)
(or
G\in\mathbf{{solCGr}^{\circlearrowright}}(X)(k,l)
in the non-unitary case) be a graph,
e_{1}=\alpha^{-1}(1)
,
f_{1}=\beta^{-1}(1)
. We set
G^{\prime}=t_{1,1}(G)
and
e=\{e_{1},f_{1}\}
to be the edge of
G^{\prime}
created in the process. Notice that if
G\in\mathbf{{solCGr}^{\circlearrowright}}(X)
then
G^{\prime}\in\mathbf{{solCGr}^{\circlearrowright}}(X)
. There are five different cases (but only the first cases appear if
G\in\mathbf{{solCGr}^{\circlearrowright}}(X)
):

1.

If
e_{1}\in I(G)
and
f_{1}\in O(G)
, then
e\in E(G^{\prime})
and
G^{\prime}_{e}=G
. By construction of
\Phi(G^{\prime})
:

\Phi\circ t_{1,1}(G)=\Phi(G^{\prime})=t_{1,1}\circ\Phi(G^{\prime}_{e})=t_{1,1}\circ\Phi(G).

2.

If
e_{1}\in IO(G)
and
f_{1}\in O(G)
, let us put
j=\beta(e_{1})
. Then there exists a graph
H

such that
(1,j)\cdot G=I*H
, hence

\displaystyle t_{1,1}(G)

\displaystyle=t_{1,1}((1,j)\cdot(I*H))=(1,\ldots,j)\cdot(t_{1,i}(I*H))=(1,\ldots,j)\cdot H,

so:

\displaystyle t_{1,1}\circ\Phi(G)

\displaystyle=t_{1,1}((1,j)\cdot(I*\Phi(H))

\displaystyle=(1,j)(1,\ldots,j-1)\cdot\Phi(H)

\displaystyle=(1,\ldots,j)\cdot\Phi(H)

\displaystyle=\Phi((1,\ldots,j)\cdot H)

\displaystyle=\Phi\circ t_{1,1,}(G).

3.

If
e_{1}\in I(G)
and
f_{1}\in IO(G)
the computation is similar.

4.

If
e_{1},f_{1}\in IO(G)
, with
e_{1}\neq f_{1}
the computation is similar.

5.

If
e_{1}=f_{1}
in
IO(G)
, then
G=I*H
for a certain graph
G
and
t_{1,1}(G)=\mathcal{O}*H
.
Then:

\Phi\circ t_{1,1}(G)=\Phi(\mathcal{O})*\Phi(H)=t_{1,1}\circ\Phi(I)*\Phi(H)=t_{1,1}(\Phi(I)*\Phi(H)=t_{1,1}\circ\Phi(G).

So
\Phi
is compatible with the partial trace maps, both in the unitary and non-unitary cases.
∎
